THE MAYORALTY.

ALDERMAN FORSHAW RE-ELECTED.

Two Dissentients.

Alderman W. J. FORSHA W was re-elected to the 'Mayoralty of the borough at the annual meeting of the Warrington Town Council held at the Town Hall at noon on Tuesday. There were two dissentients from the resolution and several members of the Council refrained from voting. Considerable interest was taken in the proceedings and there was an unusually large attendance of the public. The Mayor (Alderman Forshaw) presided in the earlier part of the meeting, and there were also present Alderman Smethurst (deputy mayor), Aldermen Bennett, Bolton, Burton. James Evans and Tinnion and Councillors Ashcroft, W. H. Bellian, Birtles, Broadhurst, Chantler, Cunnington, Hawthorn, Dr. Joseph, C. Jackson-Holmes, Lashford, Locker, W. H. J. Jenkins, G. C. Howard, Pemberton, Roberts, Rogers, Sankey, Steel, Fulton-Smith, Wilkinson and Wilson, with the Town Clerk and several other officials of the Corporation, as well as the Mayoress and her two daughters, the Rev. G. C. Briggs (Mayor's chaplain), and Mr. Robert Pierpoint.

ELECTION OF MAYOR. IMPORTANT WORK DURING THE COMING YEAR.

Alderman S3IETHURST having been voted to the chair pro tern, Alderman TINNION moved "That William John Forshaw, Esquire, be and he is hereby re-elected Mayor for the ensuing year, and until the acceptance of office by his successor." Alderman Tinnion slid : I can only express the hope that to this resolution there will be no opposition, that it will be carried unanimously— (bear, bear)—and that the Mayor for the ensuing year will have the active support and co-operation of every member of the Corporation. I know there is a feeling —a very just and proper one—that every member of the Council should have the opportunity and the honour of occupying the Mayoral chair, but for the last thirty years' at any rate in Warrington, it has been the custom for the Mayor to occupy the chair for two or more years. This, of course, has its advantages, as it enables the occupant of the chair to correct any error he may have made, to avoid future mistakes, and profit by his pest experience, thereby enabling the work of the Council and of the town to go on as harmoniously as it possibly cen. (Hear, hear.) I appeal to the members of the Council to do all they can to help forward the very important work we have certainly ahead of us during the next twelve months. All of you know that we are going to Parliament for an Act to carry out many great and important works. I shall not enumerate them all, as probably the Mayor will have something to say on that subject, but there is the question of the bridges, the development of Victoria Park, the carrying on—l won't say the corn- pietion—of the widening and improvement of Market Cate. Some of the properties are, in my opinion, not held on tee most sound financial basis. The money ought to be borrowed and the properties put on a proper financial basis. I appell to you all to let bygones be bygones and to sink all personal differences. (Hear, hear). We ought to work with that ability and • harmony which in the end must be for the benefit of the town. The Mayor has done a very great and impor- ant work, and be has been very ably assisted by his good wife the Mayoress. (Heir, hear) do not think that I could better descrine the charac- ter and disposition of the Mayoress than in that homely phrase "a good, genial soul;' aver ready and willing to "help forward every good work. (Applause). The Mayor and Mayoress have known no party or sect dur- ing their year of office, and have been ready and willing to help forvvird every institution that was for the uplifting of their fellow-townsmen. They have done all in their power to forward the work of every society and institution which tended to the comfort and happi- ness of the people in the town, and may I hope that during the next year we may look forward to, and not only look forward to but realize, that it is the paramount duty of every member of the Council to do all he can to further the best interests of the good old town of Warrington. And that can be done if every member of the Council will be determined that from to-day his only object shall be to work harmoniously with all the other members. (Hear, hear).

Alderman BENNETT, in seconding, said :—Last year it was my privilege, as one of Alderman Forshaw's oldest friends, to propose his election to the high office of Mayor, and I did so with great pleasure. To-day it gives me equal pleasure to second the proposition which has been so ably moved by my good friend Alderman Tinnion. I am inclined to agree with some remarks of his with reference to the length of time during which the office is usually held, and there is something to be said for the custom, but in London, Manchester and other similarly unimportant places—(laughter)—the term is limited to one year. There is also something to be said for that, in the main because it is not fair to expect a man to make the inevitable sacrifices which the high office demands of time and money. I think the motto of the mayor of a town ought to be the motto we see emblazoned on the Prince of Wales's feathers "I serve "—(mar, hear)—because his worship has to work very hard and to be continuously at the call of the whole of the people of the town over which he presides, and be has to undertake great responsibili- ties and make great sacrifices. I think the office of Mayor is the highest honour we can bestow upon the citizen of any town. It is no light thing to be the chief magistrate of any town of any importance, but in a borough like Warriogton, which has so great a past and has I, believe, so great a future, so large a population and so many difficult problems, the position is one Of exceptional dignity and honour. (Hear, hear.) And now the question arises "How has the Mayor fulfilled the duties of his office during the past 12 months?" Well I think he has performed them in the main to the admiration at any rate of the vast majority of the people ; and in many ways he has won universal approbation. (Hear, hear.) Alderman Tinnion has spoken very well about the duties which are not inci- dental to the duties in the Council Chamber but are rather an accompaniment to the position, namely the duties outside. The bitterest critic could not take exception to the way in which Alderman and Mrs. Forshaw have discharged those duties, those continuous duties. Every class and every portion of the com- munity has to be attended to, and it means that during their term of office the Mayor and the Mayoress have to be at the disposal of the whole town. Then in regard to another phase of the question. The Mayor is the chief magistrate of the Borough, and I have never heard the slightest syllable of criticism as to the way in which he has performed his magisterial duties; he has been absolutely impartial and dignified. His conduct in the chair here in the main has given satisfaction. I think he has shown impartiality and dignity, and although I should he the last to pretend that he has never made a mistake, on the whole his conduct will compare favourably with that of any of his predecessors. The best of us make slips. To err is human, and, knowing that, we must feel that to expect perfection from anybody in this world is to expect an impossibility.- I have never for a moment regretted my proposition last year that Alderman Forshawbe elected to the proud position of Mayor of his native town. During the whole of the year he has been animated by one motive; even if he has made mistakes in one or instances his motive has always been the same—the dignity and good fame of the town. (Hear, hear.) The past year has been an important and memorable one and the year before us in some respects is likely to be more important still Never in the history of the Council were there greater queations demanding atten- tion, or greater necessity for co-operation. The delay over the erection of the bridge has been very tiresome to a great many of us. I think, however, we have lately made distinct progress, and that during the coming year we shall have the satisfaction of seeing the work commenced. The other questions which have to be dealt with include widening of the streets, the circus at Market Gate, the extension of the Water Works, and the development of Victoria Park. Then there is a matter full of the utmost importance to Warrington. I refer to the Town Planning Bill, which is likely to become law, and which will for the first time put into the hands of the Council power to map out the whole future of the good old borough and avoid the mistakes which have disfigured and disgraced the past. I second the plea which has been put forward by Alderman Tinnion for harmony, that any feeling which may have existed in the past will be buried, and that we shall unite to work for one object—the good of the town. (Hear, hear.) The Town Council has not always reflected credit upon itself or done justice to the town' and one sometimes hears criticisms which are painful. We sometimes seem to have forgotten the great work in front of us, and our dignity in the acrimonious quarrels which ought not to arise, and which 36 enlightened and intelligent citizens ought to avoid and overcome. (Hear, hear.) I have great faith in the 36 gentlemen who have been appointed by the citizens of the town to act for them, and I make an earnest appeal to you all to throw aside all feeling and to unite as one man in support of the Mayor, and to help to carry forward the great policy that lies before us in the coming year.

===== 0002601_19091113_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNCMLOR PEMBERTON'S CRITICISMS.

Councillor PEMBERTON: I cannot endorse all that Alderman Bennett had said and I do not think his remarks are quite fair. While I agree that there should be a harmonious feeling you cannot get 36 men to Fee eye to eye, but Alderman Bennett imputes to those who do not see eye to eye that we are not doing, what is right for the good of the town. I deny that. Too much has been said about that. For instance, Alderman Tinnion had said that the Council are going to Parlia- ment for a Bill which has not yet passed this Council. I think that a contentious matter like that ought not to have been mentioned. Alderman Tinnion and Alderman Bennett have spoken about the duties of the Council, butthere is a duty which the Mayor owes to the Council. I don't think the Mayors of this town have any cause to complain that they have not received the support of the Council, and ever since I have been in the Council the Mayor has been supported loyally. There is a duty to the Council which the Mayor should not forget. Alderman SMETHURST: I don't want you to repeat yourself, Councillor Pemberton. I had to dram your attention at the other meeting On two occasions when

you made long speeches, about which you were entirely wrong. I want to call your attention to the fact that a minute has been passed by the Council to the effect that the Town Clerk proceed by a Bill in Parliament for powers to construct a bridge and other , matters requiring Parliamentary sanction. Councillor Pemberton: When was that passed by the Council?

===== 0002601_19091113_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
Alderman S3I ETHURST : On October 26th, 1909.

Councillor PAMBERTON : Well, I did not know of it. If it is confirmed it can be altered by special r,esolu- tion. I attend as many Council meetings as any mem- ber but unfortunately I was away from the last meet- ing, as I was in London. Before you proceed to Parliament you have to get the sanction of the rate- payers. Alderman SMETHURST : I wish you would stick to the subject before the meeting—(hear, hear),—and that is the appointment of Mayor. Councillor PEMBERTON : You did not call Alderman Tinnion to order when he mentioned the Bill. The CHAIRMAN : He did not discuss the Bill. He spoke of the Mayor's ability. If you will speak in the same strain and say what you think about the Mayor, we can proceed with the business. You should not ramble about and make statements which I have proved were wrong. Support the resolution or oppose it. Councillor PEMBERTON : That is not a proper remark to make. How do you know what I was going to say ?

Alderman Smethurst: I shall not allow you to speak in that sort of manner. lam goinglo keep you to the resolution. (Hear, hear), Councillor PEMBERTON f. am keeping to the resolution.

The CHAIRMAN: What you are speaking about now is not on the agenda. Councillor PEMBERTOM : It is not right for you to tell me to g.) on and to say that I was going to say something that I did not intend to say. Alderman Tinnion mentioned about property that is on our hands. It was not proper for him to do so, as it was contentious matter. Alderman Tinnion says we are going to do this— Alderman Tisstox : I rise to a point of order. I did not say we were going to do this or that. I said they would have to be considered. (Hear, hear).

Alderman SMETHURST : That is What he- said.l want to draw your attention to that.

• Councillor PEMBERTON : I have great pleasure in endorsing every word which has been said about the Mayoress.

Alderman SMETIII7I3sT : lain very glad to hear that.

Councillor PEMBERTON : Everyone- who has known the Mayoress for any length of time can only endorse all that has been said about her. I cannot find language strong enough to speak about her. A kinder lady I never knew and she is always willing to help everyone who is in need. It is a great pity we have not got more like her.

The resolution was then put to the meeting and carried by 23 votes to two, the dissentients being Councillors Cunnington and LashfOrd. Several mem- bers refrained from voting.

Toe proposer and seconder of the resolution then went to acquaint the Mayor with the decision of the Council, and his Worship shortly afterwards entered the Council Chamber and proceeded to make the usual declaration. While this is being done, it is the custom of the members of the Council to stand, and the Deputy Mayor, noticing that several Councillors renrtined seated called upon "all gentlemen who are members of the Council to stand." Messrs. Pemberton, Cunnington and Lasttford, however, did not respond to the request.

===== 0002601_19091113_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MAYOR'S THANKS.

The MAyort then addresser-1 the Council as follows: —Mr. Alderman Tinnion and Mr. Alderman Bennett. let me first thank you most heartily for the kind and gracious words in which you have proposed and seconded my re-electioa as .Mayor of this important and ancient borough. I sincerely thank the members of the Council for having re-appointcd me to the office which I have had the privilege of holding during the last 12 months. I also thank Alderman Tinnion and Alderman Bennett for the 4ery kind expressions they have used towards my wife. (Applause.) It is with feelings of great gratification when I recall the fact that during my Mayoralty I, on behalf of the Council and Burgesses, had the privilege of welcoming his Majesty King Edward VII. and the Queen and Princess Victoria, and . tendering to his Majesty an address on behalf of the inhabitants of this loyal and ancient town, and it would be lacking in duty on my part to pass over this matter without again thankipg the Council and the inhabitants for the splendid manner in which they helped to make the visit of his Majesty an event which will be remembered for many years to come. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19091113_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MUNICIPAL YEAR

has not been without its striking features. A reduc- tion of 4d. in the .£ on the rates was a start with which all might be content, and with the exercise of care and economy I do not see why another reduction should not be made. (Heir, hear.) The trading concerns of the Corporation, presided over so ably by my friends Aldermen Smethurst. Pierpoint, and John Evans, are being conducted upon sound business and economic lines, and we hope that in the future, as in the vast, they will be valuable factors in the reduction of the rates, and in the reduction of the price of gas to con- sumers. The Corporation have obtained, I am glad to say. a Provisional Order for the widening of portions of Sankey-street, Ilorsemarket-street and Buttermarket- street, and the formation of a circus at Market-gate. In the course of a short time the erection of the first

building neon the circus will be comMenced, and before long the whole of the improvement will be completed, giving Warrington the honour of possessing in the centre of the town, an almost unique example of Georgian architecture. Warrington NEW BRIDGE

around which the waves of controversy have played for a longer period than one could desire, is now in a fair way to progress. It has given me great_pleasure to know that during my year of office the Council have definitely decided to build a new and substantial bridge for the town and to make the main artery between

Lancashire and Cheshire a width which ought to accommodate the traffic for another 100 years. lam sorry indeed that we have not at present been able to get a bridge at Howley and thus open out Victoria Park to the pecple and children of the north-east side of the river. That bridge and a bridge at Walton must come. (Hear. hear.) My year of office has also wit- nessed the placing of two most important duties on municipal authorities. I refer to the payment of old age pensions which have gladdened many a bumble home—(hear, hear)—and in the administration of which the committee have most conscientiously and carefully investigated all cases. The second duty is the collection of local taxation licences which, in the able hands of our Town Clerk and his staff, will, I think result in a gain to our revenue. A sad side of public life still remains to be touched upon. Death has been busy amongst us during the past year. I refer to the late Alderman Ellison, Councillors Nield and Roberts. We have lost members whose services will never he forgotten in this chamber. (Hear, hear). Mr. Samuel Davies, an ex-member of this Council, is no longer with us; and I feel that my remarks would be incomplete without a reference to our late good townsman, Mr. John White, who for so many years has been a foremost figure in the town. (Hear, bear). Leaving the past behind us, let us look forward into THE FUTURE

where a wide field of municipal improvement opens out. In the next Session of Parliament, a Bill is to be pro- moted for the building of the new bridge, and many

other momentous matters which have bee.- ti discussed from time to time in this chamber must be dealt with. These important subjects will require the most careful consideration of the Council and its committees, and we cannot carry on the good government of the borough unless a good feeling and spirit with unanimity exists amongst the individual members, and for that good feeling I earnestly appeal. I must sincerely ask each _ . _ .

and all of us to forget and forgive differences that we may have had in the past, and t remember that we are all here with one common object in view, and that is the best interests and advancement of our good old town of Warrington—God bless it. (kpplause). thank you, gentlemen. for the confidence you have again reposed in me. (Applause).

THE DEPUTY MAYOR.

The Mayor re-appointed Alderman Smethurst as Deputy Mayor for the ensuing year.

THE MAYOR'S CHAPLAIN.

The Mayor re-appointed the Rev. G. C. Briggs, vicar of Christ Church, Latchford, as his chaplain.

NSW STANDING ORDERS.

ALMOST NO SKIN LEFT On Face and Neck by Eczema.

"I was seized with chronic eczema. The disease broke out on my face and spread to My body, arms and scalp. After attending hospital it gradually got worse and on my face it turned to running eczema. After a fortnight of hospital treatment, I had hard 4 any skin left on my face and neck and it was so itchy that I had to be strapped with my arms-to my side at night. It was a rough rash and itchy and sore on my body and arms, but it was running all day long on my face neck and scalp. I bejgan to think that I should never get well again but I am now well again and have all my hair on my head, thanks to Cuticura. After the first set consisting of Cuticura Soap, Cuticura Ointment and Cuticura Pills, I began to find the benefit and after the second set the disease had almost disappeared. I can safely recommend the Cuticura Remedies to all who suffer from eczema in any shape or form. I never had the pleasure of leaving the house for over six weeks and I had to leave my employment and was out of work for over four months. D. Hyde, P,39, Stony Stanton Rd., Coventry,. Eng., Dec. 2, 1908.7

minutes shall previously ask if any other member wishes to speak on such minutes," and" a member shall be entitled to ask the chairman of a committee any question arising out of the minutes of the committee which are being moved for adoption. The asking of such a question or the reply thereto shall not be con- sidered es a speech upon the minutes." Councillor WILKtssoN seconded, and Alderman BOLTON supported. He said the new Standing orders would prevent bickerings. Alderman TiNmoN said it seemed to him that the addition was unnecessary. The suggessions made were already the custom, although there might occasionally e a slight deviation through an oversight. It would appear as though they hid no comidence in future mayors. The Standing Orders certainly required amending. Some of them were absolutely silly and it was impossible to understand their meaning. The pro- posed new orders would be open to abuse as some gentlemen were cleverer than others and were able to make three or four speeches when asking questions. He objected to the additions because they were entirely unnecessary.

The MAYOR : It is certainly undue interference, Councillor PembPrton, with the rights of the chair, because this standing order in practice has been carried out.

- Councillor PEMBERTON said at the caucus meeting the additions were unanimously agreed to and certain understandings come to, and it did not seem the thing to object now. Councillor ROBERTS said the only objection he had to the additions was that they gave verbal expression to the order of procedure ps it obtained now. There was nothing new in them. It was no more necessary to adopt them in that form than to have n standing order that the chairman of a committee should move his own minutes.

Alderman SM ET H URST considered the new order would place chairmen of committees in an awkward position, as questions might be sprung upon them about somethitag they knew nothing of. Alderman JAMES Eva:vs said if a chairman was unable to give a satisfactory answer it was easy for him to say hd had not got the information.

Comic'llo-r Wir..xtssoN supported Councillor PEMBERTON'S proposition. He said there was no reason why any member should not ask any question he liked. The reason for Councillor Pemberton's proposition was that one night in that Council Chamber a member asked a qusstion of a chairman of a committee. The chairman answered immediately and by some mistake the answer was taken as a reply on the minutes. Mistakes had been made, and would be made in the future. No disrespect was intended in making these additions to the Standing Orders. The additions were accepted, and the Standing Orders approved.

Alderman SMETHURST proposed and Alderman BURTON seconded the appointment of Alderman James Evans as the representative on the Lancashire Asylums Board. Councillor PEMBERTON said he was not going to move an amendment, but he would point out that an alder- man once held this position for years. At that time he (the speaker) moved an amendment, which was carried, that another gentleman be appointed. He did not think that this appointment should he held hy one gentle- man continually. It should go round the Council. He meant no disrespect to Alderman Evens, but he would move that the position be given to another member on another occasion. The resolution was carried. ===== 0002601_19091113_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON MUSICAL SOCIETY.

MIN

===== 0002601_19091113_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
ITS WORK REVIEWED.

[CONCLUDED. j

1 must now digress for a moment to refer to Mr: F. H. Crossley, who about this time appeared on the scene. For some years previously he had been in Natal where he had been conductor of a society in Maritzburg and trainer of the Cathedral choir. He returned to England in 1887, and shortly afterwards went to study in Berlin with Messrs. Sauret and Louis Wolfe (the celebrated violinists), and Herr Tappert, returning to England in 1889. Shortly aftei his return he and a few friends met to discuss the possibility and advisability of forming a new society. This resulted in the birth of the "Orpheus Orchestral , Society," consisting of ladies and gentlemen, and whose main objects "shall be in the practice and occasional performance of such good classical music as shall come within the range and capacity of amateurs." It must be borne in mind that the Musical Society's band had again become practically defunct, and that the new soCiety was not in any sense antagonistic to the Musical Society, and also that for, the first time in Warrington, it gave facilities for lady instrumentalists meeting for practice in what may be considered as a • fully constituted orchestra. Mr. W. E. Atkin was the first secretary, and it was decided to ask the late Sir Gilbert Greenall to become president. This society at once got to work, and on February 22nd, 1890, gave a very successful concert with the 'help of Miss Mein (Mrs. W. E. Atkin), and Mr. F. W. Reynolds, at which an orchestral composition -by Mr. F. H. Crossley was performed. The proceeds of the concert were handed over to the Warringtoa Infirmary. Returning now to the Musical Society, the general meeting referred to above, was held on August 7th, 1890, when the following resolutions were passed : (a) That a Sub-committee be appointed to approach the Orpheus Orchestral Society with - a view to amalgamation, then to report and to submit proposals as to the future working of the society to an adjourned meeting,. . _ _

• (b) -That a special effort be made to wipe out the debt.

(e) That a hearty vote of thanks be accorded to Dr. Hiles for his service during the 25 years he had acted as conductor, together with an expression of regret that the present conditions of finance do not admit of his services being retained. (d) That Mr. F. H. Crossley be asked to accept the conductorship of the Amalgamated Societies. (e) That Mr. W. E. Atkin be asked to act as 'joint secretary with another gentleman to be appointed. (f) That the &ciety give four concerts during the season.

These resolutions were carried out and practices were commenced in due course in Trinity School, a very successful season resulting. For the third con- cert soloists were selected from each division of the chorus by votes of makers. On March 19th, 1891, Mr. F. H. Crossley gave a very interesting and instructive lecture on " Meridelssohn " in the Wesleyan School, illustrated by vocal- and instru- mental examples of the master's music, rendered by several members of the Society: On April 29th, the follotving resolution Was passed by the Committee :—" That this Committee place on record their appreciation of Mr. F. H. Crossley's unvarying patience, his indomitable perseverance, and unvarying courtesy throughout the season, and would . pleased to offer a sum of twenty guineas as an. 'honorarium to their conductor not in any sense as a recompense tor such services, ,but as a slight recognition." The treasurer's accounts for the season showed a balance in-hand of £22 4s. 2d. During the following season, 1891-2, Mr. A. Sutton kindly placed his Music Room at the disposal of the Committee for orchestral practices. The same year a scheme was evolved to adapt the Public Hall for a theatre. This necessitated great structural alterations, and the removal of the Society's organ. A meeting of the trustees was called. and a committee was appointed to try and dipose of the organ. This; however, proved impossible, and ultimately it was taken down by Messrs. Wadsworth, of Manchester, and was given storage room at Bank Quay by Mr. John White. The following seation, 1892-3, was a very unsatis- factory one on account of the small-pox epidemic, and owing to the fact that professionals declined to come to Warrington. On this account two concerts were abandoned altogether. The third of the season was given, and Mendelssohn's " Athalie "was performed, Mrs. Arthur Edelsten very kindly reciting the narrative portions of the work. This concert, given on April 26th, 1892, was the last given in the Public Hall prior to its conversion ,into the Royal Court Theatre. The first performance after was a series of tableaux vivants, with an appropriate musical programme, which were given in aid of the funds of the Musical Society. This building (the Royal Court Theatre) proved so very unsuitable for concerts, that it was decided to give no more there, and the following season, 1893-4, the first concert was given in the Drill Hall, and the second in the Gymnasium, but there was a great expense incurred in fitting them to accommodate the band and chorus, which now numbered about 180 performers, and a further loss resulted, making the total deficit £lO5. It had been previously announced that J. C. Parr, Esq., intended building and presenting to the town a new Concert Hall,. and at the annual meeting on September 25th, 1894, the Chairman (Mr. White), referring to this, stated that Mr. Parr was very favourably disposed towards the society, and that it was probable that it would be invited to take part in the opening ceremony. In view of the impossibility of working with any success, pending the completion of the new hall it was decided to give no more concerts till then, but to continue rehearsals. These were, however, discontinued later, and not resumed till January 22nd, 1895. In January, 1895, the Latchford Dramatic Society gave some performances in aid of the Musical Society, which resulted in £32 being handed to the Treasurer of the Society. In May, 1895, the organ trustees met to consider an offer made by Mr. Parr to find £4OO on loan wherewith to enlarge and 'rebuild the organ in the new hall under arrangement with -the Corporation, which gave the Society a rental and the Corporation the proceeds of letting, and also the option to pur- chase at any later date agreed upon. This offer was accepted, and Messrs. Gray and, Davidson were entrusted with the work. A new case was designed to suit the hall, and the organ was erected in time for the opening of the "Parr Hall" on September 12th, 1895. The total cost was about £350, and a special effort 'was made to raise the money. By February, 1897, a stun of £232 had been raised, and a further sum was subscribed later. (The agreement with the Corporation remained in force for about three years, when, the receipts from letting the organ not proving sufficiently large, the Corporation declined to continue the payment to the' Society, and fresh negotiations were entered into and continued till about June, 1903, when the trustees (having taken legal . advice) trans- ferred the organ to the Corporation on satisfactory terms).

During the seasons 1894-6 the Society gave no regular concerts, but Mr. F. H. Crossley, Mr. F. W. Reynolds, assisted by a vocalist and Mr. Carl Fuchs (cellist), gave two chamber concerts in the Co-operative Hall, under the auspices of the Musical Society. The accounts showed a loss ofl2 ss. 11d. The following season 1895-6 began on September 26th, 1895, the date of the, opening of the Parr Hall. At Mr. Parr's request, the Society gave two concerts, "Judas Maccabeus " at 2.45 p in. and a miscellaneous concert at night, when Sullivan's "On Shore and Sea" was included in the programme. Three more concerts were given, and resulted in a deficiency on the season of 4() 7s. 2d.

In 1896-7, three concerts were given, inclqding the " Messiah" which was given very successfully on December 19th, at popular prices, and it resulted in a gain of ;£26 19s. Od. The third concert (on March 31st, 1897) opened with" God Save the Queen " in

memory of Queen Victoria's Diamond Jubilee. At the annual meeting on June 13th, 1897, Rule 1 was altered and members' subscriptions were raised to ss. for single-members, and 2s. 6d. for each additional member of the same family. The chorus was reported as numbering 161 and the orchestra 34 members.

During the following season, 1897-8, the Society sustained a great loss in the death of Mr. John Rylands, who might well have been called the father of the Society, having been more or less actively and officially connected with it from its earliest days, and for many years its president. The report of the annual meeting on June 16th, 1898, was a very satisfactory one. The chorus and band numbered 192 members and "had attained great efficiency und6r the able conductorship of Mr. Crossley," An alteration and reduction in the scale of subscriptions and admission prices which had been made appeared to have borne good fruit, and the treasurer reported total receipts of £460 13s. 11d. (probably :the largest income received by the Spciety in any one year). A profit on the season of :C47 6s. 6d. had resulted and "This excellent result was in great 'measure due to their conductor, who was not Only a good musician, but was also a good business man and concerned himself intimately in the working of the Societ:::." , The next season 1898-9 was again fairly successful, the band and chorus numbered 227 and the accounts shewed a small deficit only of .:£3 is. 9d. The , " Messiah "„was again performed as an extra concert:

Season 1899-1900. -The band and chorus numbered 222 members, and had done some excellent work including Gounod's "Faust " and "Redemption." The " Messiah " had been given. again, but the concert had proved, rather disastrous and lost £3O. The accounts showed a deficiency of £29 on the season, against which there were outstanding sub- scriptions to come in, amounting to £l2 17s. 6d. At the annual meting on Jtily 9th, Mr. John White (who was in the chair) remarked "that the season had been one of the most successful musically, and it was gratifying to notice that perfect harmony had prevailed throughout the Society owing mainly to the tact and ability of their respected conductor. As regarded the debt, the Society had almost always been in debt, and would be, but the amount was small and they need not worry, but they might soon have to take steps to wipe it out." Mr. Lord was appointed honorary. assistant secretary.

The following season Mr. J. B. Caldwell resigned his position as honorary organist, and at the annual meeting. (July 9th, 1901) a rose bowl was presented to him in recognition of his services. The report on this season was unsatisfactory, the loss being £7l ; s. 9d., increasing the debt to £154 14s. 6d. The Chairman, commenting on the report, remarked" that the season had been a disastrous, one, not only for Warrington, but for other societies, owing to the lamentable death of the Queen and other events which had placed the country in mourning; he thought that some suggestions should be made which would tend to place the Society upon a more satisfactory financial footing. As an educational body, they could not be doing better work . . The conductor said it had been suggested that one of Gilbert and Sullivan's operas should be given; he however did not consider such music suitable for concerts, but he would be quite willing to do one in operatic form, and invited suggestions from outsiders and others. A dance was suggested and later a ladies' committee was formed to make the necessary arrange- ments. This dance was given in the Parr Hall on Tuesday, February 11th, 1902, and realized, with subscriptions, £5O. At the next annual meeting, July 13th, 1903. the Treasurer reported a loss on the season of £26 65., and the debt as standing at £136 Os. 7d. Mr. Crossley again referred to the question of giving an opera per- formance, and suggested that , if a guarantee fund could be raised to avoid arty possibility of loss, an opera should be given for three nights, the probable cost being about £lOO. This suggestion was acted on and a special Opera Committee was elected to make all arrangements after obtaining the guarantee fund. Mr. G. F. F. White, who had for some years acted as honorary librarian, resigned, and Mr. G. Johnson was elected to fill the vacancy thus caused. The next annual meeting took place on May 4th, 1003, when prospects looked §omewhat brighter. There had been a small loss on the'season, £l6 14s. 6d., making fhe deficit £156 16s„ but the Secretary announced that "after four years negotiations,the organ trustees bad (as mentioned above) come to an arrange- ment with the 'Corporation as to the organ, which (though a valuable asset) had been somewhat of a white elephant. The arrangement would clear off the debt brought forward from the previous sedson and the debt on the organ, leaving only the loss on the. last season (116 14s. 6d.) to be dealt with." He also stated' that a suggestion had been made by Mr. Monks, that a reserve fund should be established, and that he (the Secretary) had been in consultation with him. He himself saw considerable difficulty in doing this, but he was in sympathy with the proposal. The matter after discussion was left in the hands of the Committee. In February, 1903, another dance was given and realized £6 10s. 9d. In October, 1903, the Society gave its first opera performance—Gilbert and Sullivan's opera, "The Yeomen of the Guard "—with great success, and not the least satisfactory feature was the united and whole-hearted way in which all concerned had worked together to attain this success. The financial result was a sum of £134 10s. 3d. being handed oveFto the treasurer. It was decided to repeat the experiment, and a isub-committee was again appointed to carry this decision into effect. The annual report for this season made at the meeting on June 13th, 1904, gave a loss on the season of £69, but after crediting the Organ, Opera and Dance Funds, there was a balance in hand of £3B ss. 9d. to carry forward. The second opera. "The Gondoliers," given for six nights in October. 1904, was even more successful than the first. and £2lB 3s. 3d. was handed over, out of which £2OO was invested as a start for the reserve fund. The concerts this season lost £4B Os. Bd., but the balance from last ' season and the ,opera funds covered this, and left £6 19s. 6d. to carry forward. At the annual meeting, September 11th, 1905, the Chairman. .Mr. Monks, suggested that the Education Committee should be approached as he thought it possible that they might be willing to vote a sum ..of money to teach an elementary instrumental class. Mr. W. E. Atkin (honorary secretary) resigned and Mr. C. P. Gough was elected in his stead. The fltird opera performance " The Mikado "was performed on October 16th to 21st, 1005, and was again very successful, £214 15s. od. being handed over to the treasurer. On July 10th, 1906, a sub- committee was appointed to meet representatives of the Warrington Male Voice Choir to discuss the question of its members joining the Musical Society, The fourth opera, " lolanthe," was given on October 15th to 20th, 1906, and was again a success, £lB2 I7s. Bd. being handed to the treasurer. On

February 9th, 1907, a special miscellaneous concert was given in aid of the Infirmary Extension Fund and ;£5O was handed over from the general funds of the Society. On October Bth, the treasurer was author- ised to invest a further sum of £2OO in Warrington o,r Liverpool Corporation Bonds, or in Mersey Dock and Harbour Board. On April 30th, 1907, occurred the death of Mr. L- W. Reynolds, then honorary treasurer. His connee; tion Nfith the society, first as honorary secretary Ott - later as honorary treasurer, dated from 1867, .a period, of 30 years, during which term he had devoted much time and enerlgy to the Society's interests. Mr. W. E. Atkin was elected to fill the Vacancy. , At the annual meeting, 1907, another loss on the season of £29 14s. sd. was announced, but a larger number of members had been enrolled than in anY previous season. The fifth opera (a repetition of "The Yeoraen..of the , Guard ") was given in October, 1907. This was not/ very successtul, and only a small profit resulted, which was held over by the Opera Committee towards the expenses of the sixth opera, which it was proposed to give in October, 1908. At the annual meeting, September sth, 1908, the loss reported amounted to Y-45 2s. 7d., and the balance in hand carried to next account was ;€8 Bs. Mr. C. r• Gough resigned, and Mr. %V. E. Atkin was requested to accept the secretaryship again. -To this he con- sented, the 'writer being appointed treasurer. resolution was passed empowering the Committee te' confer honorary membership upon any member or members who had successfully followed music as 3" profession, and had rendered good and useful service to the Society. " This resolution was carried int° effect later, when Mrs. Booth and Miss Anderto were elected as honorary members. For the Society's sixth opera, given on October 12th to 17th, 1908, Edward German's "Mer"e England" was selected. This being more ambitious than previous operas, and requiring a greater number of perforMers and more space on the 'stage than i 9 provided in the Pailr Hall, an arrangement was made with the manager 'of the Court Theatre, and the performance was given there. This was in every W',33r excellent, and was most successful, but unfortunate'Y'' owing to heavy expenses and low prices of admission, it, was not a financial success, and the sum of 35 17s. Bd. was all that could be handed over to the Musical Society on account of this and the previoas- year's performance. Of the season 1908-9, there is nothing of special interest to record, but on April 6th an important committee meeting was held, when an Opera Cotn- mittee for the ensuing season was appointed, and proposal was was made to form special ladies and gentle- men s committees to endeavour to bring the publia more in touch with the society, to circulate .a more thorough knowledge of its work, and in any other waY to further the interest of the Society. It is hoped that this will result in a largely augmented subscription list, and a useful and tangible increase in the reserve • fund. I have now brought this history up to date. What the future will bring no one can say, but all must admit that to have existed, as it has, for 75 years* under so many adverse circumstances as are hereiu. recorded, the Society must possess great vitality. 01, the excellent work it has done and is doing still, all the immense influence for good which has emanated from it, there can be no question. If one person out. of every ,hundred of Warrington's population would 'take a practical interest in the Society, much more good work could and would be done, and if the issue of this history, will help the .attainment of this end, the writer will feel well repaid for time and trouble.; given. The Society .has, during its existence, lost many friends and workers, too numerous to mentioa by name. To this list must, to the sorrow of all whO knew him, be added the name of Mr. John 'Whitet. whose lleath has occurred since he revised these sheets. His connection with the Society covers a. ipriod of 51 years, during part of which he filled the' (Rees of assistant secretary, secretary, treasurer and president, and did much and useful work.

Begin by ring t" pou dle oil from the bottle all round the welt, so that the Bob between the sole and upper leather is quite filled w oil, and then proceed all over the boot, includiat,„n edges of the soles, rubbing it, in with the hands. ""1., half till is done, have a turn at the other, and so alternatea4 till you have got in about a tablespoonful and a to each boot.

THE CROWD AT THE WARRINGTON ":A" v. OLDHAM "A" MATCH.

The original of the portrait marked with a X will receive the " Examiner " prize of ss. on calling at the office in Bewsey-street on or before Friday next, November 19th. Two prizes of ss. are offered for every First "A " Team match at Wilderspool. The prize for the Warrington " A " v. Pembe' 104, Greenall's-avenue, Warrington.

Team match, and one prize of ss. for every

ton match has been claimed by Joseph Bostock,

ing the first class. Frawley did some clever things, but he will never get his international form back until Warrington find a centre who can and will work with him. I am convinced Warrington have a fine wing in the Australian if he is only encouraged and found opportunities on the field of play. A word of of praise Is due also to Smith for a smart and plucky display despite a badly gashed knee. Mclntyre played one of the soundest games I have seen him play yet, but for once his goal-kicking was" oph."

The bar on the importation of Colonial players was bound to come, and little can be said against the resolution of the N.U. Committee which was, passed on Tuesday night. That resolution reads as follows: "That any player belonging to any League club in membership with the Northern League, Australian League, or the New Zealand League, shall not be allowed to play in either of the other leagues without the consent of the club to which he belong 6, such consbnt to be obtained through the League of which such club is a member. In the case of a player wishing to leave one League for another. and his transfer being refused by the club he is leaving, such player shall be allowed to play in the county of his adoption after a two years' residental qualification, any player's touring qualification to date from the completion of the tour."

Warrington certainly have the credit or is it disgrace ?—of being the cause of such a resolution being passed. Their cabling for Stuntz was a new idea in football management, and opened the eyes of the governing body to possibilities which might tell against the interests of the Union. Certainly, if most of the crack clubs in the N.U. League were to follow Warrington's example and import Colonials who had won fame on their native soil, there would be little chance of the game 'flourishing in Australia and New Zealand. Morever, the people "down under" might retaliate in kind. " Jack " Fish or Jimmy Leytham might set sail for the Antipodes if there was a bag of gold waiting to be picked up on arrival. The thing could not be allowed to develop, obviously, and Warrington are lucky in having booked O'Malley, Frawley and Stuntz before the embargo has been put on. There is a good sprinkling of Colonials in the Union already, and Clubs ought to be com- pelled to cultivate local talent. The matches for to-day are :

LANCASMIIE CUP SEMI-FINALS. Leigh v. Runcorn. Swinton v. Wigan.

The Warrington men should take the field with light hearts to-day, for Merthyr can hardly be counted serious Opposition, though they were only beaten by 5 points to nil at Wilderspool last season. These figures should he quadrupled to-day, for Warrington were at their worst last year when Merthyr were their visitors, and to-day they are at their best. There has been one notable change in the team. Brooks is standing down "by order" to see if a rest willerestore him to form, and Lloyd fills the vacancy. Probably the "A" team man will be good enough for the occasion.

The team will be: Tilley, fullback; Fish, Dicken- son, Taylor and Bradshaw, threequarters ; Jenkins and Lloyd, half-backs; Boardman, Shugars, O'Niell, Belton and Thomas, forwards.

NOTES BY " OVALIST."

It is hardly worth while claiming a reputation as a prophet on the strength of the result at Oldham. I expected Warrington to lose, and so did most people but they came nearer winning than was thought likely. Anyhow they had to sacrifice the leading position in the League and it will take some real bard work to regain the honour of heading the table. They have excellent prospects during the current month, however, with home engagements against Merthyr, Runcorn, and Wigan, and Wakefield away. Why should not all those matches be won?

There is no fault to find with Warrington's per- formance. To run the Watersheddings brigade to a try on their own ground was a fine feat, and quite as much as Warrington supporters expected. A little bit of luck would have given Warring- ton the victory, for they thoroughly overplayed Oldham in the first half and ought to have Secured a commanding lead. They did secure the first five points and ought to have had ten, but near half time Oldham scored twice and gained what proved to be the winning lead. The second half was largely in Oldham's favour, but five minutes from the Close Brooks rushed over near the posts and there Was much dissatisfaction in the Warrington ranks When Referee Smith judged the pass from Fish to have been forward.

The game was a fine one to _watch, and Oldham found themselves opposed to a team as good as them- selves. The Warrington forwards played a great game in the first half and quite dominated the proceed- ings, while the backs did some strong work, though the combination was not as smart as could have been desired. O'Malley was brilliant in the loose and had the hardest of luck in having the ball kicked away Just as he was grounding. On another occasion he saved the situation for Warrington in dramatic style. Five Oldham men had a clear field and the ball in their possession when the Australian dashed up behind and with a flying dive brought down the man with the ball by the heels. Jenkins also did grand Work, his tackling being an object lesson. Brooks ?nade some strong dashes on the attack, but was not In his best form by a long way. As a rule Fish does /Int distinguish himself • greatly at Oldham, and on Saturday he was employed more on the defence than the attack. Several times he was busy appealing when he should have been playing.

The sensation of the day was Bradshaw's try admitted all round to be the finest effort seen at Oldham this season. He seized the opportunity in fine style and the way he ran for over three-quarters 3f the field was treat to watch. Deane and Tyson found the task of overhiuling the young winger beyond their powers. It was a weak kick by the Oldham full bock, who was too close up, which gave Bradshaw the oPening, and well he turned it to account. It was thought by many that he got a second try at the Corner after passing, but the verdict was against him. Anyhow he played a rattling good game. But what Of Warrington's goal kicking? Fish missed con- l'erting Bradshaw's try from a position right in front of the posts. Thomas missed an easy penalty, and it Was left to Dickenson to drop the only goal which Warrington obtained. There is a falling-off in goal- kicking which will cost Warrington dearly if not remedied. They have only kicked thirteen goals in ten matches, out of 29 tries and numerous penalties. Is there any serious practice of place-kicking by any raember of the first team? I have never heard of it, and truth to tell it has been a neglected point for Years.

,The Warrington men were much struck with the ...display of Anlezark at half-back. He was one of the kangaroo " stars" in the last tour, but was rarely seen owing to an injured knee. To-day he is playing a fine game for Oldham, and works the scrum with the utmost skill. A feature of his play is variety. He uses his brains as well as his boots, and keeps the other side on the Watch by alternating the long pass, the run round, and the short kick. Warrington's halves were considered he better pair at Oldham but they would have been lafinitely better if they had shown the resource of ,Anlezark. He taught them a thing or two which I 'lope they will carefully remember. Deane played a strong centre game, but on the whole the Oldham backs found themselves well matched. It is rather 1111 usual for neither Tyson nor G. W. Smith to get a try. The same may be notea in respect of Fish, who also tetired pointless for only the second time this season.

Warrington lost their position as leaders of the League on Saturday at Oldham, but they played a excellent game and were only beaten by 8 points to 5. Fine weather favoured the match, which was a popular one at Oldham, and 16,000 spectators assembled. Both sides were at full strength and the game was full of exciting incidents q.nd good football. Tbe teams were: Oldham—Wood, back; Tyson, Deane, Llewellyn, and Smith, three-quarter backs; White and Anzelark, half backs; Ferguson, Avery, Jardine, Owens, Helm, and Wright? forwards. Warrington—Tilley, back; Fish, Dickenson, Taylor, and Bradshaw, three-quarter backs; Brooks and Jenkins, half backs ; Boardman, Belton, O'Neill, O'Malley, Shugars, and Thomas, forwards. Referee, Mr. J. H. Smith (Widnes). Touch judges, Messrs. Edwards (Rochdale), and Tillison (Salford). Warrington started in very strong fashion, and were early on attacking in the home quarter? where Wood defended gallantly. Oldham responded with good play by Anzelark, Deane, and Tyson, which was met in proper style by the Warrington hacks. The stay in the Warrington quarter was brief and a magnificent rush by Warrington forwards drove Oldham to the line again. Hero Tilley marked in good position, but Thomas could not manage the goal. Hot work was seen in the home quarter, and both wings were nearly in. Oldham made another incursion in the Warrington half where Wood punted rather wide. Bradshaw fielded well and finding himself well placed he dashed between Deane and Llewellyn and was soon clear of the ruck. Then followed a great race, but, the Warrington lad kept his lead and scored a great try under the cross-bar where Fish missed the easiest of place kicks. Oldham pressed on resnrning, and Smith was held up by Fish. The home side did not hold their advantage for long, for O'Malley, Boardman and Shugars were able leaders in a rush, which almost overcame .the defence, O'Malley

However, a score was soon forthcoming, for from the resulting scrim- mage the ball was splendidly handled by Brooks, Jenkins and Dickenson, and the latter dropped the bail over ,the bar, thus placing Warrington five points ahead. Oldham showed a slight improvement on resum- ing en 4 forced matters at the Warrington end. Tilley's defence continued to be sound and twice he ably dealt with efforts by Llewellyn end White. RUNCORN LEAD AGAIN.

Swinton mot Runcom at Runuorn, before 2,000 spectators. Runcorn were quickly on the attack. White, Watton, Moran, Butterworth, and Hughes handled the ball in brilliant fashion, the last-named scorine behind the posts Pr Moran to add a goal. Wattcn and Padbury added two more tries for Runcorn, which Moran and Richardson failed to augment. Half-time :—Runcorn, 2 goals • and 6 tries (22 points), Swinton nil. With such a good lead Runcorn took matters easier in the second half. The visitors then played with great vigour, and ;Williams passed to D.. Davies who ran over in grand style, Parker adding a goal. Follow- ing this, the home forwards dashed away, and Kennedy getting possession scrambled over, Padbury failing with the gosl-kick. In the last minute Williams scored a try for Swinton, Parker being unsuccessful at goal. Result :—Runcorn 2 goals and 7 tries (25 'points), Swinton 1 goal and 2 tries (8 points). LEIGH v. ST. HELENS.

Played at Leigh, before 8,000 spectators. Leigh had the best of matters in the early part of the game, and after splendid passing Tom Johnson was going over the line near the posts' when he was deliberately 'tripped. A penalty kick was awarded, and Clarkson placed a goal. Leigh made repeated attempts to add to their score, but for a long time the St. Helens backs defended tenaciously. 'Eventually, following a eross•kick by Neville, Whittaker started a passing movement, which ended in Tom Johnson making an opening for his brother Sam, who scored his first try this season. No goal resulted. St. Helens were rarely dangerous, and at the interval Leigh led by 5 points to nil. The second half opened in a sensational fashion, for after a fruitless attack by Leigh, Matt Creevey broke clean away in a scrummage in the St. Helens half, and, on nearing Clarkson kicked over the full back's head. His brother Charles followed up, took the ball on the bounce, and scored a clever try, and kicked a goal, thus equalising the scores. A few minutes later Sam John- son intercepted a pass from Turtill to Flanagan, and grounded the ball behind the St. Helens posts. Clarkson failed at goal. Matt Creevey, the St. Helens half back, was now sent off for striking, and Leigh made an incessant attack upon the St. Helens line, Neville eventually scoring. Sam Johnson placed a goal, and Leigh won by 2 goals 3 tries (13 points) to St. Helens 1 goal and 1. try (5 points). Teams : Leigh—E. Clarkson back; Sam Johnson, T. H. John- son, D. Davies, and B. H. Neville, three-quarter backs • Bolewski. and S. Whittaker, half backs; P. 0. Neill, 13,1 Gallop, W. Winstanley, E. Jones, 11. Marsh, and A. Lee forwards.

St. Helens—Turtill, back ; Flanagan, J. Greenwood„ C. Creevey, and Bate, three-quarter backs; M. Creevey and H. Greenwood, half backs; Briers, Mavitty, Pope, Walker, Helsby, and Bradley, forwards. WIDNES WIN AT ROCHDALE.

There were about 3,000 spectators on. the Rochdale Athletic Grounds when the Hornets had Whines as visitors. The played up well, and the Hornets' forwards were beaten for possession. Lambert dashed down the wing, but his pass to Reid was intercepted near the line by Schofield. Blong had a good opening, but he kicked weakly. There was no score at the interval. Soon after resuming Robinson clean missed a pass by Schofield when left with an open line. Widnes attacked, and the visitors kicked over the line, but the ball went dead. Baxter gathered in the loose, and sent to Taylor, whose pass to Bamber was intercepted, and Daintith got over at the corner, but he failed to land a goal. Then Johnson marked from a weak kick by Bamber, and Daintith kicked an easy goal. A few minutes later Taylor mulled, and the Widnes forwards dribbled over but Farrar saved in the nick of time by falling on the ball. Just before the close Baxter broke through and served Robinson with a grand pass, but that player was bowled into touch practically on the line. Result : Widnes 1 goal 1 try (5 points), Rochdale Hornets nil. Teams

LANCASHIRE COMBINATION

WARRINGTON "A" STILL WINNING.—The visit of Oldham was regarded as likely to be a test for the Champions on Saturday, and quite 5,000 people assembled at Wilderspool. Both sides were strongly represented, Oldham including such well-known players as Cash, McLean, and the two Dixon*. From the out- set the game was very fast and with Warrington show- ing superior combination. Dixon was often troubled. Landor placed his side on the aggressive, and a second attempt by the same player was spoiled when he threw the ball forward to Smith. Civil, Webster and Cook eased the pressure slightly, but eventually Frawley ran well to the corner. After keen scrummsging Lloyd succeeded in opening the scoring, bluffing his opponents smartly and scoring near the posts. Mclntyre goaled. Oldham repeatedly threatened danger and met a stout _defence set up by Mclt tyre. Another raid on the Oldham line resulted in Boardman scoring, the home side leading at the interval by one goal two tries (eight points) to nil. Turning round Oldham forced motters and for the first quarter of an hour were the attacking party. Landor, Lloyd and Mclntyre defended splen- didly, and on one occasion a smart breakaway by Lloyd lacked assistance, or a tangible record would have been notched. Webster failed at in easy penalty shot, and up to the close of hostilities the game was fought out in strenuous fashion without the defence being pene- trated. Final: Warrington, eight points; Oldham,nil.

SWEET-SCENTED FLOWERS.

During the planting season from now until thespring one thinks of the flowers that possess certain character- istics—colour, freedom of growth and bloom and fragrance. Certain flowers are used for their beauty, but when they expand they distil no scent, although this attribute is enjoyed everywhere if it is not over- powering. The scent of sweet briar as one opens the gate, the faint perfume from the roses and the honeyed fragrance of the night-scented stock make the Earden a real garden. I turn gladly from scentless though, perhaps, glorious flowers to those musk, that have this rich gift, from the glowing dahlia Ao the homely jasmine, honeysuckle and the olove.—E,T,c., in "Co untry Life."

FOOTBALL GOSSIP.

BY B. BENNISON.

ELLI7STRATED BY JACK DODWORTII.

Tho Players' Union having come into line with the Football Association, there is peace— permanent peace, eyerybody hopes. All that is now necessary is a formal settlement and a strict understanding. It is not necessary to consider whether the Players have been wise in forsak- ing the trade unionists, who have done so much in helping them to assert and insist on their rights. From the point of view of spert— and by sport I mean chivalry—it is not nice to think that footballers aro first a trade society and afterwards men who arc engaged in a fine, manly pastime that has to do with tho physical wellbeing of the youth of the country;. but, how- ever much we may be disposed to blink at facts, we have tho sure knowledge that with the de- velopm,ent of the League, and the growing fond- ness for competitive games, there has come a

spirit of commercialism, severe and undoubted, that at times makes one quite uncertain whether the sport of tho thing is the first con- sideration; whether it is not all a question of pounds, shillings, and pence. The "011 Boys" Trouble. ".

It is admitted that few clubs are paying con- cerns; there is not much money to be made out of running a big professional football team, but tho system is wrong. The foundation of the game as it is understood to-day is wrong; there is too much of the circus atinpsphere about the game. • The deep-thinking member of the Foot- ball Association knows this to be true; and now tho trouble with the Players has been settled the question of overhauling the whole government of the game must be considered. One does not like continually to harp upon the breach between the High Holborn Parliament and the Old Boyd; but reference to it is inevitable when one is de- sirous of seeing the entire football world at rest. It does not matter in the least degree, to my mind, whether the Football Association make overtures to the amateurs or the amateurs come to the Football Association. One side must give way. The time has come when dignity might very well be put at one side; there is on'y need for tolerance and magnanimity. The one desire should be to forgive and forget. All amateurs must swear allegiance to the Football Associa- tion. So long as the public schoolboy is shut out and boycotted by the ruling body, EO long will the best class of pcople hold aloof from As- sociation football. The amateur and the profes- sional must play together again. Calm reflection on the situation—a situation that ought never to have arisen—shows that there can be no re- presentative English football so king as one class of player is taboo. Bradford's Bold Policy.

The officials of the Bradford City clut are comparatively young in the ways of bow best to run a big profekeional team, but those who have taken note of what they have done will give them credit for the display of business ability. They have always pursued a bold policy; their many unhappy experiences last year have not discouraged them in the least, and to-day the Bradford City team 16 well up to the best tradi-

Lions of the First League. The engagement of Maskery, of Derby County, means that the City have now ono of, the -very best goalkeepers in the country. Derby has been remarkably prolific in the production of goalkeepers. First they brought out Robinnon, who appeared -for his coinary so many. times. Then came Fryer, now with Fulham, and afterwards Mockery. It is difficult to account for the migration of such talented custodian from Derby. However, per- haps the County have another ready to fill the breach.

, Manchester United are very lucky in picking up such a promising young centre-forward as homer of Kidderminster. He is shaping very nicely, and with James Turnbull expiating his offences against orderliness he has a rare oppor- tunity of • making a big name. He should cer- tainly develop in his present company. How many young players would like to be helped by two such grand forwards as Alexander Turn- bull and Halse

Chelsea's Chequered Career. When one consider that the average attend- ance at the matches at Chelsea is 30,000 it is almost impossible to believe that the South- erners are not stronger. Frankly, Chelsea are a very moderate side. An epidemic of injuries has, of course, had a great deal to do with their indifferent displays. But whatever the cause it is alanoet too much to think that the club will continue to hold the London public as they do at present. The mammoth crow& that put in an appearance at Stamford Bridge goes to show that a really great team in the centre of London would be worth untold gold. It is well for Chelsea that they are playing as well—as far as results go—or better than either Woolwich Arsenal or Tottenham Hotepur. Woolwich are having no-luck at all; the goal which lost them the game against Bradford City ought never to have been scored. It was the greatest fluke imaginable. The value of an old and experienced player is strikingly proved by the success of Holford, who, when Stoke could no longer afford to keep him, went to Manchester City. There was a time when I thought Holford to be the greatest little centre-half since Johnny Holt, who will best be remembered as the leader of Everton's middle division. Holford has charge of the forwards at Manchester, and, if only because he can shoot with much accuracy and force, is indispensable to a side fighting for promotion. He won the game for his club against West Bromwich —II4E. TALAce SH A R'pyt 0 &tie

Albion a Iseek ago; and on the same day Hed- ley, who two.yearz ago had so much to do with the defeat of Newcastle United in the Cup final, scored the goal which drew Wolverhampton's game with Fulham. Still Finding the Net. Crystal Palace deserve to be complimented übon their splendid goal average. There is nothing like it in the Southern League. They have a rare defence—really wonderful, judged by figures—and a better scoring line of forwards one could scarcely desire to see. If they can keep UD thair nr44,64mt form Crystal Palneo will again give a great deal ot trouble in the Uup ties. One will never forget that they once de- feated Newcastle United in the first round ut St. James's Park.
