FROM WARRINGTON TO WIEN AND BACK.

WITH SOME IMPRESSIONS BY THE WAY.' (SPECIALLY WRITTEN FOR "THE EXAMINER" BY ALDERMAN BENNETT).

ARTICLE IV.

===== 0002601_19091113_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
We had arranged to leave Stuttgart early on Wednesday morning, but, before we started, I found time to rush once more across the Schloss Platz, gaze up in the daylight at the Jubilee Column, crowned with its great Concordia, pass the Alte Schloss, or quaint Old Palace, with its three round towers and old-world look, and, in a small square in the shadow of the two-towered Sttftskirche, founded far away in the twelfth century and used for Protestant worship since 1534, to pay my homage at the statue of the poet Schiller, one of the great German singers whom I dearly love. Then, hurry- ing back amid the fountains, and the flower beds, and the trees and statues, past the Prinzen-bau and the Konigs-bau, and the great Post Office, exactly opposite the Hotel Marquart, I found that I had time to buy some German " cigaretten "—half paper mouthpiece, half vile tobacco—and to join a bustling throng of fellow-passengers at one of the big, long, low platforms at the Central Station; to fish out my coupons and find my luggage; and then to once more scramble hastily into a German train. The weather had repented, and the day was fine as we sped out through Cannstatt, and climbed the right bank of the Neckar, through one of the loveliest districts in Swabia, on our way to Ulm. The hills were clad with vines, much taller and more picturesque than those which we had seen at Rilly and on leaving Rheims, and presently we passed through Esslingen, a free imperial city once, still partly girded by protecting walls, and now the centre of the busy trade in sparkling Neckar wine. Here, too, you see the largest engineering works in Wurtemburg, and here the Neckar needs a bridge above a hundred yards in length to span its broadening waters. The town can boast a population of some thirty thousand; but I have not space to dwell upon its churches, or on the old Rat haus, originally the home of the poet Count of Wurtemburg,or the lions of the Hohenstauen, hewn in stone; or to more than mention Plochingen, with its fortified church, or Giippingen, a town with twenty thousand citizens which boasts a mineral spring; or Geislingen, where the poet, Schubart, once eked out a living as a humber usher in a school. _ _ _

From hence, the railway climbed a wooded limestone hill to the tableland of the Swabian Alb, the watershed between the Neckar and the Danube, and a second engine has to be invoked to aid in the propulsion of the train. At Beimerstetten, after travelling over fifty miles, the line descends towards the valley of the Danube, and presently the fortifications of Ulm come into view; we pass by Wilhelmsburg, the lofty citadel where, after the battle of Elchingen, in 1805, some 30,000 Austrians, under General Mack, surrendered to the brave -French Marshal, Ney. And now we have arrived at Ulm—" Ulm-on-the-Danube," as its citizens proudly call it; an ancient town with 56,000 inhabitants, exclusive of New Ulm. The seat of a Carlovingian Palace, and one of the must important free imperial cities of the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries, Ulm has belonged to Wurtemberg sinde 1810. From 1842 to 1866 it was a fortress of the• German Confederation; since 1871 it has been a fortress of the German Empire; and it has a garrison of 7,500 men-12,000 somebody informed us in the afternoon.

It was one of the many admirable features in Mr. Aldridge's arrangements that our hotels, where possible, were situated- near the Station,and the Russischer Hof, at Ulm, almost adjoined it and looked out across a pleasant square. As soon as we had seen to the arrival of our bags, we re-assembled in the entrance hall to find a little company of cordial Ulmfies ready to conduct us to the famous Miinster, said to be the largest Gothic church in Germany, and to boast the loftiest spire in the world. Cologne and Strassburg both must "pale their ineffectual spires" before it, for it is thirteen feet higher than the first one, and sixty-two feet higher than the other; is a good deal more than twice the height of our own tall spire, and more than half the height of the immense Tour Eiffel. Babel, probably, was scarcely in it. Very beautiful both spire and build- ing looked as suddenly we reached the corner of the Minster Platz, and paused to gaze up at their fine proportions; to observe the interesting sculptures on the portals, and the sparrow with the straw in its beak, high up upon the roof, the legendary emblem of the city. The Church is Protestant ;is said to be capable of accommodating 28,000 people; was, founded in 1377; built at intervals down to the beginning of the sixteenth century; and restored and completed from 1844 to 1890. We passed inside to find the great nave filled with a great congregation, and to take our seats and listen to the Organ and Violin Recital which, I understand, is given every day; and very sweet it was to sit and dream to such high music, thrilling through the aisles and slender pillars, dying down to tender cadences, or thundering triumphantly amid the splendours of the lofty nave. The organ, with its 101 stops and its 6,200 pipes, is itself a thing to wonder at, but with that plaintive, palpitating violin to interweave its subtle melodies amid -the deeper strains of the celestial music, it became almost a thing to worship.

And when, at last, the matchless sounds had 'died away, and silently the crowd of eager auditors dispersed, and one by one we rose reluctantly to view the building, we felt like sad sky-pilgrims come back from some heavenly star to common earth again, and did not like the change. But the Cathedral was itself inspiring, and we soon surrendered our reluctant souls to its peculiar charms, and gazed with growing interest at the numerous escutcheons of the Swabian families that decked its walls; the modern windows, the pulpit, with its great carved cover ; the fine stone ciborium, 93 feet high ; the fresco of the Last Judgment, above the great arch of the choir; the choir stalls, wonderfully carved by Syrlin the Elder, and said to be amongst the finest of the kind in Germany; and,not the least of these abounding charms, the beautiful High Altar. In the South Chapel we were shown a lovely portrait of Eitel Besserer by Martin Schaffner, and, in the Neithart Chapel, the original design for the tower, worked out with a beauty of con- ception, a fidelity to detail and a charm of draughtmanship which made the ablest architects amongst us mute with envy and with admiration.

===== 0002601_19091113_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
We left the Illiinster Platz reluctantly and, sauntering on through one or two quaint streets, came to the ancient Rathaus, built at the beginning of the sixteeenth century and • beautiful externally *Rh coloured frescoes. At the south-east corner is a handsome fountain, and adjacent to it a statue of the Emperor, William the First. Escorted by our civic friends, we entered the old building, and, passing up some stairs into the old-world CouncilChamber,were most warmly welcomed by the Oberblirgomaster (Herr von Wagner), and privileged, as we had been at Stuttgart, to sit down at a great horse-shoe table and listen' to a full description of the .place ; to see the usual model of the city ;.to inspect its plans • and then, in an adjacent room, to gaze admir- ingly upon a large collection of photographs which gave us some conception of the numerous model dwellings for the working- classes which, with singular public spirit, have been erected here by the municipality and other bodies.l was also specially interested in the designs for a fine new bridge across the Danube, which was, I understood, to be built of ferro-concrete. I have elsewhere described at length the distinctive .municipal features of the city, and spoken of the civic policy which has secured for it the admiration of Housing and Municipal Reformers all the world over, and I will merely say that its fundamental principle is municipal owner- ship. Beginning with the fortifications, which it bought from the Imperial Govern- ment in 1902. Ulm is continually adding to its municipal possessions, and it owns, to- day,above three quarters of the land within its boundaries. And consequently, Ulm, unlike the towns and cities of this country can say quite truthfully : "I am the Master of my Fate, I am the Captain of my Soul." It is able to lay out it streets, gardens, parks and buildings in its own way. Its civic enterprize is not obstructed at every turn by some obstreperous and selfish individual whose dirty little bit of property, in Warrington at any rate, so often blocks the way to some most necessary public scheme. And what is the result? The city claims to have made, within the last five years, a profit of a quarter of a million marks ; the value of its land is said to have increased by a million and a half; and I may add that its local taxes are the lowest of all the greater towns of Wurtemberg. I am a believer in the principle of taxing the unearned increment. I am utterly opposed to the policy of laissez faire which still permits a man to exact for any piece of land he has to sell a price beyond its real value, and, in case of a dispute, imposes on the town the tedious and expensive remedy called Arbitration, with all its retinue of parasites who prey upon our civic needs. But Ulm has found a better way. It is to purchase all the land which it can get at a fair price, and, if it sells a part of it, to retain the right to buy it back at the same price, and so to keep the whole of the increment which may accrue for the community who made it. In Warrington, unless we actually need a piece of land for some immediate public purpose, our one desire appears to he to instantly get rid of it, and as to buying land because it is a bargain, scarcely anyone will hear of our doing anything so sensible, and the mere suggestion sends our sage municipal economists into a fever. Moreover, up to now, our English laws compel us to dispose of any surplus land as soon as we have finished our improvement, and so some lucky citizen steps in and can eventually claim the extra value due to Warrington's initiative entirely for himself. Some day every yard of land in Bridge-street may be worth three times its present value. But Warrington will not be able to obtain a cent of it. Unless, indeed, we waken up and try to frame our laws to benefit the many rather than assist the selfish few. But lam digressing. The civic trams awaited us outside the Rathaus, and gently bore us back to the Hotel, where the Oberburgomaster and his estimable colleagues gave us a delightful lunch. Herr von Wagner was a typical German, a pleasant, dumpy, little fellow with a yellowish moustache who might have stepped right out of some cartoonist's album. But no Mayor in England and no Oberburgo- master in the German Empire has done more to further civic progress and the public welfare, and to bring "the ideal city" right within the range of practical politics than Herr von Wagner. Of course we toasted him with great enthusiasm, and it was amusing to observe the gallantry with which he walked about the room to clink his glass with every lady in the party, and to make to each a most profound obeisance; and, eloquent as all our spokesmen were, we all felt glad when Mr. Aldridge called upon Miss Gurney to sum up in a few charming words what every one of us was feeling. All afternoon we rode about this pleasant little city, visiting the model houses, girt with gardens, and obviously in many cases based upon the methods of our English Garden City and Suburbs rather than upon the lines of those big tenements which we had seen at Stuttgart, and which still alas, pre- vail in many of the German towns! But many of the houses here had been erected by the Corporation, for Ulm is not deterred from doing the wise and proper thing because some selfish group of interested persons skillfully set up an obvious scarecrow and, with cries and shrieks and lamentations, called out "Socialism I" We noted many soldiers, as we drove about the city—fine, stalwart, well trained service- able men they were—but everywhere the fortifications were disappearing and broad and pleasant boulevards arising in their place. One feature in the city plan was the provision of direct roads from the gates of the old wall to the main roads leading to the country. Another was to set apart specific portions of the town for special purposes, a feature which prevails in other German towns. For instance, miscellaneous trades, small indus- tries and dwelling houses for the general population are located in the valley between Ulm and Ssflingen. The greater industries are carried on in the district to the east of Soflingen, and directly connected with the railway. Moreover, special areas are set apart for the workpeople and the peasantry, for ordinary family houses, for villas and better class houses, and "for the best class people," to quote the memorandum which had been prepared for us—" the millionaires' quarter," our conductor grandly called the latter; whilst the woods at Friedrichsau are i carefully reserved for sport and recreation; ,

===== 0002601_19091113_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
and "for a future harbour, a land in the east of Friedrichsau, near the Danube." We saw the old bridge, as we drove about in the bright sunshine, and the hollow which betrayed the presence of the famous river, but we did not actually catch a glimpse of its green waters, and I had to wait for the expected thrill until a later opportunity. The German gentleman who was in my carriage was, like all our hosts, extremely kind and anxious to assist us. He told us that the nervous twitching which we noticed in his face was the result of a duel, and that, as a matter of fact, he had fought not less than seven in his military days. He seemed, indeed, to be distinctly proud of this, and talked of duelling as "a fine sport." But he was a humane and splendid fellow, and, as we passed the Military Prison, sadly spoke of it as "the dark house," and said that he did not like it, confiding to us that he had spent a fortnight there or in some similiar institution,whether as a, penalty for duelling or not I cannot say. After visiting a big and handsome building belonging to the muncipality and mainly occupied by single young women, we drove a little distance out into the country to another singularly pleasant group of work- men's cottagesrand went inside, and chatted with their clean and happy and contented population. There was not any carriage- way between_ the houses, merely a broad path amid the flowers. One friendly dame insisted on loading two or three of us with a fine collection of nasturtium seeds of her own growing, and all of us were fain to linger in this veritable Garden Suburb, resting peace- fully amid its leaves and blossoms on that sweet September afternoon. I have already alluded to the fact that the Corporatiou of Ulm has itself erected many, houses for the working people (175 in all),but it may interest our local Housing Reformers , to know that this good work is supplemented by not less than four great voluntary societies and that the city lets them have the land at a low rate in order to encourage them. In three of these societies, the interest payable to the shareholders is limited to four per cent. In the fourth, each mem- ' ber has to give security for not less than 500 marks, and may become the purchaser of his I own property. The company builds the houses, the ground remaining the property I of the town, and, at the end of eighty years, the town agrees to buy the houses at 80 per cent. of the building value. Most of these houses, following the English custom, are for one' family only. The rest of them are usually let in flats, according to the , German method. Some of the indus- trial concerns have also erected suitable dwellings for their emp/oyes, and the State of Wurtemberg has built over a hundred for the railway staff. Altogether, not less than 388 buildings, with 1,006 flats, for 5,000 inhabitants, have been provided for the populace of Ulm under conditions which preclude an increase in the rents, however the land may rise in value; and it is not any wonder that the Corporation proudly claim, in characteristic English, that "Ulm stands foremost as one of the best towns providing for cheap ground and for workpeople's comforts." •

The final visit which we paid was to the Wagner School, so called in honour of the Oberburgomaster. We were particularly struck by its magnificent installation of shower baths, and local educationists may be interested to learn that Ulm has nine school doctors, a school dentist and a dental clinic.

Our hosts became our guests at night, and Mr. Aldridge asked me to take charge of another typical old German. He scarcely knew a word of English, and, as I have said, I scarcely knew a word of German, but, through sheer good-fellowshii), we somehow managed to make ourselves understood. We had toasted each other in a light wine of the country, and I asked him afterwards to join me at "a liqueur of the country." He genially declined, and I asked him, then, to kindly pick one out for me. To this he smilingly assented. I shall never forget that liqueur. It reminded me of the Irish Member's joke about the whisky which " went down one's throat like a torchlight procession," and it made me pull a very wry face, The amuse- ment which the incident occasioned to my friend was a sight for the gods, and I strongly suspect that the dear old fellow was having a little joke at my expense. How- ever, I enjoyed it quite as much as he. _ _

Amongst the after dinner speeches, which were all felicitous, was one from M. Georges Benoit-Levy, the Secretary of the French Garden City Association, who had come over specially from Paris to meet us, and who greatly pleased me by his generous tribute to Mr. Ebenezer Howard, the founder of the Garden City movement. Afterwards, no doubt remembering the previous night, we naturally fell into song, and I shall long recall the light which came into those kindly German faces as they joined in rolling out a noble patriotic ballad of the Rhine. Of course we sang a song of England in return. It was the song which Mrs. Ehrenbacher had so sweetly sung for us in Stuttgart—the lovely "Home, Sweet Home ;" but was it any wonder that, amid the glamour of our strange surroundings and the universal atmosphere of kindliness and goodwill we changed the final word, and sang with might and main, to the immense delight of our warm-hearted auditors:

"Mid pleasures and places though we may roam, Be it ever so humble there's no place like Ulm " ? At the suggestion of a very genial little English tutor (the only Englishman in the town I think), who had accompanied our party during the afternoon, I left the heated atmos- phere of the Hotel when the dinner and con- cert were over,glad to get a breath of the cool, fresh,night air ; and as my brief glimpse of the Danube had been so unsatisfactory-, I asked him to conduct me hither. It was considerably after eleven by this, and, if Stuttgart, on the night of my arrival, had seethed singularly silent and deserted, Ulm looked positively lonely now. As we strolled along, I told him of my German comrade's joke with the liqueur, and laughed out ' heartily as I recalled the old man's mirth. " Please do not laugh or talk so loudly," he observed. "It is forbidden aften ten o'clock in Ulm." I stared at him, and thought that he, too, in his turn was having a joke at my expense; but he told me that this was one of the local regulations, intended, I understood, to promote the orderliness of the city and to protect the worthy populace from any interruption in their virtuous slumbers; and he added that any wayfarer who disobeyed was liable to spend the night in the lock-up. I felt inclined to laugh again, but thought better of it, for my friend was perfectly serious, As we proceeded, we met some other mem- bers of our party, who were also out for a quiet ramble in the still night air. "Where are you going ? " they asked. "We're off to

===== 0002601_19091113_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMAN'S METAL POLISH

the Danube 1 " I replied. The object of our pilgrimage apparently did not appeal to them, and so we went upon our solitary way. Presently we heard a roar of falling water and dropped across a stream which dipped beneath the street which we were traversing and vanished in a white cascade; and then, diverging, we came right into the oldest part of Ulm—a part we had not seen before—and walked amid a maze of narrow byways and quaint old gabled houses, and, turning down a dark side alley, came upon a little stream which wandered thrdugh a lane of antique buildings. It might have been a bit of Venice or of Amsterdam, but the water gurgled musically, and this was obviously a river, not an arm of the great sea, or even a. canal. It was, however, strangely pictur- esque, and I was not surprised to find that my friend, who was somewhat of an artist, had attempted, more than once, to reproduce its old-world beauty with his gifted brush. It must have been the little river Blau, I think, one of the two small tributaries which joins the Danube here; and, following the sound of it, and presently emerging on a part of the old city walls, and passing a dim tower and archway in the darkness, we could see below, a little to the right of us, a broader stream, which flowed before us dark and sombre, with scarce a solitary gleam reflected in its ffiryving waters. But I knew it instantly. "Can I forget, the night we met Beside the Danube river ? "

I quoted instantly. And I did feel something Df the expected thrill,at last,as we approached the handsome bridge, and, pausing in the centre, gazed down at the dark and supplying stream which flowed through sunny Austria, and by the gates of Wien, and on through Buda-Pesth, and the sad heart of Turkey, to its deep home in the Black Sea. "Let us go to Bavaria," I said suddenly, and we instantly set out across the bridge. But to get into that famous province, it was neces- sary to walk a little further,and to also cross a tiny, silent island, with dark, silent houses in the shadowy streets, and scarcely a sound or a footstep to disturb the vigil of the silent stars; and •preiently we came to a sort of narrow arm of the great river, and, crossing this, too, by a smaller bridge, we stood, almost at midnight, in another country, or at any rate, another province, and also in New Ulm, the modern portion of this very ancient town. Upon the way, my friend had told me how, some centuries before, a citizen of Ulm had sought to solve the problem of human flight by constructing a huge pair of wings and launching himself boldly into the air above the Danube. So far as I could ascertain, he landed in the river; but the story was authenticated by a picture post card proudly shown to me by one of my companions in the train the following day. And, in the year which witnesses the proud solution of the age-old problem, I am fain to pay my little tribute to this quaint old citizen of Ulm who did not fear "To put it to the touch, To win, or lose it all."

My comrade was extremely anxious to introduce me to to the finest lager beer in Europe, which, he said, was brewed at a house in this vicinity, and he said that, if we hastened, we might reach it ere the doors were closed. But it was already nearly twelve, and, as we hurried through the dark and noiseless streets, we heard the bell of the great Miinster tolling out the midnight hour. At last we reached our destination. There were lights in the windows, but, just as we arrived, they were extinguished. My friend knocked loudly on the door, but nobody responded. We were just too late. And so I did not taste the peerless beverage after all. My companion was obviously disappointed, but his kindness had not any limits, and he insisted on accompanying me back to my Hotel; but, ere we reached it, we fell in with Mr. Aldridge and Mr. Shawcross. "Where have you been ?" they asked. "We've been to Bavaria!" I proudly answered. They, however, had been rambling to and fro without any definite objective, and finding that they had not come across the quaint old corner which contained the oldest houses in the city, we immediately consented to conduct them thither, and, in the starlight, traversed the familiar ground again. And then we warmly thanked our tireless guide and, quits worn out, crept willingly upstairs to bed. (To be continued).

THE INFIRMARY.

IN NEED OF MONEY. £1,300 A YEAR WANTED.

The urgent need of funds for the carrying on of the useful work of the Warrington Infirmary was discussed at a special weeting held in the Pavilion on Monday afternoon. Sir Gilbert Greenell, Bart., presided over a small but representative gathering. • The SECRETARY read letters of apology for non- attendance, from Messrs. H S. Bleckly (who increased nis annual subscription to £5), John Fairclough, Hedley H. Taylor (who said the proceeds in connection with the visit of Sir E. H. Shackleton to Warrington in March would be given to the Infirmary). Mrs. Gertrude M. R. Willis (who said that the Rector and she would double their annual subscription and give £2 23.), Miss Massy, Messrs. C. Jackson Holmes and J. Atkinson.

Mr. J. J. Bleakly promised to increase his annual subscription to £lO for three years, and Mr. W. 11. Bleakly £lO 10s. for three years, and Mr. J. J. Whitley promised an annual subscription of £lO 10s. Mr. G. Dakin and Mr. 'Charles White have increased their annual subscriptions to £5 each. £1,300 A YEAR EXTRA WANTED.

Mr. W. PETER riveerips moved the following resolu- tion: "Raving regard to the greatly increased useful- mad of the Institution resulting from its recent enlargement, and to the fact that the growing needs of the people of this industrial district can and are now being more adequately met, as is proved by the adeilienal number of cases treated, this meeting fully realizes that the increased expenditure consequent upon such enlargement must be adequately met or the usefulness of the Institution will be vary seriously curtailed. We therefore pledge ourselves to do all in our power to assist in increasing the revenue of the charity, and we appeal to all the residents of Warring- ton and the surrounding district to help us by generous subscriptions." Mr. . Rylands sketched the history of the Institution and the growth of the work which bad led up to the present requiremets. They had now 80 beds as against 40 before the extension, and an increase of maintenance to the extent of 50 per cent. During the years preced- ing the extension, the income of the Institution had steadily, grown, and grown approximately in proportion to its requirements. Owing to the fact that they made the Institution meet the requirements of the town, naturally the cost of its upkeep was very low in com- parison with the population of the town, and the sudden incrense in tho accommodation naturally resulte l in an equally suiden increase in the cost of the maintenance. Consequently it followed that while they might expee: by the natural process of time their income would gradually grow as it had gradually grown in the past, and become equal to tkeir requirements, just at the time there was a serious hiatus—a serious gulf over which they wished to bridge. It was in view of the importance of that matter that they had sum- moned that meeting in order to consider the question. Before leaving the actual question of • finance, he had taken out the figures of eighteen infirmaries in important towns of a similar size and standing to their own. He found that the average income per 1,000 was £7l, and the average expenditure was £74, showing that in those cases the expenditure generally exceeded the income. The expenditure in Warrington was £72 per 1,000 of the population, so that their expenditure was slightly less than the average of those eighteen representative towns. ' Their income at present was only £5l per 1,000. There were three main sources of income. They were the source of endowment, the source of workmen's contributions. and the source of voluntary subscriptions. He was sorry that their endowments had been at a standstill for some years past. It could only be owing to the fact that the needs of the Infirmary prior to two years ago were not so prominently before them. He hoped as the years went on that they might see that source of revenue increase as it bad increased in other hospitals. The workmen's contributidris had grown steadily, and he believed that this question was before the Working Men's Committee. Probably they would do everything they could to increase that source of revenue. At the present time it exceeded £l,OOO per annum. (Applause.) That was a handsome sum, but he was sore that, having regard to what other industrial centres did, they might reasonably expect that that sum should be substantially increased. The average for other infirmaries was about £1,400. In Wigan, which was perhaps one of the best, the working men's contributions came to £8,371 per annum. He, therefore, thought that War- rington should contribute substantially over £l,OOO. It was a question of organization, and he thought if the employers of labour did something to encourage the men to pay their halfpenny a week, or whatever it was, out of their wages, that almighty halfpenny would amount to a very largo sum in the course of a year. (Applause.) He suggested also to large employers of labour that if the time should come that they could not accommodate themselves to meeting the expenses of the Infirmary, the burden would be very much treater than any sub- scriptions which they might make to it. At the present time, the shortage in income was something like £1,300 a year, and they were extremely anxious to find some means of bringing over that amount. A- good deal could be done by private subscriptions, and they hoped the large firms would recognize the necessity of the cage. While looking to private subscriptions, be also thought the workingmen's organizations would strive to do something to help as well, and that they would thus be able to find the £1,300 a year increased iucome necessary. He wished to emphasise the resolution, trusted that it would receive the cordial sympathy of all in that room, and that it would echo throughout the town outside, and the Board of Management might ' before long cease to feel the keen pinch of poverty which faced them at the present time. (Applause.)

MR. J. J. BLECKLY AND EARLY DAYS.

Mr. J. J. BLECKLY seconded, and said the deficiency this year promised to be something like £1,441, and over and above that there vvas an accumulated defici- ency of .C 1,872. He did not think it desirable to jump at that all at once, but it was claimed that there should be a revenue of £5,000 a year. Am.,ngst the list of sub- scribers there were various compmies-100 companies and private firms and about 300 private subscribers and the Board of Guardians and the Corporation of Warring- ton. The Guardians and the Corporation were very much relieved in their duties by the operations of the Infirmary and Dispensary. Instead ot subscribing £l7 103. they ought to ciaim from them .C 270 at the very least and they ought to make a strong appeal on that ground. (Hoar, hear and applause.) Then about the workingmen's contribution. Here Mr. Itylands was quite right, and when he said that the Institu- tion might be taken out of their hands and administered by the Government he forgot that not only would the ratepayers have to contribute whether they would or no, but the workmen would contribute out of the rates in the same manner and to a much larger extent than at present. (Hear, hear). He appealed to the local patriotism of them all. Here they had a great Institution, which they had inheritei from their predecessors and which wt.s commenced 100 years ago on a very modest scale. He recollected it 50 years ago when it was 'erected in the Market place and was called the Dispensary, and their much respected friend, Mr. Charles White, was Dispenser and Doctor. En those days he was called " Charlie White," but there were few of them now who would venture to address him by that familiar name as they looked upon him as the doyen of the medical profession in the town. (Applause).

A MILLION PENNY FUND.

Mr. J. 11. SMETHURST supported the resolution, and said that during the past three years he (the speaker) had probably seen as much, if not more, of the internal working of the Institution than any other member, and he could say perfectly straightforwardly with Mr. Rylands that every penny that could he saved had been saved. (Applause.) Ho did not think it was at all creditable to the people of the term that the institution should be kept up by a comparatively few. If the people who used the Institution wished to keep it efficient they should be the first to come forward. Ile agreed with Mr. Bleckly that the Town Council and the Board of Guardians were not doing their duty to that charity. The cause of humanity demanded that they should do something more for their fellow men than throw the burden on the rates. Lie contended that the entrance hall should be improved. When an accident case was brought in, probably It man badly mangled, he had to be taken past the patients and their friends, and that was a case in point where more money would he needed. It had been suggested and practically adopted that they should try a million penny fund to enable every person to contribute some- thing to help on the useful work which was being carried on in that Institution. That did not mean that they would not ask for more than a penny; indeed one gentleman had said that he would be pleased to give a 1,000 pennies. (Applause.) In conclusion, Mr. Smethurst referred to the good work done for the Institution by the children and Mr. J. Moore Murray and others, and said he had no doubt that the rTheme would go forward if they could get hold of tho young people. With more money they might have a con- valescent home to which they could send poor children. (Applause.) Mr. WILLIAM LONG willingly and cordially endorsed the sentiments of the resolution, lie thought he should like to use a Lancashire phrase and " rub in " some of the remarks of the last speaker. (Hear, hear.) After What Mr. Rylands had said with regard to the figures, he could not say that he felt very proud of Warrington. They had been provided with that handsome building —not so much handsome as useful, and competent for any work to be carried on in it—aLd ho did not think that it was very much to their credit that

they had not already assessed themselves, which he thought in those matters they all ought to do. (Applause,) A man should consider what his income was, and what he could afford for the best institution in the town. He earnestly begged all present to go home and think in what way they could best help the Institu- tion and what their subscription ought to be, from their position and obligations, whether they were rich or poor. He hoped they would all be proud of the place and help it forward by every means in their power. (Applause). A CENTENARY APPROACHING. Mr. ROBERT PIERPOINT also supported the motion, and said it might be worth noting in appealing to the public, as they were going to appeal, that the Dis- pensary, which was actually the Infirmary, was opened on the 12th of April, 1810, in a building rented by the committee in the Market Place, but in the year 1818 neat 11 nd commodious erection was built for the per- manent use of that valuable establishment at a cost of £1,030, in Buttermarket-street, where about j,OOO • persons yearly partook of its benefits. If a population of not more than 14,000 could be so generous' when there were no great works, it was very proper that they should remember what had been done in the past, arm what ought to be done in a community like Warrington was at the present day. (Applause.) The burden of a great institution was placed on very few. He endorsed as strongly as he could the warning to the town against getting this institution taken over as a Government department. From his experience, which was a fairly long one, he had the greatest horror of any institution becoming the prey or the care, whichever they chose, of a Government department. He urged upon the town to make the Infirmary a voluntary institution and support it as generously as it ought to be supported. (Applause.) The resolution was then put, and carried unanimously. THE COMMODORE'S BRACELET

BY HAROLD BINDLOSS author of "His Adversary's Daughter," "Held Up," "The Kingdom of Courage," " Ainslie's Ju-Ju," "A Wide Dominion," &e.

The second day of the regatta was over, and it was a fine summer evening, when Gellatly strolled up and down the railed-off portion of the pier behind the yacht club pavilion ,with Miss Millicent Aylmer, who listened with exemplary patience while he •discoursed upon chains and anchors and run- ning gear. Gellatly was an enthusiast, and, .in some respects, painfully young, or he -might have chosen topics she would have found more pleasure in. The girl was not only very pretty, but got up very 'daintily in blue serge and little pipe-clayed shoes, while it is possible she had a reason for being gracious to him, since there were .men much 'better worth powder and shot who would have been glad to stroll about the pier -with her. There were also elaborately- dressed women, waiting to hear what arrangt- ments the committee had made for tht 'Ladies' Cup Race on the morrow. It was an event of some importance, that fit, -socially, for the chief proviso was that each yacht must be steered by a lady, though men who sailed for sailing's sake, and valued their 'boats, had very little to say in its favour. At 'last a door in the pavilion opened, and Nortii- rop, Gellatly's skipper, who came out and -found Millicent Aylmer and his amateur hand leaning. upon the rails, endeavoured to make the latter understand that his company could be dispensed with. It was, however, not every day that a girl like Millicent Aylmer was gracious to young Gellatly; and he stayed where he was, regardless of the fact •-that one of his shoes had burst during the racing, and there was rest from an old chain :cable, as well as salt stains, on his clothing. "It's all arranged," said Northrop. "You are to sail the boat for me, Millicent, and we have Peyton for third hand. We allow a minute to Octoroon, and she is the boat I am -most afraid of. Miss Jardine steers her." Miss Aylmer sinned -somewhat curiously. 4." I wonder if you know that Lucy Jaidine is not exactly a dear friend of mine?" she said. -"You may have noticed, too, that Peyton is about a good deal with her, and be is a man I have no great liking for either. Why didn't .he go in Octoroon with Lucy?" "I don't know," said Northrop. " Alay way, the Commodore asked me to take him, and he's rather a good hand, though I'm no fonder of him than you seem to be. Oh, 'there's the secretary wanting me again!" He left them. evidently against his will, and Gellatly and Mies Aylmer stayed where they were, looking down on the sliding green water and listening to the band. At last the girl smiled at her companion. "I mean to win that cup for the skipper. and the Commodore's bracelet for myself, if I can." she said. Gellatly laughed. "It's rather curious, but I fancy I just heard Miss Jardine say the same thing. She's talking to Peyton yonder." Millicent Aylmer turned, and saw a man 'leaning over the back of a lounge chair in which another young woman, also attired in blue serge, sat not far away. Her voice reached them plainly. One would 'fancy from the handicap that it lies between Northrop's boat and Octoroon." "von certainly shall heal it if I can do anything towards arrangire the thing." ."But you are sailing in Northrop's Nile enna!" said his companion. The man laughed again. "Yes," he said, “I believe I am." Millicent Aylmer glanced at young Gellatly ns she drew him nway. "If people i will talk in a loud voice when there are other folks about they can't comnlain of being over- heard," she said. "Of course. he mayn't have meant anything in particular, but I don't -like Mr. Peytnn." - Gellatly didn't either; but Northrop. who appeared just then. signed to him imperi- ously, and he excused himself and joined him. "I think it's about time you went off and helped Jimmy to Straighten the boat up," he said. "Any way. one would fancy Miss Aylmer has had enough of Yon. You, don't suppose a girl of her kind thinks of nothing except sails and anchors!" Young Gellatly went off to the Nirvana, end presently sat in her forecastle talking to Jimmy, the red-haired paid hand. The latter expectorated disgustedly when the race was mentioned. "If to-morrer was over, and t•nli and me all safe, I'd be glad." be said. "If {here's a breeze them young women is going to drown somebody. ' After , that they jumped for sheets and hal- liards, and the great sails came down, while when they swung to their anchor ten . minutes later a boat did away from the Octoroon's side with a very wet and equally ragged man in it. Miss Aylmer, who saw him, smiled at Northrop. "It you were right—and, now I remember how /he bent on the topsail, I think you were —he deserves it," she said. " Lucy's man has evidently" torn mcst • of. his jacket off him. Still, isn't that a protest flag on Octoroon?" " It is," said Northrop, drily. " \\Te haven't got the cup yet.. The committee- will no doubt go into the thing this evening." He was quite correct. There as a very solemn meeting a few . hours late , in the \r Yacht Club pavilion, but before it was over Northrop, who came out smiling, found Milli- cent Aylmer and young Gellatly waiting him on the pier. It was, naturally, the girl he turned to. / .

" The bracelet's yours, and I almost fancy Peyton isn't feeling exactly • happy just now, he said. " Still, we'll no doubt hear what the Commodore thought of him to-morrow, and there are One or two things I want to talk -about in the meanwhile."

Young Gellatly saw the girl's face flush, for it was clear moonlight, and it occurred to him that they Would be just as content without a third • that night. He afterwards felt more sure of it when, leaning on the rails of the pier, •he watched two shadowy figures saunter away down the lonely beach. In any case, .when Millicent Aylmer came bask, she met Miss Jardine on the steps of the pier, and the latter returned her greet- •ing with very scant civility. I was never a great admirer of Henry Northrop, but I scarcely expected what he did to-day even from him," she said. "Still, I suppose you don't mind so long as he has given you the bracelet." Millicent laughed a little. "1 claresay you can't help being angry, but you . evidently dcn't understand," she said. " You see, Mr. Peyton didn't fall in. He went in, on pur- pose, and that we why we left him for you. As to the bracelet, you can have, it if you really want it ,very much. Harry has given me something I value a good deal more than that."

She glanced down at one ungloved hand on which there flashed a ring, .but Miss Jardine did not seem to notice it, and gazed at her in blank astonishment. "You are quite ,sure? You daren't tell me he went in on purpose if it wasn't so." "I think," said Millicent, quietly. "you should .ask one of the committee. If they hadn't believed he threw himself in so we should have to pick him up they certainly wouldn't have awarded Us the cup." Miss Jardine turned and left her. Still, she evidently made inquiries, for when Peyton came upon her a little later he heard suffi- cient to make his ears tingle; and, having a good deal of assurance, betook himself for consolation to the smoking-room attached to the Pavilion.

"If I can have this Ladies' Clip abolished it will be clone," he said. "It's an ab- surdity under any circumstances. Women aren't to be trusted with a boat: 'Today one of them unconcernedly left me behind to drown, and another did her utmost to knock my brains out. Then, as if that was not suffi- cient, she turned round and abused me savagely." The man he spoke to laughed. • "I almost think the less you say about • it the better it will be. You could hardly expect Miss Jar- dine to be flattered by your thinking she would appreciate the little game you evi- dently meant to play. In fact, from what the Commodore said, one could fancy you were lucky that they didn't ask you to get out of the club."

November 13, 1909. LOCAL PARLIAMENT.

POOR LAW REFORM BILL.

Poor Law Reform was the subject for debate at the weekly meeting of the Warrington Parliamentary Debating Society on Tuesday evening, a Bill "to pro- vide for the better working of the laws relating to the relief of poor persons in England and Wales," being introduced by the President of the Board of Trade Mr. J. McDermid). Tee full provisions of the Bill were given in last Saturday's "Examiner." Mr. MCDERMID, in introducing the Bill, traced the history of the Poor Law. He said it was the fashion to decry the Act of 1834, but there could be little doubt that for the first forty years of its existence it did its work both economically and efficiently. The rise and development of the great towns arid cities in more recent times had,however, changed the aspect of the problem, and there was a well-founded impression that the existing Act did not deal adequately with the industrial and social conditions of the present day. The growth of expenditure on the Poor Law had been enormous and out of all proportion to the numbers relieved. It had increased from 5.;.1 millions in 1849 to 15 millions in 1908, and in the same period the cost per head had risen from loss than £6 to over £l6. Under the Act of 1834 a gross sum of ovEr £600,000,000 had been spent on poor relief. There was a very prevalent feel- jug that we did not get value for the money spent. The Bill now introduced was substantially on the lines of the majority report of the recent Commission. It would, he claimed, secure proper classification, increased efficiency of administration, and a just and equitable distribution of the burden of cost. It would reduce the number of authorities from 643 to 135, and in abolishing Boards of Guardians it would carry out the practically unanimous recommendation of the Com- mission. It was worthy of note that one of the most democratic franchises we possessed had proved a failure in Guardians' electious, only one tourth of the electors exercising their power of voting. The co-ordination of existing charitaLle agencies would prevent overlapping and enormous waste. The evils of the present. system of mixed Workhouses were generally recognized and separate classification would be made possible by the extension of the area of administration which would in many eases leave avail- able within the area several separate establishments where the different classes of persons requiring relief could be dealt with. It had long been telt that the Workhouse was no place for children. A large pro- portion were now dealt with in other institutions, but a percentage remained in the Workhouses, and the associations must have a damaging effect upon their future careers. There was a growing opinion in favour of the traditional Scottish system of boarding out. Aged folks ought not to be forced to associate with depraved and disreputable characters. The out-relief at present given to widows with families was often inadequate. That people should be disfranchised for receiving medical relief was outside the region of Common sense. Mr. G. WILSON (Hyde, C.) seconded the adoption of the Bill.

Mr. W. FURNESS (N. Bristol, L.) moved that the Bill be read that day three months. He described the Bill as one of the most extravagant that any Government could conceive. No idea of the cost of administration had been put before the House,but he had no hesitation in sayieg that it would involve the ratepayers lean expen- diture of about £16,000,000. The Bill would have the opposite effect to what was intended. How could they reduce the expenditure when they would at once be called upon to erect new buildings to accommodate the various classes. The alteration of areas and the adop- tion of the co-optive principle would add to the com- plex character of the adruinistrttion. It had been estimated that the recommendations of the Royal Commission would in% olve Bolton in an expen- diture of £60,000, if put into operation there, and that would apply to a large number of boroughs. In Warrington all the machinery was in the borough and therefore tthe' county would have to make pro- vision for new institutions to deal with the rural parts of the Union. The present expenditure in the War- rington Union, was about Os. 20. per head, of which 6Ad. went to the maintenance of paupers, the balance being absorbed by administration expenses. The present Bill would probably increase that cost and would place a millstone round the neck of the rate- payer, without lessening the number of paupers. An irresponsible authotity would beset up, power being given to it to spend money without public control. He had no objection to women serving on Boards of Guardians—they were very useful--but he would limit the proportion of lady guardians to one-fourth. Women were inclined "to think with their hearts" instead of with their brains. Under the existing Poor Law, Boards of Guardians had power to classify the inmates, if they desired. The provisions embodied in the Bill were opposed by the majority of Poor Law administrators and clerks of Unions who were convinced that they were not in the interests of the poor or of the ratepayers. Mr. P. O'BRIEN (North St. Pancras, L.) seconded the amendment. •

Mr. W. NIGHTINGALE (NOTMantnn, S.) -said he rose neither to curse nor to bless. He considered the arguments advanced by Mr. Furness-, were pre- posterouig. There was, however, nothing in the measure that went to the .root of the npitter ; it was only patching up. It, made one sick to hear the talk of expense. The country was full of Money, but it was in the wrong hands. (Laughter.) The speaker expressed disapproval of the present "humbugging "voluntary systems of administering charity. "Give me tbe State,' he declared. The Poor Law was a canker which, unless it was removed, would kill the country.

, _ ..... Mr. G. H. W. STEEL (First Lord of the Admiralty) contended that Mr. Furness had condemned his. own , arguments by pointing to the enormous expense incurred in administration under the present system. The exist- ing Poor Law was a disgrace to any civilised country. The present Government had introduced measures of Church reform, electoral reform, and Poor Law reform, and when they had finished with the present pleasure they would bring in tariff reform—(laughter) to find the money to pay for it. When the Conservatives brought in their Budget real unemploy- ment would be done away with and there would only be the "unemployables." (A Voice : " Mr. Balfour d es not say so.") With regard to co-opted members, he contended that the syetem worked well on the educa- tion committees, and he protested against the uncalled for remarks of the member for Nord; Bristol regarding women members. As to the proposed detention colonies for persons of "vicious : ad pernicious habits," he (the speaker) would advocate not detentien but extermination. (A Nationalist Member: "A good old Tog policy.") Mr. FELIX MAGINN (W. Clare, N.) said he objected to the Bill, not because of its inherent defects, but because he suspected the motives that had caused it to be brought forward. He was oue of the people who suspected the motives of the Education Act of 1902. He regarded it as an Act to dis- solve School Boards and to throw the apple of religious discord into municival elections. It operated very, successfully on behalf of the Conservative party. He saw in the present Bill exactly the same tactics lepeated—anotber elective body dissolved, another nominated body set up. He utterly distrusted the Conservatives when they masqueraded as reformers. They always had their eyes upon party advantage. Ile believed the moving reason for the introduction of the present measure was: How many votes they could gain from the poor and the keen sympathisers with the poor and how much authority they could gain by putting their friends and nominees on the Public Assistence Authorities. If the present attempt stood by itself, he would not be so free in his assertions. At the present time e Conservative party was going about the countrythanking God for the House of Lords and callin upon that House to defend the country against the democratically elected Holl£o of Commons. The Conservatives were at present, through their organs, putting before the public a scheme of peasant proprietorship, but it must be within the recollection of many members of the House that the Conservative party thirty years ago condemned peasant proprietor- ship, when it was proposed as a cure for Irish poverty, as confiscation and robbery. Not more than twelve years ago the objection was put for ward that although peasant proprietorship might. be a good thing for Ireland, the Conservative party could never take it up keens° the English peasants and farmers would be wanting it as well, and they could never stand that (A voice: Did they get it ?")On compulsion. The Corsei votive Party generally resisted until they brought the country to the verge of resolution and then turned round and ate their own words. (Laughter.) As to the Education Act of 1902, the School Boards were popularly elected bodies elected on a peculiar franchise which gave minorities representation. The Corservatives swept them away and threw education into the cauldron of municipal contention and resorted to disruptive tactics, thus succeeding in separating the Irish Catholics in this country from their natural allies the Liberals (Cries of" hear, hear" and "no, no.") With regard to old age pensions, the Conservatives opposed a non-contributory scheme in that assembly contending that it would panperiso and degrade the country.

Mr. J. JARMAN (Burton-on-Trent), L.), said he agreed with some parts of the Bill and not with others, and he did not as yet feel justified in voting for its rejection. He did not agree that the Bill would increase adminis- tration expenses, because many of the institutions required for classification had already been provided and the pressure upon them would be relieved by tbe alteration of areas. He did not agree with the principle of co-opting members to public authorities. It was quite time the whole of private charities were at last under State supervision and that we knew the amounts which had been left to them and how the money was being disposed of. If such a proposal bad come from the Liberal side of the House it would have been "confis- cation and robbery." (Laughter.) Coming from the Conservative side, it was simply a mild form of com- pulsion. (Laughter.) He suggested that instead of paying out-relief in money it should be in kind, and that there should be a central distributing warehouse with one large buying authority, under expert manage- ment. We could do with more of the sympathy of women on Boards of Guardians. (Hear, hear). The debate was adjourned on the proposition of Mr R. DONE (Newport, C.)

FOR PEACE.

The Christian Churcbes of Germavy and the United Kingdom held an interchange of visits in 1908 and 1909. The resolutions passed in London and Berlin are as follows:

===== 0002601_19091113_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
_l. RESOLUTION OF LONDON, IST JUNE, 1908.

"We as representatives of the. Christian Churches of Germany and of the United Kingdum, recognising bow greatly the world's peace depends upon the amicable relations between our two countries, appeal to all classes in both nations to promote, by their earnest endeavours, a mutual spirit of goodwill and friendship. Our nations are closely allied by tbe stock from which both peoples spring, by the kinship of our Soureigns, by our history, our long friendship, our wmutual indebtedness in Art Giterature and science, and above all by our common Christianity. We believe that the consciousness of these great traditions is deeply engraved in the hearts of our peoples, and that they endorse our conviction, that frank co-operation between us will do much to promote the coming of the Kingdom of Peace on earth and goodwill amour men." RESOLUTION OF BERLIN, 15TH, JUNE, 1909. _ . ,

"This meeting confirms wits great satisfaction 'the resolution of the Ist June, 1908, in the following words :—` We are one in, the earnest desire to do, every man of us, nll that in our power lies to strengthen more and more the Bond of Peace between our peoples. We are one in the sincere request to all our com- patriots to aid us in our endeavour, £0 that the, old voice of blood relationship may not call in vain, the old and the new voices of mighty spiritual influences and historical traditions may gain authority, and the eternal voice of the Gospel of Love may prove its sovereign power. Finally, we are one in the earnest prayer to the God of Peace that He will richly bless our work for His Name's sake and the coming of His Kingdom." Local Churches and peace organisations are asked to consider and pass the following resolution :---" Having considered the resolutions passed by the representatives of the Christian Churches assembled together in London on the Ist June, 1908, and in Berlin on the 15th June, 1909, we approve and endorse the expressions of good- will and friendship contained therein, and express our own earnest desire that the bond of peace between the two peoples may be preserved and strengthened."

===== 0002601_19091113_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
"BRITONS WILL BE SLAVES."

Dr. Clifford and the Veto of the

Lords.

Dr. Clifford has Addressed the following to the Press Association :

"The rejection of the London Elections Bill by the Lords is another indication that Britons will be slaves' as long as the veto of the Loids is allowed to exist. Therefore, tbe immediate duty of the friends of freedom and of progress is to abolish that veto. That is the alpha and omega of our freedom. That is the key to our national well-being. Members of the House of Commons are now giving—as one of them told me yesterday—ten or twelve nights to their con- stituencies. The duty to be urged with insistent enthusiasm is not merely the claims of the Budget; they are generally understood, and widely admitted; but the task to be undertaken is to prepare the con- stituencies for crippling the House of Lords by depriving it of its veto. Allow that House one veto against a measure sent up by the Commons ; but not a second. The Budget will pass if not at once, yet speedily. The next election should turn, not on that simply,but on the abolition of the veto.lnose who have taken part in London elections have seen the intoler- able wrongs inflicted on our fellow-citizens by the present arrangements. A man loses his vote by crossing Edgware-road from Paddington into Marylebone, or the other way about. He may be within fifty yards of his former abode, but his franchise is destroyed. Scotsmen and Manchester men compare Glasgow and Manchester with London, and ask why this gross injustice Is permitted to continue. Thousands of men are robbed of their voting power every year. Our Imperial masteas, the peers who bate freedom, know it, rejoice in it, and continue the robbery; and then they wonder why men detest them and wish their lordly House could be brought to the ground. The fact is they are determined to keep London dumb. Its acres are in their despotic grip. Its increasing wealth has filled their bursting coffers. They know they have not earned it. They are not quite sure they have a right to it, and they are afraid tl—it if London is permitted to speak at the polls it may demand a snrill fraction of their immense gains to pay the cost of the protection and defence of their property; and therefore they have slain this measure.

"But the London Elections Bill is only one of their victims. Where is the Plural Voting Bill? Slain by the Lords. What became of the Licensing Bill? Slain by the Lords. Where is the Education Slain by Lords. Who destroyed the Irish Land Bill? Again it is the Lords. Who stand in the way of the nation's advance •in sobriety, in justice, in freedom, and in social reform? The Lords. And the murderous weapon

===== 0002601_19091113_art0079.txt | included=true | included =====
TENNYSON AND RELIGIOUS THOUGHT.

"The relation of Tennyson to the thought of his day" was the subject of the fourth of the series of University Extension Lectures delivered in the Assembly Room at the Conservative Club on Wednes- day evening by the Rev. W. L. Schroeder.. The lecturer devoted a large part of his discourse to Tennyson's religious views. He said that although Tennyson was bold in speculation at times, he was always reverent. He was not powerful in his handling of fundamental problems, but he always had regard for decency.. He told us to "beware of breaking up the soil of faith when you have no better seed to sow." The merely destructive aspect of the materialistic philosophy of the- 19th century appalled him. In an interesting comparison of Carlyle and Tennyson, the lecturer said 'Carlyle with his strenuous intellectnal language seemed at times the very antithesis of Tennyson. Carlyle with his dogmatic utterances, Tennyson with his mild honest doubt—he did not know that the two men could be compared, at least not ,to the dis- advantage of Carlyle. We felt at once with Carlyle that we were with a man who had a tremendous mind, great intellectual .power, keen insight into the heart of things, with the genius and artistic ability to make his knowledge live for us: Tennyson made us think attimes, because his poetry was full of suggestion, but he did not always convince us that he himself was a strong thinker, handling problems in a definite, straight and fundamental manner. Tennyson was not a philosopher primarily ; he was- not a system maker. He was priMarily a poet— a thoughtful poet.. He was in the strict sense a broad Churchman—a man with deep, liberal syntpathies with many phases of theology, a Man who. did not care for the kind of dogmatic . handling of \religioa which brought religion , into such disrepute. For .that aspect of his life and thought. many were pro- foundly thankful. • He (the lecturer) thought he was safe in saying that Tennyson was not a high Anglican

—that he-wa-s not a Catlolic in the mode-rn sense of the term but a Catholic in the truer and more general sense. There was always in Tennyson a strong hold of certain fundamental ideas but he held these rather by faith and not by virtue of intellectual strength. He believed that belief in the self conscious personality of God was necessary to the welfare of men and women. A mere rationalist in religion was a man some- what abhorrent to his mind and soul. Tennyson'a power was not so much in the realm of thought as in the realm of religious experience and the emotional soul. Against the doubts of materialism he could rise up and say "I feel that God is, and that is enough for me." He once said that the English view of God was as of an immeasurable clergyman—a kind of glorified parson—and also that some mistook the Devil for God. His creed he would not formulate. He ivould not state definitely at any time what he deeply and fundamentally believed, but at any one moment he would allow an emotional expression to serve for his poetry and consequently wO often found ourselves bewildered when we tried to understand what his idea of God and the relation of God and man was. To him free will was the one great fact upon which men might go; he regarded it as an act of self-lim;tation by the Infinite and yet a revela- tion of Himself. When we came to summarise Tennyson's relationship to the thought of his day, we found that his best work lay in the realm of the scientific imagination, that he embodied in his poetry the general conclusions of science. Right throughout his work he had the strong ethical appeal. He brought to bear on the problems of the day a strictly poetic and artistic mind. It was no great discredit that he failed to embody in his poetry a complete solution of the philosophic problems that had disturbed the world ever since time was. The only thing of which we complained was that he thought' it necessary to embody his mere mood, or to give his temporary fancy as if it were of real value, and he took no great. care to bring his later work into any kind of con- sistent, philosophic,poetic whole.

THE EXAMINER 'Series OF WEEK-END NEWSPAPERS COMPRISES:-

THE WARRINGTON EXAMINER published every Saturday morning. The principal and the oldest weekly paper for War- rington, Stockton Heath, Penketh, Winwick, Padgate Lymm, &c., being one of the largest and best weekly papers in Lancashire and Cheshire. The district covered by this paper comprises a large trading- and agricultural tract in South Lancashire and Mid- Cheshire.

TEM RUNCORN EXAMINER, published every Saturday morning. It is the best weekly paper br Town and district, and offers the best and cheapest facilities to advertisers. - - -

THE WIDNES EXAMINER, published every Saturday morning. Widnes contains the largest number of chemical works of any town in the Kingdom, and is a rapidly growing centre of industry. THE EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON EXAMINER, published every Saturday morning. The largest local paper containing the news of Earlestown, Newton-le-Willows; Golbome, Haydock, Lowton. The district thus comprised is a most important part of the Lancashire coalfields. THE ST. HELENS EXAMINER, published every Saturday morning, circulating in St. Helens, Prescot, Parr, Sutton, Peasley Cross, &c.—the centre of the glass, chemical and watch trades of the Kingdom.

NATIONAL CHILDRENS HOME AND ORPHANAGE.

Entertainment at Padgate. In the Padgate Wesleyan Schoolroom on ThursdAY night a most enjoyable entertainment was held in aid of the above institution, arranged by the Padgate Branch of the Young Leaguers' Union. The programme took the form of a cinematograph exhibition of living pictures and was in the capable hands of Mr. William Parkinson of Warrington. Two action songs were effectively rendered by a number of young Leaguers', which had been admirably trained by Miss Clive Cladding. Mrs. James Bennett gave a brief survey of the excellent work being done by this progressive institu-7 tion, and during her remarks hoped that every encouragement would be given to the local workers. Limelight views of the various " Homes " in different. parts of Englant, were thrown on the sheet. A striking feature of the evening's programme wail the animated pictures depicting scenes of farm life 11 Canada, including ploughing by steam power, reaping and threshing on such a large scale, being almost inconceivable to the average English man's mind. The views portraying the Atlantic to Pacific Express crossing the Canadian Rockies were awe-inspiring. The train rushes along the deep momi- tain gorges, through vast tunnels, and round precipi- tous curves, the whole being illustrative of the marvellous strides made in railway construction. These films were obtained from the Canadian Pacific Railway Company, through the instrumentality of Mr. Leonarci Bennett, of King-street, Warrington, the local agent. Subjects of a humorous character were also greatlY appreciated. The attendance was highly satisfactory, the room being crowded, and the result was most encouraging.

The Proposed Abolition of School Fees. The Rev. W. Bracecamp writes as follows in St. Paul's Parish Magazine: The Vicar would like to draw the attention of the readers of our magazine t° the movement which has been started for the abolitico of school pence in our day schools. We are quit!: sure of this, that the majority of the parents who see° their children to our schools prefer to pay a little towards their education, rather than have anythieg on the cheap. If the school pence are abolished it spells ruin to a great many of our Church schools. At present three-fourths of them go to relieve the rates, and one-fourth is given to the managers t° help to keep the schools in proper repair. If they are given -up it means that Chutchpeople will have to pay more in rates, but will have to give extra if they wish (and we feel sure they do) to retain the schools in their possession as they are at the present moment. It is incomprehensible that Churchpeople should even suggest such a scheme, and we hope when the proper, time comes every Churchman will demand justice an° not be carried away with the cry, Free schools; free books; free breakfasts, and free everything. Where does independence come in ?" " PEVERIL " FIRE GRATES Still Unsurpassed, Inspection invited.—J. S. GARLAND, Kitchen Range and Stove Warehouse, 51 to 55, Bridge st., Warring- ton.—Advt.

If " what everybody is THE NEW ASPECT saying must be true," OF THE there can no longer be STRUGGLE.; any doubt about the

fate of the Finance Bill. The Lords will reject it, not because the sluices which produce an inrush of "the wild men" will be opened, but because a motion for rejection will be made by the MARQUIS of LANSDOWNE, the Tory leader. That, we must admit, is a very strong,and, so far as we are concerned,an unexpected step. It has been most difficult to make oneself believe that even the House of Lords, the least reasonable Second Chamber in existAnee so far as we know ,could make such a plunge as this otherwise than as a herd of buffaloes goes over a preci- pice—that is, just because those who are behind, and know nothing of the danger, push on those who are in front. However, what now appears is that the Budget is not to be thrown out by a stampede, but by an orderly movement under the direction of the head of the herd. This is much the most satisfactory arrangement so far as the Liberal party and Liberal principles are concerned. The semi- official peers will not be able to shelter them- selves behind the representation that they were overcome by the untrained bands of the Peerage.

It is said that there is to be a declaration that the issues at stake are so serious that the Lords consider it inconsistent with their duty to pass the Finance Bill before the country has been consulted on the proposals of that measure. That is an imposture, of course. The Bill will not be thrown out on any such grOund, but because the landowners object to the land taxes and because the liquor interests, which have very powerful representatives in the House of Lords, are prepared to spend half a million of pounds stirling in opposition to the party which proposes to make "the Trade" pay a more reasonable proportion of the national taxation. Excluding the land taxes and the' licensing duties, the Budget, as it actually

stands, would be brought in by a Conserva- tive Government if a change of Administra- tion could be-effected to-morrow. So much

is admitted by Mr. BALFOUR. Men like Lord JOICEY have .ratted too soon. They are appalled by the super-tax and the revised death-duties. They should have waited to hear the leader of the Conservative party in

the House of Commons declare.that he does not object to the proposed income tax or the super-tax, or the revised death-duties. To act with so much haste is not merely to -throw a protest away; it is to make the protest ridiculous.

The Finance Bill will be throvk out on preposterous grounds, and the appeal to the country, as is already abundantly evident, will be made on false pretences. Tory 'candidates are to "talk unemployment." -There is at present less unemployment than -there was when the Conservatives were in power. But it has never been so well advertised. Capitalists are often accused of exploiting the working man. Some of them are at pres6nt 'exploiting him as an unemployed person. Apart from the great game of politics, they would be much more profitably engaged if -they were finding work for the unemployed. But if unemplojment is to be made a leading factor in the forthcoming elections, it will probably not be forgotten that the Tory Government passed an Unemployment Bill which had no funds behind it,and that one of the first tasks of the present Administra- tion was to give that measure some sort of reality and some power of usefulness. Another election cry is to be: "You must make the foreigner pay." As nobody has .ever devised any means by which the -foreigner can be made to pay, either in this country or any other, the electors should °surely ask for proofs and illustrations of this :silly dogma. We put the case simply and 'shortly. Is it on such a mass egregious representations as this, on the small addi- tional taxation placed on the liquor trade, that the country will take so appalling a 'step as to declare that the Lords may not only control its finance, but decree the death of a Parliament?

Like Oliver Twist, of - -

Dickens fame, the Infirmary is always asking for more. Warringtonians will not be able to recollect the time when this institution was not appealing for funds, Fortunately there is no discredit attached to the ever-recurring appeal. The Infirmary is •our leading public charity, its work is unceasing and invaluable, and if .we do not, each and all of us, realise our responsibility for its adequate maintenance, We shall some day be compelled to shoulder the burden -through the rates. Owing to recent exten- sions, and the demands of a growing popula- tion, the Infirmary needs an increased revenue of £1.300 a year. Some valuable •suggestions were made at a Board meeting .0n Monday, and there is every prospect of immediate practical help from the Working- lnen's Committee, the Corporation, and the Board of Guardians. But the main effort is demanded of the public. and the individual. Zir. W. Peter Rylands, chairman of the

THE INFIRMARY.

Board, who has devoted a great deal of time and labour to the service of theF institution, emphasises the urgency of the appeal in another column, and we have great pleasure in doing what we can editorially to commend the scheme to our readers.

We congratulate THE MAYORALTY. Alderman Forshaw on his re-election to the office of Mayor for a second year. It has been the custom, for a considerable time past, to invite the Mayors of Warrington to serve a second year on completion of the first and we should not have liked to see an exception made in the case of Alderman Forshaw. The fact cannot be disguitsed that there has been friction in corporate circles on the ques- tion of the Mayoralty, but we feel convinced that the best interests of the town will be served by the peaceful procedure of Tuesday. The Mayor makes no claim to infallibility, and it is only reasonable and fair to conclude that those mistakes which he admits having made in the past were due, in the main, to an excess of zeal in the public service. His Worship, on the threshold of a new year,asks his fellow-members and towns- men to forget and forgive, and work with him in harmony for the good old town. We hope the appeal will not be disregarded. Little differences should be sunk, and every encouragement given to the occupier of the Mayoral chair to produce the best that is in him. There are many important and far-reaching schemes claiming the attention of the Council, and we hope that the ensuing twelve, months will prove to be not only a period of great progress, but one on which Mayor, Aldermen and Coun- cillors will all be able to look back with satisfaction.

The House of Lords DISFRANCHISEMENT have refused to pass a BY THE PEERS. measure to remove the disfranchisement, every year, of 40,000 London electors, and allow them to qualify by successive occupation as in Liverpool or Birmingham. It was expected; it is all part of a fatal policy which the Lords have adopted in recent years, so as to revise the Constitution that our old system of government by the representatives of the dominant political opinions in the country shall be destroyed, and instead of a two- party system, we are to be under the control of the Conservative Party whether they are in office or in opposition. It is no use for the electorate to return a majority to Parliament to remove the anomaly of denominational education quartered on the rates without public control; to introduce Temperance Reform or Housing Reform, or Small Holdings or Valuation in Scotland; or to abolish Plural Voting, or a .hundred other things which the electorate may desire. No use, that is, so long as the House of Lords possesses its present unrestricted right of veto. The Conservatives may be in the most insignificant minority in the House of Commons. They can still "thank heaven that there is a House of Lords." and snap their fingers at the demands of the people.

If we take a summary How THE BUDGET review of the results of DEBATES HAVE the Budget, we shall WOBICED OUT. then get something like this :—(1) The Leader of the Opposition has abandoned the claim that the Budget unfairly taxes capital and property as a whole. (2) He thus entirely casts away the suggestion that the Budget is 'Socialism.' (3) His sole objection to the principles of the Budget rests on the ground that it proposes to remedy the unfaiimess of two monopolies—land and liquor—paying far less than their share of taxation, and of vacant urban land escaping altogether. "No," says Mr. Balfour, "you must not turn to special forms of property which are under-taxed; you must clap on taxation te property all round." (4) He does not pretend that Tariff Reform would raise all the necessary revenue. And that (5) he has virtually given an assurance that rather than fail to meet national obligations, he might even adopt Mr. Lloyd-George's taxes.
