..,-.---, NOTES AND NOTIONS.

The Knutsford District Liberal Associa- tion are holding meetings every night in the constituency until the whole of the division is covered. "If work will do it," says Mr. Will Ford, the Liberal agent, "this seat will be retained." One of these meetings will be held at Stockton Heath, on Monday evening, the principal speakers announced being Mr. A. J. King, M.P., and Mr. A. A. Haworth, M.P. * * *

One of Warrington's standing grievances is the poisoning of the atmosphere in the Longford district by the Sanitary Depot, and we cannot see that it will' be consummate wisdom to plant a new elernentary school in the neighbourhood. A correspondent says that on Sunday morning the perfume was enough to kill Old Harry." What about young Harry if the Educational authorities condemn him to breathe these poisonous gasse for the best part of each day? * *

Congratulations to Sir W. J. Crossley Bart. M.P., who was among the recipients of the Royal birthday ,honours this week. The member for the Altrincham Division well-deserved the, signal recognition of his many great services to suffering humanity, the cause of temperance, and commercial industry 4 His elevation to a baronetcy was the source of much gratification throughout ,the constituency, and particularly in Lymm were Lady Crossley received a right royal welcome at a meeting of women Liberals on Monday evening. * * *

Schemes fraught with great possibilities for the future of Warrington will have to be undertaken in the near future by the Town Council, and with this object in view a Bill is to be promoted in Parliament. Some of the projects which it will probably be sought to include in the measure were foreshadowed at the Council meeting on Tuesday. Foremost, of course, comes the new Warrington Bridge and other questions in respect of which powers may be applied for are the develop- ment of Victoria Park and the completion of the widening of the main streets. The General Purposes Committee will meet on Tuesday next to decide what subjects shall be included in the Bill. * 4, *

The editor of the "Holy Trinity Magazine" has been letting himself go in the sort of political tirade which brings clergymen into disrepute. Before criticising temperance reform in the carping spirit of which he is guilty in the extract quoted in our corres- pondence column, this gentleman had better prove himself a qualified critic. The temperance cause owes little in Warrington to his help, and temperance workers have not-forgotten how he and his brethren of the cloth were found wanting when such leaders of their own church as the Bishop of Liver- pool and Canon Hicks stood up manfully for the Licensing Bill. Local clergy underwent a humiliation on that occasion which they

had far better refrain from recalling to the public recollection. * * * There is a little unconscious irony in the letter on page 12 from the Warrington Women's Suffrage Society deprecating the use of violence in political propaganda, for after condemning the acid- throwing,iwindow- smashing suffragettes the suffragists "pro- test most earnestly against the manner in which the suffrage agitation has been handled by the responsible Government." It is all a question of how far earnestness of protest carries one. . .

* * * Local churches, chapels and peace organisa- tions are invited to pass a resolution endorsing the views expressed by the Aritish and German representatives of the Christian churches who exchanged visits in 1908 and 1909 for the purpose of discussing the further- ance of international peace. The text of the resolutions passed in Berlin and London will be found on another page. It seems to us that there is much more to be said for this method of promoting peace than in the acceptance of the false doctrine that the best security for peace is to be prepared for war. We want a nation of ambassadors, thinkers, and reformers and not a nation in arms. * * *

Sir Robertson Nicoll, as we must now call him, has an admitted claim to general gratitude in his discovery of Mr. J. M. Barrie. Mr. Barrie had been writing for a good many years as a journalist before Dr. Nicoll was struck by the quality of an anonymous article; and, getting into com- munication with the writer, gave him the opportunity of writing a weekly signed article under a pen-name. .In due course -he published serially the earlier of the books which made Mr. Barrie famous. Essayist, biographer, journalist, literary critic, and all-round man of letters, Dr. Robertson Nicoll is the keeper of the literary conscience of Nonconformity. As a politician he wields his pen as vigorously and as effectively as he does upon the vexed aspects of religion, and a great section of Nonconformity look to him for guidance. Dr. Robertson Nicoll is, in short, the soundest exponent of Noncon- formist thought in the literary and political fields.

October was the best mqnth of the year in the total of fovign trade, although the exports are rather less than they were in July. Moreover, the character of the trade done is satisfactory to us as a manufacturing nation, for practically the whole improve- ment in exports is in manufactured goods, while the main increase in imports is in raw materials. The comparison is: Imports. Exerts. Food drink and

tobacco Raw materials Manufactures Here we see working out under Free Trade precisely the conditions which, we are assured, Tariff Reform would bring about. On the ten months we are well ahead of the similar period of 1908 as regards imports, but are some ' seven and a half millions behind in the matter of exports. We can scarcely hope (says the Westminster Gazette ") to make that deficiency up in two months, but the quantity of raw materials now coming in gives promise of great manu- facturing activity next year. ** * .

That extremely intelligent and practical 'organ of the leather trade, the "Shoe and Leather Record," has some useful things to say about the calculated pessimism which Tariff Reformers apply to one industry after another for electioneering purposes. The organ of the trade denies indignantly that the leather trade of the country is dying, and it justifies the indignation by the following definite statements: •

"Speaking broadly," says the "Shoe and Leather Record," "English sole, harness, and belting leather is still the best in the world, and home production is increasing largely. it is exported all over the world, both as leather and in the form of boots and shoes, saddlery and harness and machine belting. And this in spite of the heavy tariffs imposed against it, both by our own Colonies and foreign Governments. Surely a trade in this excellent position ought not t 3 he subjected to the slanderous attacks of politicians anxious only for votes in a constitu- ency where the industry happens to have changed its character through local conditions with which foreign competition has nothing whatever to do." Now that Mr. Dumphreys sits for Ber- inondsey, those who trotted the "ruined industry" bogey round the constituency will probably ignore the refutation.

* * * The Chief Conservative Whip (Sir Alexander Acland-Hood, M.P.) addrsssed his constituents at Williton on Octobet 27th. The following passage appears in the report of the meeting in the "West Somerset Free }less" of October 30th : "Mn. S. CHIDGEY : I should like to ask if the • present price of bread is owing to the Government. • Since the Government has been in the price has gone up. Is that owing to the Government ? " "SIR ALEXANDER HOOD: All I can say is— when I went out of office bread was 4W.; now it is 6d. I don't know why it has gone up." Question—ls a hot 'and impromptu " lie " any better than the "frigid and calculated' variety? And will Mr. Balfour, our new Political Censor denounce his Chief Whip's calculated ignorance, obviously designed to fasten upon the Government responsibility for the increased price of,bread ?

In view of the prospective rejection by the House. of Lords of the Finance Bill we should like to put a few questions for the considera- tion of the Conservative party. The first question we will ask is : Is it true that Mr. Arthur Balfour, who is now such a Dassionate advocate of Old-Age Pensions, himself abstained from voting on the second and third reading of the Old-Age Pensions Act? The second is: Is it true that the Unionist rank and file, who now talk as if they had passed Old-Age Pensions themselves in the face of Radical hostility, voted thus on the third reading (July 9th, 1908) : Against . 11 For the bill Abstained

The third is : Is it true that the House of Lords, now the chief interpreter of the mind of the Uhionist Party, carried by 77 to 45 an amendment, moved by Lord Cromer, limiting the Old-age Pensions Bill to seven years, so that if the House of Lords had had their way the bill would have expired on December 31st, 1915? Is it true that that amendment was only not supported by the Tories in the Commons because it was ruled out by the Speaker ,on the point of privilege? Lastly, is it true that Lord St. Aldwyn, one of the oldest props of the Tory Party, moved and carried in the House of Lords an amendment that would, if it had been accepted, have condemned,—by omitting the time-limit to the disqualification—all the poor old people who have ever accepted poor relief to perpetual exclusion from the pen- sioner?

It will be difficult for our friends the enemy to say "No !' to any of those ques- tions, for all of them are• drawn from the official records of the two Houses. Would it not, therefore, be better for them to stop shouting " Lies !" and to observe a decent

•

attitude of repentance on the Old-Age Pension question? _

Tea and Concert at Legh-street.

The quarterly tea and concert in connection with Legb-street Primitive Methodist Church was held on Thursday evening, and proved a great success in every way. The arrangements were on this occasion in the hands of the married ladiestof the church. A good number were present for tea, there being two sittings, Ito which the tables were removed and the concert proceeded with; the performers being all married ladies. The programme consisted largely of humorous items. Mr. J. Griffiths took the chair. The choruses "Johnny Sands" and " Good-night " were well ren- dered by all the ladies. The recitation "The model church" was given by Mrs. Mother (the oldest mem- ber of the church). At a certain point in this recitation the singing in the church was initiated in a realistic manner by singers in an adjoining room. Mrs. Green, a new member of the church, was highly successful in her songs "Abide with me" "The beautiful dream" and "Dear old home songs," for the latter of which she was encored. Mrs. Mills gave an aniusing recitation entitled "My old bonnett." Mrs. Cowap rendered the pathetic recitation Laddie " with 'feeling. The Lancashire sketches " Owd Billy Fates ke " and "Dick's courtship" rendered by Mrs. J. Nurse, provoked much merriment. The dialogue " Women's Rights " which was highly topical, proved very amusing and was well done in spite of the fact that two of the ladies had to imperson- ate males, one being a gouty old man and the other a policeman. The final item was a dialogue" Washing done here" and caused outbursts of laughter. In addition to the names already mentioned the following ladies took part: -Mesdames Atkinson, Ashworth, Bennett, Cook, Dutton, Dodd, Elwell, Homer, Landor, Parkes, Swales and Spruce. Miss Annie Titchett proved an able accompanist. A vote of thanks pro- posed hy Rev. 3. B. Buglass, and seconded by Mr. J- Gliffiths concluded an enjoyable evening.

CHILDREN'S PARTY AT THE TOWN HALL.

A hundred merry children thronged the Town Hall on Thursday evening and a right royal time they had, for it was the occasion of the party given by the Mayor and Mayoress (Alderman and Mrs. Forshaw), to the boys and girls who were fortunate enough to receive medals and brooches on Empire Day and to the children of members of the Corporation. The function was inaugurated by Alderman Smethurst during his mayoralty and proved such a complete success that its annual repetition seems to be asEured. It will be recalled that the medals and brooches were presented by the Mayor on Empire Day to the scholars whose good conduct, according to the votes of their fellow-pupils, entitled them to the honour. For Thurs- day's party the entrance hall, Council Chamber and. committee room, had been most tastefully decorated with plants and flowers by Mr. R. Whitfield, the head gardner, and presented a bright and inviting appear- ance. The youthful guests were received by the Mayor and Mayoress, who were accompanied by Mr. W. B. Forshaw and the Misses Forshaw, and after they had been cordially welcomed games and dancing were engaged in with great zest. Mr. A. Starkey's band provided the music. An interesting feature was a conjuring entertainment by Mr. W. A. Lloyd. Refreshments were served, the catering being in the hands of Mr. and Mrs. Powis. Amongst those who- assisted the Mayor and Mayoress and family in making the evening a thoroughly enjoyable one for the children were: Mr. and Mrs. J. H. Smethurst, Mr. and Mrs. J. Moore Murray, Mr. and Mrs. R. B. Roberts, Mr. and Mrs. T. H. Spinks, Miss Haddock, Miss Henshall, Miss Lyon Whittle, Misses Roberts (Harleydene), Miss Burton, the Misses Smethurst, Misses Houghton, Miss Sankey (Church-street), Miss Sankey (Croft) and Miss Mehrten (a visitor from Australia). During the evening several members of the Council looked in, including Aldermen Smethurst and Burton, and Councillors Wilkinson, Singleton and Wilson.

The Mayoress was presented with a. bouquet of chrysanthemums by Miss Jane Timmins, one of the scholars of the Blue Coat school.

Before the guests separated, Master Tinniou pro- posed a vote of thanks to the Mayor and Mayoress for their kindness in giving the party, which all had enjoyed so much. The vote was passed with acclammation and the Mayor in acknowledging the compliment, said it was sufficient recompense to the Mayoress and himself if the children had enjoyed themselves, and judging by the way they had entered into the games they must have done so. He hoped next year he would be able to give a similar party. (Applause). Cheers were given for the Mayor and Mayoress and all the helpers, anti a happy gathering concluded.

ROLLER SKATING.

THE EMPIRE RINK

This has been another week of special attractions at the above new and handsomely appointed rink. There was a crowded attendance on Tuesday evening when the final for the mile championship of Warrington was run. The four heat winners—all members of the Empire Hockey Club—were C. Baxter, J. Bowcock, W. Baxter, and Wadsworth. In the final W. Baxter gainad the lead in the second lap and main- tained it to the finish,winning an excellent race in the time of 3min. 43 2-ssec. C. Baxter had a fall in the second round and this enabled Wadsworth to come in Second. The winner received a magnificent gold medal, while the second man in was awarded a gold centre medal. The Empire Hockey Club have joined the Manchester and District League, which comprises many of the leading roller skating hockey clubs, and there is a prospect of some first-class games at the Empire in the near future The promise of the presence of Crosfields's Band and Mister Norman Kiddie, of Widnes, at the Empire Skating Rink on Thursday was a great attraction, as there were 400 skaters and about 600 spectators' Amongst the selections by the band was "The death of Nelson," which was very well received. Master Kiddie gave a fine exhibition and was presented with a gold medal with the Warrington Coat of Arms emblazened thereon and inscribed " Presented by the Directors of the Empire Skating Rink for exhibitions in graceful and fancy skating on the opening of the Empire Rink, October 12th, 1939." Alderman Smethurst made the presentation, and the winners of the Warrington and District Amateur Mile Championship (Mr, W. Baker and Mr. Wadsworth) were the recipients of a gold and gold centred medal respectively. There will be another special attraction next Thurs- day when balloon: and hoop races will be held.

PAL.A.TINE RINK.

A. large .number of people attended the Palatine Rink on Thursday and were the spectators of a very exciting hockey match between Warrington Palatine and St. Helens, both of • whom up to Thursday had not been defeated. The teams were at full strength and the game was very fast. Roth teams showed considerable combination and although War- rington lacked the weight of their opponents they made up for it in dash and vigour.The home custodian H. Whittle proved a pillar of strength and stopped. some most difficult shots. Whittaker secured a goal for Palatine during the first half. Half time score: Warrington Palatine, one . goal; St. Helens, nil. Resuming, both teams settled down in determined fashion to win and the ball travelled rapidly over the floor. At this period the excitement was intense and Beard scored for St. Helens, making the teams level. However, a short time after Davies secured another goal for the home team and the result was : —War- rington Palatine, 2 goals; St. Helens, 1 goal.

IS THE BUDGET SOCIALISM? Councillor Hawthorn Addresses the I.L.P.

"Is the Budget Socialism ?" was the subject of an interesting discussion at the I.L.P. Debating Class on Monday evening in the I.LP. rooms. Mr. E. Horrocks presided over a good attendance. - Councillor HAWTHORN opened the discussion by invitation, his contention in the course of an exhaustive resum4 of the Budget's proposals being that the Budget was not Socialism. He said that, tried by every test. there is nothing to prove that the Budget is a step in Socialism at all. It is not based on Socialistic principles. That is easily ascertained by asking a few questions from the Socialist's standpoint. Does the proposal to tax land values involve State ownership or control ,of land, capital or labour ?—No. Does it restrict competition ?—No. On the contrary, by making land cheaper and more accessible it will muitiply competitive opportunities. Does it interfere with freedom of contract?—Certainly not. With free or equal access to the storehouse of all wealth, bargaining, wnether between seller and buyer or between employer and workman, will be placed for the first time on a free basis. Lastly, does it neces- sitate the fixing of an artificial standard of values ?—No, again. The prices of commodities will still be regulated by the market, and the work- man's wage will be not that which a wages board may arbitrarily determine to be a living wage, but as a minimum' that which he can earn by working for himself. In conclusion, Councillor Hawthorn said: It is not so muCh the Budget itself but the vast potentialities which lie behind it in helping to solve some of the social problems of the day that appeal to every man and %omen of progressive thought. The Budget lights up many of the social evils of our generation and shows the path by which escape is possible. I believe it is a cause worth fighting for and there is a call for men who will be as brave and bold as the Cromwellian Ironsides. The opposition aroused by this modest instalment of reform is a foretaste of what will happen 'it hen the more drastic proposals of the Socialists are put forward for adoption. The forces are already gathering in battle array; the trumpet call will shortly be sounded, and it will rest with all lovers of progress to say whether they will grasp the occasion to improve our social conditions or fall back defeated into the social morass. Mr. R. PIERCE WILLIAMS maintained that the Budget was not comprehensive enough in its concep- tion of the regulation of social and industrial func- tions to be termed Socialism. It did not vitally effect the cause of our social disorder. Instead of funda- mentally dealing with our competitive system itself it sought only to mitigate some of its evils. On the other band, as it taxed socially created wealth for social purposes, it was Socialistic. Messrs. H. Wardman, Whiteley, Highley, Howliston, Spencer, Murby, Watson, Wilkinson, and Handles also took part in the discussion. A vote of thanks moved by Mr. W. 11113R13Y and seconde by Mr. W. WATSON, was accorded to Councilior Hawthorn.

WARRINGTON MALE VOICE CHOIR. Annual Concert.

The Warrington Male Voice Choir ia entitled to the gratitude of local music-lovers for arranging such a rich treat as was presented for their delectstion at the ninth annual concert held in the Parr Hall on Thurs- day evening. It is encouraging to find that the choir's consistent pursuit of high artistic ideals is evoking a ready response on the part of the t evnsoeople. With the exception of a few of the higher-priced seats the hall was filled in every part with an audience which was discriminating as well as warmly appreciative. That the choir% having a good educational influence cannot be gainsaid. The success of a concert entirely devoted to high-class music, and free from the inter- vention of the one-time indispensable humorist,is suffi- cient proof of that. The competitive movement,wholly commendable as a means to an end, is not allowed to absorb all the choir's interest and energies but musical culture for its own sake is the ultimate aim. Under the careful training of Mr. w. S. Nesbitt, the talented conductor of the famous Manchester Orpheus Choir, much progress has been made and Warrington has every reason to be proud of a choir which is capable of doing justice to intricate w irks by the great tnasters of song The compositions selected for Thursday's concert gave the choir plenty of scope to demonstrate the range of ite capabilities and whether in stirring martial pieces, which figured prominently in the programme, or in a quiet, ear haunting work like German's " 0 peaceful night," the members showed intelligent grasp of the composer's intention and sang with marked expression as well as accuracy. As was the case last year the m ,st ambitious item was Elgar's "The Reveille "—an intensely dramatic and powerful setting of Bret Harte's poem. Sir E. Elgar has certainly treated his subject on bold and unconventional lines but the effect is to present a thrilling and even wierd renregentation of the teamp of armed thousands and the insistent call of the drum. The inspiration of genius is unmistakably manifested in the work and, as vividly interpreted by the Warrington choir, it helil the audience spellbound. At the close there was a hearty encore which was responded to, with that whimsical and amusing p'ece " Franklyn's Daggs." Sullivan's "The Beleaguered," Walford Davies's 'Hymn before action," German's "0 peaceful night," and Schubert's "Fur beyond all Ken" were all given with refine- ment, the fine b ,dy of voices whicth Mr. Nesbitt has under his control, blending admirably. Mr. J. H. Foster is the choir's accompanist. The promoters of the concert showed commendable enterprise in securing the services of the famous Blackpool Glee and Madrigal Society, which has had the honour of singing before the Queen and other members of the Royal Family in London. Mr. Herbert Whittaker has brought his choir to a remark- able state of proficiency, and rarely have we seen an audience compelled to such rapt interest or moved to such delighted enthusiasm by choral singing as was the case on Thursday evening. The visitors' singiug was characterised by crispness bf attack and delicacy of expression ; in short they displayed a complete mastery of their highly diversified series of choruses and part songs, and no detail was overlooked. Tschaikowsky's imposing and devotional "Angels ever blessed," with its wealth of vocal colour, was interpreted in a dignified and inspiring manner. Elgar's beautiful " Weary wind of the west" aff rded a striking contrast in style, and the pianissimo passages were exquisitely done, the audience being so pleased theta repetition had to be conceded. Granville Bantock's brilliant and dramatic setting of William Blake's fameus lyric "The Tyger," was another notable item, in which the choir distinguished itself, and Bruch's joyous "Morning song of praise" was sung with such delightful freshness and fulness of tone that a recall was accorded and the choir responded with Mendelssohn'a "Dearture." The lady members of the society did themselves credit in three fine part songs "The Snow" (Elgar), "Summer wind" (McDowell) and "The Miller's Daughter" (Brahms), the rapturous and fanciful composition by McDowell being given with particularly pleasing effect. The visit of the Blackpool choir was a success in every respect and we hore it will not be the last time by any means that Warringtonians will be afforded the opportunity of bearing this splendidly trained body of vocalists. It is further to the credit of the Blackpool society that the vocal soloists at the concert —Miss Marion Bailey and Mr. William Walton—are both members of that organization: Miss Bailey is a mezzo-soprano with a sweet and flexible voice and her tasteful and sympathetic interpretations of "Ah ! my heart is weary (A. Goring Thomas), "All through the night," a 'traditional Welsh air, and " The little silver ring " (Chaminade), afforded great pleasure. Miss Bailey was encored and responded with "My treasure.' Mr. Walton's resonant bass voice was heard to dietinct advantage in four well-chosen item,' by Liza Lehmanns, MacDowell and M. V. White. He entered into the sdirit of the songs with the greatest zest and the stirring way in which he sang" King Charles" (White) gained a well-deserved encore. Miss Bailey and Mr. Walton were associated in a dainty, tuneful duet " Snowdrops " (Liza Lehmanns) which quite captivated the audience. Mr. James Richardson played violoncello solos by Heberlein end Lasserre, as well as a couple of his own compositions, with soulfulness and skill, his clever performance of Lasserre's "The spinning wheel," with its delicate touches, proving especially popular. Mr. A. Vivian Jackson, of Blackpool, accompanied all the soloists with excellent taste.

Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P.,-president of the Warring- ton choir, Mrs. Crosfield and other prominentitowns- people were present at the concert.

BITONETCY FOR MR. W. J.

CROSSLEY, M. P.

Much satisfaction was felt in, the Altrincham Division on 'Tuesday when it was learned that among the King's birthday honours was a baronetcy for Mr. W. J. Crossley, the member for the Division. Sir William is the .second son of the late Major Francis Crossley and was born in 1844, at Glenburn, Dunmurry, Ireland. He was articled as an engineer with Sir W. G. Armstrong and Co. Elswick, in 1862, and started business in partnership with his late brother, Mr. F. W. Crossley, of Manchester in 1867. The firm , took up gas engine manufacture in 1869 and were the' founders in England of what has now become a great trade. Mr. Crossley was a director of the Manchester Ship Canal Company from the beginning until his retirement in 1906, and is a freeman of Manchester. He was elected for the Altrincham division in 1906, and is a member of the Royal Commission on Canals and Waterways. He has been for twenty-five years chairman of the Manchester Hospital for Consumption, he built a great part of the Hospital for Consump- tion at Bowdon, and he also built the Crossley Sanatorium, Delamere. e has always taken a great interest in the welfare of boys. He founded twenty- two years ago the Openshaw Lads' Club, and is chair- man of the Boys' and Girls Refuges, Strangeways. He is president of the Y.M.C.A. Manchester, and treasurer of the United Kingdom Alliance.

SCOTTISH CONCERT AT THE

PARR HALL.

Local Scots were regaled with a feast of their national verse and melody at the Parr Hall on Monday evening, and to the mere Southerner also the occasion was not without interest. The CaledOnian Association is to be commended for arranging this pleasant annual re-union, and the fact that the proceeds are to he divided between the association's benevolent fund and the Warrington Infirmary should have ensured an even more generous measure of support than was accorded to Monday's concert. But while there was room for improvement. in this respect, a fairly laige audience attoned, to some extent for deficiency in numbers by the interest and appreciation with which it received the programme. EVery effort was made to conjure up that Scottish "atmosphere" so necevsary to the complete success of the affair. . On entering the hall we were greeted with the stirring strains of the pipes, played by a typical Scot in Highland cpstume. The decoration of the. platform was quite effective, the English and Scottish flags being crossed above a table which was draped with the Union Jack. Musically too there was much to inspire the Scotsman with memories of the homeland, but the strain of melancholy was too pronounced, and a -little more variety in the choice of items would have been acceptable. Of the artistes, chief interest centred in • the appearance of Mrs. Margaret Booth, the well-known local soprano, who has lately been studying in London, under the direction of eminent tutors, and has also figured with some success on the concert platform in Manchester as well as in the metropolis. She possesses a voice of exceptional power and sweetness and' her interpretations of several choice Scottish songs afforded much pleasure. Mrs.. Booth sang the pathetic " Flora Macdonald's lament" with feeling, and the quaint humour of "0 whistle and I'll come to you my lad" was also well realized. Miss Jennie Armstrong, who recently won laurels in local amateur operaTsang the ever-popular "Caller Herrin," with such delightful freshness as to call forth a hearty encore, to which she responded with "Ye banks and braes." Later she was heard to even better advantantage in that beautiful composition "My am n folk." Mr. J. Kerr Campbell makes artistic use of a tenor 'voice of excellent purity of tone, the tender sympathy which he infused into his rendering of "Mary o' Argyle " marking it as one of the most enjoyable items of the evening, and he. was enthusiastically recalled. Mr. G. Platt, the advertised baritone, was indisposed, and his place was taken by Mr. Harold Heslop who scored a success with the favourite song --indispensable at a Scottish gathet ing --" Annie Laurie." His interpretation of "The Trumpeter" was disappointing, for it lacked the necessary spirit and contrast. The voices of the four -vocalists blended nicely in the quartette" Scots wha .ha 6 with which the concert began. Mr. Theo. Crozier, a gifted young violinist who was so well received last year, made a welcome re-appearance and enhanced his reputation: Delicacy ard refinement of expression and brilliance of excution were the most prominent characteristics of his playing of selections by Wieniawski, McDowell and Schubert, and a fantasia of Scottish airs was given in a soulful and truly artistic style which roused the audience to .a high pitch of enthusiasm. Mr. Alex Chrystal is a

FAMOUS BIOLOGISTS DEATH.

The Wesleyan Church as well 'as the scientific world, has suffered a loss by the death of Dr. Dallinger, the famous biologist and microscopist, who passed away at his home at Lee, at the age of 68 years. The Rev. William Henry Dallinger, D.Sc., D.C.L., L.L.D., F.R.S. was barn in Devonport, and entered the Wesleyan ministry when 20 years of age. He was appointed to Faversham, Cardiff, Bristol, and Liverpool circuits successively. He stayed 12 years in Liverpool; then became Principal of Wesley College, Sheffield. But it was as a man of science that Dr.Dallinger earned distinction. In 1870 he began a series of microscopical researches on the life histories of minute septic organisms which extended over ten years, awl to him is due the refutation of the theory of the spontaneous generation of life from the remains of dead organisms, which was so confidently propounded by certain scientific men. In conjunction with his friend Dr. Drysdale, Dr. Dellinger arranged for the taking of continuous observations; one relieving the other until every point in the life history of the organisms viewed under a most powerful ruihroscope had been recorded. It was clearly shown that they were evolved from spores or germs, as in the case of higber forms, and these germs were so retentive of life that they would grow in a temperature of 300 deg. Fahr. for 10 minutes, while the adults perished at 140 deg. Fahr. Dr. asllinger's discovery became the talk of the scientific world, and the Royal Society voted him a grant of £lOO for further researches. He lectured on the subject before the Royal Institution of and the University of Oxford, and in 1881 he went,at the request of the British Associa- tion, to Canada to attend the meetings of the association in that colony and to explain the nature and results of his researches.

In 1880 Dr. Dallinger was elected a Fellow of the Royal Society, and other honours were showered upon him in rapid succession. Dr. Dallinger continued to hold his appointment in the Wesleyan Church nntil 1888, when he resigned to devote himself to scientific research, though he afterwards frequently occupieu the pulpit. Dr. Dallinger had a wonderful laboratory. fitted up with every conceivable appliance to ass,st him in his minute reseirches—one instrument enabling him to cut objects I,oooth to the 2,000 th of an inch in thickness.

WEIGHING THE MAYOR AND CORPORATION. The usual ceremonies were observed at High Wycombe on the 9th inst., when Alderman Walter Birch was elected Mayor of the ancient Borough for the third time since 1900. He was duly presented with the silver stick and minatnre mace. After the administration of the'usual oaths, the Mayor and Corpor ttion were duly weighed on a personal weighing machine, kindly lent by Messrs. W. and T. Avery, Ltd, of London and Birmingham, in place of the old fashioned coal weigh- ing machine hither to used. Miss Dove,who was last year rejected as Lady Mayoress, was also among the number to be weighed. It might be mentioned that the Mayor, who is head of a large Chair-making Company, scaled 15 stone Bibs,

BRUTAL ASSAULT AND LENIENT SENTENCE. At the Borough Police Court on Monday, Michael O'Brien,a farm labourer,employed at Winstanley's Farm Stretton, and Harry Maloney, also a farm labourer, of Stretton, were charged with assaulting George Cann, a deaf and dumb young man, in Wilderspool-road, on Saturddy night. They pleaded guilty.—The Wet Constable stated that on Saturday afternoon the prosecutor was passing along Wilderspool-road, when he W 8 13 stopped by one of the prisoners who made an enquiry. They asked him for something and then without any provocation O'Brien struck him with his clenched fist on the back of the bead. Maloney struck him from the back, and then O'Brien took off his belt., and deliberately struck him with it.—Sarah Cann, mother of the prosecutor, said that on arriving home on the night in question her, son complained to her. He said he had been set upon by two men. Witness saw be bad two lumps on the back of his head. The left side of his face was also swollen. He complained very much that day.—The Chief Constable said that the prisoners asked prosecutor for a match, and being dent and dumb, he got out a piece of paper and pencil and asked them to write down what they wanted. It was an unwarranted assault. P.C. Campbell was in the vicinity and hearing cries of" Murder" went on the scene but prisoners had decamped. With the valuable help of the Cheshire Constabulary, he traced them to Stretton and there arrested them.—Both prisoners had nothing to say.—O'Brien was fined 20s. and costs or a months' imprisonment for using the belt and Maloney was fined 10s. and costs or 14 days. The money was paid.

He described the begging as a regular nuisance. It had been ascertained that both girls had escaped from from a hospital in Manchester, and the shawls they had on then belonged to the hospital. One girl had no parents at all and it was very desirable in the interests of society that tbey should be taken back to the hospital. The bench agreed to this course. Daresbury Petty Sessions.

On Tuesday before Messrs. W. Long (in the chair), H. Congreve, H. C. Border, and T. Baxter. UNHAPPY MARRIED LIFE.—John Kirby,Chester-road Warrington, a coachman, was charged with having deserted his wife, Amy Mary Kirby, on November sth. Mr. Browne appeared for the complainant, and stated that the case arose out of one which was adjourned from the last Court held at Stockton Heath, in which complainant summoned defendant's two daughters, and a cross-sumtnons was issued by them. It was on his (Mr. Browne's) urgent application and with the entire concurrence of the Bench that the case was adjourned to see if the conduct of the husband would alter in the ' meantime. Nothing whatever had been done. Com- plainant was defendant's second wife, he having married her on January 2nd, 1907. Defendant had two daughters both of whom were schoolmistresses. Defendant having had to remove from Greenbank Cottage, after being discharged by Miss Massy, another house was taken in the name of defendant's daughter. Defendant told complainant his daughter had taken a house in Chester-road and if she did not go with him there she could go where she liked. Complainant told him she would not live with his two daughters, and thereupon defendant offered her four shillings per week, which was absurd, as he had been earning 255. per week and had a house free at Miss Massy's. On Friday last a van came to defendant's door and took away the furni- ture, including Miss Kirby's wedding presents, and left Mrs. Kirby alone with her personal belongings in Greenbank Cottage. Complainant slept at her mother's house that night, but returned the next day and finding that the new tenants of the house would arrive she bad to clear out. The case was adjourned from the last time to see if the daughters would leave or else there shonld be a deed of separation between husband and wife. Nothing had been done in the meantime, and complainant had been/ treated with contempt by defendant's daughters.— Complainant corroborated and said that when left alone in the house she was without food. She would agree to defendant paving her Bs, per week but no less.— Defendant said he could not afford to pay Bs. per week at the time, lie only earned 245. per week when with Miss Massy, and ever since he had married complainant there had been a disturbance in the -house. He had never had such disagreeable conduct before, and com- plainant had stated that "she would give him no peace so long as the daughters were there." She had got defendant into debt and he had bad to put a notice in the papers about it. She also took in sewing and could earn 14s. or 15s. per week by her trade. He could not' afford to pay Bs. per week. Two or three shillings was the most he could afford.—The Bench ordered defend- ant to contribute 6s. per week to his wife's mainten- ance.—Mr. Brow.ue asked for advocate's fee, which was allowed.

Buckley-Street P S A __ • . •

Mr. James Urrnston- presided at the monthly open meeting of the Buckley-street Society which was held on Sunday. Mr. B. Richardson (of Liverpool) gave an address on " The fruits of the thoughts." Mr. Harry Nurse, a promising baritone, gave two solos in credit- able style. Miss M. Plinston was the accompanist. Bold-street Men's Bible Class.

There was a good attendance on Snnday last when the Rev. R. E. Letheren gave an address on St. Mark, whose Gospel, he said, shovied us how all our failures could be pieced together and helped us in the future. He also spoke of the comfort to be derived from the reading of this portion of the Scripture. Messrs. Dean end Julian took part in the meeting.

Wesley Guild Rally. The members of Wesley Guilds in the Warrington Circuit look forward every year to a particular week end, during which a guild rally is held. Another rally is in the dim future if this year's rally took place on Sunday and Monday last. This time the rally was held in Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Church and the Rev. H. Mudie-Draper (London),was the special preacher and lecturer. The service were well attended on Sunday and on Monday evening the chapel was crowded. It was really a flue sight to see as many as eight guilds gathered in one building under the chairmanship of Mr. .1. W. Hayward, J.P. Mr. W. E. Plinston (secretary of the guilds) submitted a report for the year which was a very satisfactory one, the total membership being 592. The secretary of each guild submitted a report which spoke well for the future of the respective guilds. The Rev. H. Mudie-Draper in a somewhat lengthy address which was illuminated with flashes of wit and humour, said that there had been no movement in the Methodist Church so far as he could gather than the career of the Wesley Guild, which had made such rapid strides during the 13 years of its existence. He com- pared Guild Rally's to marriage, which signified that they met together for mutual help, comfort and bless- ing. The greater part of his address was upon the need for enthusiasm and earnestness in Christian service. He said that the most powerful foe against tbem was the weakening of spiritual obligations. He was afraid that they had lost to some extent tbat kern) sense of the spiritual which distinguished their forefathers. Instead of going to the devotional meeting some went ricking. His advice to the young folks was to be care- ful in that particular line. Here he instanced a case in a certain town in which respectable young women had pawned their clothes in order to go rinking, They should not carry their , pleasure to an unholy extent. He did not mean that they had only just got to think about the other world. He did not think that there was any danger of that at this particular time, for he was rather atraid the danger was the other way—of becoming far too practicrl and disbelieving in those things which were spiritual. They had great facilitiesat the present moment which were competing with the Christian life, and that was secular education. He had nothing to nay against night schools, but secular educa- tion was not the most important thing even in life. It might be possible for them to spend so much time upon secular education that they would forget the spiritual education. Continuing, he said that there would be no topic which would be so keenly debated inside the Christian Church during the next 20 years as the social topic. .(Hear, hear.) They should all know something of the 'Social condition of their brothers and sisters. There were wrongs to be righted and justices to be done ; there was a whole kingdom of false political economy to overthrow, and Jesus Christ's political econoiny to he built up; there was a great weight of s.lfishness and greed to be lifted up from many people. What was needed to-day was a practical application of the simple gospel of ,the Lord Jesus Christ to the trial life and conditions of this country. (Applause.) They expected all their young people irrespective of creed or party, to have an intelligent knowledge of all the problems of the day and of their solution. At this point he told a story of a man who went to the steward of a ship and asked for a berth. The steward questioned him as to where he had been sleeping the three previous nights, to which he replied that he had been sleeping on the top of a sick man but he wair getting better and he would not let him con- tinue thus. Continuing the rev, gentleman saidthat England had had its sick man and be was getting better and he would not stand any longer someone sleeping on the top of him. (Applause). That was Jesus Christ's work. They had no right to lewe it to any mere politician to do. Those were questions which affected the coming of the kingdom of Christ. He asked how it was possible for people to live pure and good lives in some of the existing conditions that pre- vailed ; for they were not in harmony with moral laws. The conditions under which numbers of our people had to live to-day made it well-nigh impossible for them to accept the blessings of the Lord Jesus Christ. How were they were going to remedy all those evils ?\ It could only, be done because they had an intense faith in the Lard Jesus Christ ; because they loved Him with all their hearts and minds and souls and strength, They could not do those. things otherwise and what- ever else they did they should not let down their spiritual life. Concluding he enforced upon them the need of keeping their spiritual life strong, vigoious and noble.

On the proposition of the Rev. Thomas Gladding, seconded by the Rev. F. W. Kernick a hearty vote of thanks was passed to the Rev. H. Slundie-Draper. Mr. Hayward,trustee and the choir of Sankey Bridges were accorded a vote of thanks on the motion of Dr. Bennett and seconded by Mr. J. C. Holland.

Parish Church Sale of Work

A very successful sale in connection with the Parish Church was concluded on Saturday lost in the Fairfield schools. There was a large attendance, over which the Rector (the Rev. Canon Willis) presided. The Mayoress (Mrs. W. J. •Forshaw) performed the opening ceremony. The RECTOR said the two days sale hitd made £2oB— on the first day and £B7 the second day. (Applause). He wished to thank them once again and congratulate them upon such excellent results. He hoped they would make the third hundred pounds that day. (Applause.) He spoke the first d-ly about the church, the second day. about institutes and missions, and that lay he intended to speak about the schools. He wanted to go back for a moment or two. When he came to that parish more than twenty years ago he found that a very great deal had been done for the church. A sum of £15,000 had been raised and spent in one way or another by Mr. Queckett, and the more he looked at the church the more he saw what a noble work was done by Mr. Queckett. When,however, they went inside they knew that it was not completed, though its proportions were vei y noble and harmonious. (Applause.) When he came there he found that improvements had been necessary from time to time. They had spent since then £15,000 upon their schools. They considered the value of their schools and buildings alone at over £30,000. He 'could not imagine any sub- ject of greater importance at the present time than their day and their Sunday schools. Until lately they thought they were tbe most competent schools in War- rington, but they had been eclipsed by the two Council Schools—it was very easy to make magnificent buildings with other people's money. (Hear, hear and laughter.) Those schools were all very good and the education given there was excellent so far as it went. They bad done all they could for the body and for the mind, but he grieved to say that very little besides a good example was done for the soul and that ought to be with Christian people the great object. Some people seemed to think that the best thing was to keep religion out of the schools altogether. They as Churchmen did not agree with that. They wanted to give religious instruction of the very best kind possible. In addition to giving it their own schools they should have the right of entry into the Council schoels to give the religious instruction to those whose parents wished it. (Applause). They would be quite satisfied with that. They did net want to thrust upon anybody that which they did not want. Attacks had been made from time to time upon the day schools and at the present moment they were threatened with a very deadly blow, by taking away the fees which the parents willingly gave. Those fees formed a very considerable source of income, and they were bound to keep in repair their buildings. If they lost their fees it would be a very serious matter to them. He hoped the Council would prevent that, but supposing they went, their second line of defence was their Sunday schools. They had to make the Sunday schools everything they should be. Sunday schools never could be what the day schools could be. In the latter, five days in the week instruction was given by the trained teachers, while in Sunday schools there was only one hour on Sunday afternoon by untrained teachers. Diet showed that it was very important that their Sunday School teachers should be well taught, and that they should give the very best teaching possible in the faith of their Catholic church to their children. The Catholic faith was not learned in a day. It had taken him years and years to learn, and he did not know that he bad mastered it completely now, although he had been 40 years studying it. That -howed how much their teachers had to learn. He, therefore, hoped they would instruct themselves and then voluntarily offer themselves to do that work of the Church which, perhaps, could only be done by them. (Applause). He had great pleasure in asking the Mayoress to declare the sale open. The MAYORESS said she was very pleased to be there that day to do whatever she could in aid of the work of the parish. She was always pleased to help in anything that was for the good of the town, and she hoped they would realize more than they wanted. She had great pleasure in declaring the sale of work open. (Applause). Mr. CHARLES WHITE proposed a vote of thanks to the Mayoress, and said that the Mayor was a thoroughly good man in respect to public affairs. (Applause). Mr. F. G.TAYLOR in seconding said that there bad been a mistake made in the counting of the money on Friday and instead of being £B7, the proceeds had been YlOO ss. (Applause). This was a record for the second day and he hoped that they would be similarly

successful that day. (Applause). The resolution was carried with enthusiasm.

Toe MAYOR, in reply, said he had a very sincere affection for St. Elphin's Parish, first of all because he was born in the parish. He was also christened in it. His early training was reccived at the Parish Church Schools, and he was a Sunday School teacher. He was in the choir at the Parish Cnurch and he lived at the Rectory for six months after he had left the Bluecoat School, so that they could understand that he must have a very sincere regard and affection for the Parish Chutch. (Hear, hear.) It was a magnificent building, second to none in the kingdom. (Applause.) They had a splendid choir and an excellent Rector. (Applause.) The students of the Training College gave excellent dramatic performances on Friday and Saturday evening and on Saturday afterno on a sketch was contributed by Mr. Ainsworth and Miss Rhoda Lister-Kaye and was much enjoyed. The proceeds are as follow :—Thu rsday, £l2llls. 2d. Friday, £lOO ss. ; and Saturday, £94 is. 10d.; or a total of £315

Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.

The usual meet ing was held on Sunday afternoon, when there was a fair attendance. Mr. Muir pccopied the ehrir and spoke a few well-chosen words on ‘• Am I my brother's keeper ? " Mr. James Ball was the speaker and he based his address on tbe words from a beautiful song" Save me from sinking down.' He said that in animal nature if it be not guided and trained ny skilled hands, the animals, which we love,wonld sink down and become degraded and wild. The same applied in the vegetable world. He now spoke of human nature, which, if it were not for the guiding influence of a Divine Power, greater than our own, would become degraded. No other power could save us from sinking downwards. Ile asked all to cling to the power of the Cross, through Christ Jesus, to continually ask God's help in all things and we should eventually become like our pattern of life, Jesus Christ. Mr. A. Healey, Mr. C. Cotton and Mr.W. Beswick also took part in the meeting. Mr. W. H. Payton. Mus. Bac., presided at the organ.

Baptist Church, Golborne-street on Sunday evening the Rev. R. E. Letheren made special reference to the forthcoming United Mission to be conducted by Mr. George Clarke in the Parr Hall. His subject was "The meaning of a mission" and be illustrated it by referring to some features in the story of the healing of the impotent folk at the Pool of Bethesda. If the people are not thronging the porches, he said, it is because so often and for so long a time together the pool is stagnant or with but the whisper of a breeze stirring the slightest ripple upon its surface, as if the angel's wing had but Skimmed it in its flight rather than moved it to its depths. The church that is not evangelistic is doomed to extinction. It is either an anomaly where the saints surfeit themselves with the good things Christ entrusted them with to dispense to others; or it is an abomination of desolation where only bats flit to and fro and the silence is broken only by the hooting of owls. A Mission is a'movement of the Spirit on the still waters of conventional chtirch life to save it from stagnancy and bring it back to its first duty. No life is free from a tendency to get into ruts and even a church may forget that the very reason of its existence is to win men for Jesus Christ; and a mission is there- , fore a reminder of our main responsibility. A Mission is also a test of Christian profession. Too many Christian people conceive of Christ merely as a Comforter and of religiop as a Sunday recreation. But religion is the link that binds us to God and the link is one of service not less than love. A man who realizes his indebtness to Jesus Christ for the best thing he has in time and for eternity must so far be in sympathy with the aim of a Mission. His attitude towards it is a test of the reality of his relationship with his Lord. A mission is an opportunity for service. The best preparation for it is personal invitation. That is a work of art of the highest order and many good people shrink from it. It is easier to find people to canvass a district for a Town Councillor than for Jesus Christ. The comfortable Church member sitting at ease in Zion asks to be excused. It costs something but it is worth all it costs. Your very interest in the man you are speaking to may be the main factor in his salvation. A :Mission is a sacred season packed with possibilities. Its influences spread in widening circles from the indi- vidual to the family, from the church to the community. Relating an incident that told how the prayer and service of one man had led to a great revival of religion in a certain town he said that the angel that had moved the waters was a stammering blacksmith. He heartily commended the Mission to the sympathy, the players and the service of Chrisiian people.

===== 0002601_19091113_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
HEALTH EXPERTS AT THE BOVRIL FACTORY.

Speech by Sir James Crichton-Browne

On Saturday the Earl of Arran, K.P., Sir James Crichton-Browne, M.D., F.R.5.7 and other Directors of the Company received about 300 members of the South- Eastern Section of the Sanitary Association at the Bovril Factory. Sir James Crichton-Browne delivered the following most interesting speech : ' "I am glad to be allowed to add a word of welcome to what Lord Arran has already said. You have come here to-day spontaneously, by your own desire, at your own suggestion, and you are particularly welcome guests to the Directors of Bovril, Limited, because' they know that you are fair and intelli- gent and discriminating critics. You are all food specialists. The examination of food is part of your daily duty, and you are therefore peculiarly capable of understanding and appreciating the various steps and processes in the manufacture of an article that has become an important element of food to the people of this country.

===== 0002601_19091113_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
A TEMPLE OF SANITATION

" I venture to think that these works which you have seen this afternoon are a temple of sanitation. Every- thing that is possible is done to secure purity and cleanliness. You have witnessed the evolution of Bovril throughout all its stages`of growth, and you have seen it clothed and decorated, that is to say, bottled and labelled and ready to be sent forth to all quarters of the globe. "I am not going to expatiate upon the scientific principals that underlie all the manipulations and methods you have seen here, nor am I going to enlarge upon physiological reasonableness of the resultant article Bovril, the Quintessence of beef, the epitome of a bovine volume. Ydu have witnessed that it is pre- pared with the most scrupulous care, and that every- thing is done to secure that it is of the very best quality. Bovril has chemists of its own in its laboratories, both 'nere and on the cattle estates, so that it is able to send out with every invoice a certifi- cate guaranteeing that the Bovil is up to standard and description. .There is an experimental analysis of Bovril carried on in the large scale by mankind at large, to which Bovril can now appeal, an analysis carried on, not in the chemical laboratories, but in the laboratory of the human stomach and body. In the mouths of millions the claims of Bovril are justified. Millions who have tasted and tried it to-day are satisfied that the flavour of Bovril excites the appetite; that Bovril is a powerful aid to digestion; that it stimulates the nerves without subsequent depression; that it relieves fatigue, and that ip is invaluable in the sick-room and during convalescence after sickness, it i not a philanthropic preparation. .It is a business preparation. If it invokes art and humour in spreading abroad a knowledge of its virtues, it makes no extravagant, no incorrect pretences: it holds out no promises that cannot be realized, but it is, as I have said, a genuine food, and rests its reputation on its merits." During the afternoon the visitors were conducted in parties ever the factory. Comments were heard on every hand as to the cleanliness and brightness of this veritable palace. The beautiful polished white tiles throughout the building, the clean concrete flowers, the plenteous supply af pure fresh air, and the abundance of light went far to explain the healthy appearance ,of the busy -factory hands.

IO one roam were seen no less than some ten to twelve million capsules, waiting to take their places upon full bottles of Bovril. The new Bovril Bonus Pictures were to be seen, among them "The King's Derby," specially painted for Bovril Ltd. by J. W. Hatherell, the well- known Academy painter. The splendidly equipped laboratories were next Inspected, where much interest was taken in the &Beate instruments and appliances with which the analytical work is done.

===== 0002601_19091113_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
VAST BOVRIL CATTLE ESTATES.

The concentrated beef materials were shown in the form in which they arrive direct from the factories of the vast Bovril estates, where the cattle are inspected by Government Inspectors, and the manufacture is carried out under the direction of the Company's own experts and chemists. In the Milling. Room, which was next visited, the most important of the many processes which Bovril undergoes in its manufacture was shown. Here was seen the preparation of the albumen and the fibrin, the feeding part of beef. Bovril contains this nutriment as well as merely the savoury watery extract of meat. This makes it superior to all extracts of meat and home-made beef-teas. The specially prepared albumen and fihrin are ground to the finest possible powder, and have to pass through a silk screen of 15,000 mdshes to the square inch. In this finely-divided state the albumen and fibrin in Bovril are easily assimilated by the most delicate digestion. It is interesting to note that during the whole pro- cess Bovril is never touched by hand. The visitors were much interested in some of the Company's other specialities, such as the Bovril Beef Essence, Kudos Cocoa, and Bovril Chocolate, and parti- cularly in " Invalid Bovril," a highly-concentrated, unseasoned preparations, specially prepared for hos- pitals and the sick-room. Special interest was also shown in the process and manufacture of " Virol," a food that has come into such special prominence in the treatment of consump- tion, arnemia, and all wasting diseases. Virol is used in 800 hospitals and sanatoria.

The visitors were much impressed by the photographs of the tine stock on the Bovril estates, and also by a large map, on which it was shown that novril Cattle Estates (over 9,000,000 acres) would cover a quarter of the whole country. if situated in England.

===== 0002601_19091113_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FORTHCOMING MISSION

Character Sketch of Mr. George Clarke.

Warrington is to have a visit from a very remark- able man, who will conduct a great town's mission in the Parr 'Hall, commencing on November 21st. It, will interest our readers to know. the kind of man. this world-fa_med evangelist is ; we have therefore taken from the "Men of our Day" series in " Tit-Bits" the following facts : Mr. Clarke is in many ways an ideal missioner. A. man of splendid physique. and of great culture. His sermons-- though he ,himself probably would object, to the word—ate an intellectual treat. It is character- istic of him, indeed, that he appeals to the intellect rather than the emotions. "Give me a man's brains, and I'll soon win his heart," is one of his favourite expressions. ' _

`-` I believe that religion is more a thing to live with than to die with," he says. "The man who tells me how I may obtain forgiveness of sin only supplies one of my wants in the far-off future; but the man who tells me how I may obtain power over my weak self supplies a greater and more pressing need in the present.

"For my own part, I find no difficnlty hi getting men to come and hear me. I attract them a good deal, perhaps, by speaking of my experiences as an athlete.

===== 0002601_19091113_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
GEORGE CL AR

My idea is that tr man who speaks to men should speak in their own language—' hit straight' as the 'saying is—and reach .their consciences through their brains, instead of. stirring up their emotion and mak- ing. them feel sentimental, which all men hate. Personally, I never mention punishment and what men call hell, nor do I say much about heaven." Nobody who does not know Mr. Clarke intimately would imagine that he has,knoWn phat it is to want for a good meal, nor would they, indeed, imagine him to be an evangelist. Dispensing altogether with clerical garb, he looks much more like a successful barrister than a preaeher, and his refined speech and scholarly appearance are suggestive of the Cambridge graduate rather than the mission preacher. His great success in business and his consequent independenqe, have no doubt contributed largely to this. It was not always so, however. Twentyrfive years ago, Mr. Clarke landed in America with fifty shillings in his ,pocket. With the spirit of indepen- dence'and self-reliance that has always,actuated he worked his way across the Atlantic On a liner. Accustomed tO the best hotels in England, he made his way to a fashionable hotel in New. York, and stayed till his financial thermometor reached' zero. Waking one morning to the consciousness that he wag penniless, and that at night a bill would be presented to him for-his day's board, the qtestion came, " What shall I do ?" Inquiring of someone staying in the hotel, it was . jokingly 'suggested, " Go and sweep snow." Out of his well-stocked boxes he took a coat, pawned it, and bought a broom and shovel. Politely touching his cap to the Irish" helps " who answered his ring, he asked. " Shall I sweep away the Snow?' In this way he made 3 dollars. That night the future evangelist added to his store by playing pool " the billiard room. Even in America money does not roll about the streets, and the rector's son oon grew tired a irregular employment. He went to . a big store and asked for a situation, "We haven't anything for you," said the manager. " Then I'll wait till you have," replied the applicant, sitting down and making himself at Nome./ Three days later, he was stilt sitting there, and the manager, admiring his per- sistency, offered him 6 dols. a week. He spent the 6 dols. in renting a top room in a boarding-house,where' he did his own washing, and persuaded a kind widow to iron his collars for him.

While in. America Mr, . Clarke heard Mr. Moody many times, and 'came under strong religioug influence, but it was not until his return to England that he resolved to devote his life to missions. Ile had come home to die, he thought, but his life wa4 spared, and his preaching was the outcome of a reso- lution made On what he regarded as his death-bed. • The first opening for work which presented itself .on his recovery was in connection with railway men - He was one of the pioneers of , railway missions, ard founded and edited the "Railway Signal," which 19 still flourishing. Mr. Clarke has a vivid recollection of his fio mission meeting--if it is not a paradox to call it se' It was in North London, and be had " billed " tr, place well. "If advertising would fill a places thought he, "the hall will be packed." The night came, and the young missioneri made his way to the hall. There he found his wife and six hundred chairs, but not a soul came! Since then he has held thousauds of meetings, and his ohief regret has been, not that there were" too many chairs, but that there were too few.

Asa preacher; Mr. Clarke is eloquent and interest- ing, and an incident at one of his meetings at Sivansel shows how well he can tell a story. He was describ- ing a blind man approaching a - cliff, the point \ wished to illustrate being as to which man is 13 greater danger—he who will not, or he who cannot see. Gradually the'preacher biought his blind Wall nparer the edge of the precipice, he himself stepPin,g nearer the edge of the platform as he talked. length the last point was reached, and another step would have hurled the blind man to the bottom of tile cliff. There was a deep silence, when a jumped up in the body of the hall and cried earnestly, "Good gracious! the man's over! woman

PAULHANS HEIGHT RECORD.

M. Paulhan has attained quite a number of triumphs since he came amongst us. His latest and greatest feat he accomplished at Sandown, where he rose to an altitude of 977 ieet above the ground, or 90 feet higher than the record esta'lished by his compatriot Rokier at Antwerp. There were over 12,000 people on the famous racecourse at the time, and the wonderful perfo:mance of the daring aviator was received with the utmost enthusiasm. At the highest point of his flight he was almost lost to view in the clouds.

The M.C.C. team set sail in the R.M.S. " Saxbn " on Saturday from Southampton for Cape Town. The combination is a particularly strong one, and although the names of some of the more famous amateurs are missing, the side selected should be able to improve °a the Test Match results of Mr. Warner's team. Mr. Leveson-Gower (Surrey) is captain, and he has with him Messrs. F. L. Fane, G. H. Simpson-Hayward, Captain Wynyard, C. Bird, and N. C. Tufnell ; the professionals being Hobbs, Strudwick, Woolley, Denton, Thompson, Rhodes' Blythe, and Buckenham. The players on our photograph, which was taken at Waterloo Station, are (reading from left to right)—Thompson, Buckenham, LevesonzGower, Tufnell, Woolley, Blythe, and Bird.

Funeral of Tom Gloyer.

The funeral took place in Leigh Cemetery, on Mon- day, of Torn Glover, the champion of the Lancashire and Cheshire Bowling Association. He was very popular, and the funeral was attended by bowlers from all parts of Lancashire and Cheshire. The cortege was headed by ten carriages containing deputations from bowling clubs, followed by over 70 members of the Leigh Railway Bowling Club. Behind came two open carriages containing floral tributes, and the hearse was followed by 14 carriages contain- ing mourners and friends. The Lancashire and Cheshire Bowling Association was represented by Mr. G. W. Coombs, deputy president: Mr. W. H. Hindley, treasurer; Mr. C. Smith, secretary ; Messrs. B. Fielding, A. Twiss and H. Royle vice-presidents.

Socialism is well in the air just now, and the object of the new volume of romance, "The Labourer's Daughter," by the Rev. H. T. Perfect, is to show that Socialism has arisen in a great measure from neglect in the exercise of Christian principle by many of our wealthier brethren, and that the only way to overcome the evil Is by the fuller exercise of such principles. It is issued to-day by The Walter Scott Publishing Co.? Ltd. ,

===== 0002601_19091113_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
A Hundred Years Ago.

For our this week's delectation we will turn back the clock of time a hundred years and walk the village of Stockton Heath.

At the close of tlie Civil War a handful of thatched huts clustering round the ima, with open spreading heath on every side,composed the village. Two roads cut through the heath and unguarded by even a fence or a hedgerow crossed at the Mulberry Tree, for the Roman Road over the hill had been diverted to more level ground and had been long grown over by the grass and weeds. Beyond the addition of a few cottages there was little change for a century. Then in the sixties of the eighteenth century there was an awakening. The Duke of Bridgwater, aided by James Brindley, marked out their great waterway and Stockton Quay became a point of considerable importance.

drawn by trotting horse. Water travel became immediately popular and the Quay became the "Port of Warrington." Turning back a century we shall see the Quay in the years of its fullest glory. The "Flying Packet," painted in all the colours of the rainbow, is moored to the Quay and two glossy well-fed horses wait to trot the first stage of the journey to Manchester. A stage coach rolls up the road, the bugle-horn giving warning of its approach and the Warrington passengers with bags and boxes are transported to the packet. The horn blows a musical blast and amid the waving of handkerchiefs the towing rope tightens and the packet leaves the steps. Amusing to us, and only undertaken as a novelty, water travel was then the most popular of all and was adopted by the most distinguished

===== 0002601_19091113_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
SOUTHEY AT STOCKTON QUAY.

There came to the Quay in 1808 a distin- guished visitor, Southey,Poet Laureate. He was making a tour of England and wrote an account of it under the assumed Spanish name of" Don Manuel." The interest of the passage we are about to quote is not only in the famous man's description of the "Noah's Ark" that brought him to Warrington, but in the curiously prophetic passage about railways.

"We- embarked at lylanchester upon the canal in a stage boat bound for Chester. This was a new mode of travelling and a delightful one it proved. The shape of the machine resembled the common representa- tion of Noah's Ark, except that the roof is flatter, and is so made for the conveni- ence of passengers. Within this floating house are too compartments, seats in which are hired at different prices, the parlour and the kitchen. Two hoKses, harnessed one before the other tow it albng at the rate of a league an hour—the very pace at which it.is pleasant to keep, up with when walking on the bank. .Sometimes we stood or got upon the roof—till to our surprise we were called down to dinner, and found that as good a meal had been prepared in the back part of the boat while we were going on—as would have been supplied at an inn. We joined with a wish that the same kind of travelling were extended everywhere. No time was lost; kitchen and cellar travelled with us; the motion was imperceptible, we could neither be overturned nor run away with; if we sunk there was not depth of water to drown us; we could read as conveniently as in a house, or sleep quietly as in a bed." -

===== 0002601_19091113_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
SOUTHEY ON RAILWAYS.

"Excellent as these canals are,rail roads are found to accomplish the same purpose at less expense. In these the wheels of the carriages move in grooves upon iron bars laid all along the road. Where there is a descent no draught is required, and the laden wagons as they run down draw the empty ones up. These roads are always used in the neighbourhood of coal mines• and foundries. It has been recommended by speculative men that they should be universally introduced, and a hope held out that at some future time this will be done, and all carriages be drawn along by the action of steam engines erected at proper distances. If this be at present one of the dreams of philosophy, it is a philosophy by which trade and manufacturer would be benefited and money saved; and the dream, therefore, may probably one day be accom- plished." "The canal not extending to Chester, we were dismissed from the boat at London Bridge, Stockton Quay, near the town of Warrington,which was just distant enough to form a pleasing object through the interven- ing trees."

When Southey ascended the bridge he would find three coaches in waiting, for Chester, Liverpool and Manchester, for the "Boat Coaches" were a daily institution.

===== 0002601_19091113_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
WHY GO TO HOLLANDI

Water travel appealed to our great grand- fathers not only on account of its greater comfort but because of the charm of the inland water. Let us quote again: "What do you want to go to Holland for? What is there that you wish to see that you cannot see at home? Have you, my friend,maturely considered that you cannot arrive at the

"Think on those things my friend and stay at home. It is, believe me, a dangerous thing to travel, and when you have got as far as you can, you have all the trouble of coming back again."

===== 0002601_19091113_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
It is a country but half made—half land and half water—and only fit for the abode of frogs. True it is, a few human beings have an abode upon it by sufferance; but every Dutchman is obliged by nature to wear five pairs of inexpressibles, and to smoke pipes and drink schiedam I and you cannot do either.

===== 0002601_19091113_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
LINKS WITH THE PAST.

The Quay, the steps, the manager's house and sheds remain almost precisely as they were when Southey stepped ashore and we need only an effort of imagination to supply the gaily coloured boats and their passengers Some day we will tell the romantic story of. the making of this great waterway. By the Act of Parliament of a hundred and fifty years ago it Was stipulated that a passenger boat should ply daily along the course of the canal. It will come as a surprise to many of our readers to learn that the passenger boat still waits daily at the Quay, and is drawn by trotting horses to Manchester.

The " Duchess Countess "—she is the third of that name we believe—generally waits for passengers in vain, but we suggest to our readers that those who love novelty might do much worse than spend three or four hours on the old waterway in a journey to Manchester, and if they will chose a fair summer day we promise them an outing of uncommon interest and pleasure. A. M.

CIIEST-IZACKING BRONCHITIS.

A flew Breathe-able & Opium-Free Remedy Found.

If as the nights grow colder and the typical November fogs arrive you begin to be troubled with a dry cough that gets more frequent and racks the chest—if you have hoarseness and shortness of breath and often make a rasping noise in the throat on account of the phlegm accumulated therein—if you easily take cold, or are made to cough violently on exposing yourself to the raw open air—and if your chest feels raw, painful, or tight, then you must know that you are already in the grip of bronchitis. Bronchitis simply means inflammation of the bronchi: that is, the tubes that fork right and left below the windpipe and convey air to the lungs. A man may fast forty days, but he can't do without breathing for many minutes.

The te—rror of bronchitis is that it threatens to .stop up the breathing passages, the cough being Nature's effort to clear an opening when these passages are getting blocked pp. The rational, sensible, midmost up-to-date remedy is found in Peps. By the ingenuity of man a splendid breathe-able remedy, capable of reaching the very seat of bronchitis, has at last been devised. These silver- wrapped Peps tablets, as they dissolve on the tongue, give off certain medicinal fumes that descend with the breath into the bronchial tubes and lungs. All the chest is practically bathed in the famous health-giving essences of the pine:forest -which accounts for the accepted saying that Peps bring "A Pine Forest into Every Home."

Peps are a remedy which, besides leaving far behind cough mixtures and cheap lozenges, have rendered obsolute and unnecessary the employment of opium, morphia, and laudanum, so often found in chest medicines. Such narcotic drugs only disturb the heart and upset the stomach, and often cause a sediment to form at the bottom of the tough mixture bottle, this providing a last nauseous and possibly fatal dose for the patient. Peps, however, are guaranteed free from opium and all such harmful drugs. Moreover, the pure medicinal fumes which these tablets give off when dissolving on the tongue pass into the throat and bronchi and carry soothing and healing influence through these delicate channels, and right on to the furthest corner of the lungs where disease germs may be in hiding. The throat, which incessant coughing has made sore, is quickly soothed by the Peps fumes; the tickling phlegm is loosened and quite got rid of, irritation and inflammation in the windpipe and bronchi are all ended ; the racking cough is stopped, and the chest pains dispelled. Thus by perseverance with Peps, bronchitis is not only skaken off but new vitality is given to the chest to enable it to resist any fresh attack. Peps are sold by chemists at is 11d. or 2s. 9d. a box. At same prices from The Peps Co., Carlota Hill, Leeds.

The Bill before Parliament has, for one of its objects, to legalise all existing policies of the class to which I have referred, and to make insurances to meet the cost of reason- able funeral expenses, likely to be incurred, legal for the future. From certain quarters the demand has been made for a Royal Com- mission to enquire into alleged abuses in connection with Industrial Life Assurance. I am very glad that Mr. Churchill has refused the enquiry in the form in which it was demanded. If the Government were to appoint a Commission to inquire into the abuses and extravagances and illegalities of industrial assurance that would assume some ground for believing that a serious state of things existed, and everyone of the 35,000,000 policy holders in the country would be put into a state of anxiety, and industrial life assurance would be paralysed for three or four years, until the Commission reported.
