FROM LABOUR'S STANDPOINT.

By Philip Snowden, M.P.

THE. MUNICIPAL ELECTIONS.

The results of the Municipal 'Elections from the Labour standpoint are satisfactory. The returns have not been arranged and calculated completely at the time I write, but it is evident that the balance of gains and losses is well on the right side. In several boroughs the Labour Party has secured very remarkable successes. In Leicester, three seats have been gained. In Halifax, Leeds, Chorley, Blackburn, and other towns seats were won, one retained, and one lost. In Bradford, the Labour Party put forward eleven candidates, and not one was succesful. This, at first sight, appears a distinct set-back, but when the circum- stances are known and the figures examined, the fact is disclosed that it rather indicates the growing power of the Labour Party in local politics. There was a combination of parties against the Labour candidates, who, though polling a vote considerably increased in the aggregate, were not strong enough to defeat the combination. The same explana- tion applies to the results in some other boroughs.

===== 0002601_19091113_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
A PEOPLE'S QUESTION.

Most people have an interest in the question of Industrial Life Assurance. There are, I believe, about 35,000,000 life policies in e)Fist- ence and something like 80,000 agents are employed in the business. The Government have put before Parliament a Bill which aims at reforming in come particulars the law of life assurance. The Bill passed a second reading last week, and when the House re-assembles it is hoped to pass it through the remaining stages into law. This Bill has brought more correspondence to members of Parliament than any other matter since the Licensing Bill. There is, it is quite evident, a good deal of anxiety among policy holders and the insurance companies on account of the present uncertainty aS to the legality of certain policies. Mr. Churchill stated that there are 10,000,000 policies in force which are void in law. They have been taken out by second parties on the lives of others, and there was no legal "insurable interest." In the vast majority of cases the policy was taken out in perfect good faith to meet funeral expenses which would be incu red by the insurer in the event of the death of the assurred. But the law does not recognise possible funeral expenses incurred by a relative as a ground for insurance.

===== 0002601_19091113_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OBJECTS OF THE BILL.

EXAGGERATED CRITICISM.

The criticisms which have been made upon industrial lfe assurance are either exaggera- tions or misconceptions. Nobody for a moment would claim that the system is not open to criticism, but most of the charges made by critics are against things which are inseparable from the system as it must be carried on by a company or a collecting society. Complaint is made of the enormous proportion of the receipts which is paid for expenses of collection and management. In the aggregate the sum is very large, but it must be remembered that it is impossible to collect small sums weekly from scattered houses except at a heavy expense. The losses of members from lapsed policies is a serious matter, but it is unavoidable under present conditions. A BETTER WAY.

There is of course a far better way of securing the benefits which industrial life assurance seeks to obtain. The present sys- tem is but one illustration of the waste of leaving such things to private enterprise. The State is distributing £9,000,000 this year to over 600,000 old age pensioners, and the whole cost of working the system does not amount to one per cent, after the initial cost of institution. A State system of Insur-. ance could be worked as economically, and if an enquiry into Industrial Life Assurance is desirable, it should, as Mr. Churchill stated, not be announced to be an enquiry into the abuses of the present system, but into the possibilities of extending and improving the benefits of assurance. The State could not very well start com- peting insurance companies which had to rely on the voluntary response of the public. The comparative non-success of the Post Office Insurance system shows the impossi- bility of that. Any system of insurance which required the policy holder to take his payments to a Post Office_ every week would fail. There would be a possibility of attach- ing funeral benefits to the system of State Insurance against Infirmity which is to be established, perhaps next year.

===== 0002601_19091113_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
ACCIDENT INSURANCE.

The same difficulty of starting a State Insurance scheme in competition. with existing Companies was met by the• Com- mittee which sat two years ago to consider whether, through the Post Office, something could be done to give small employers the means of cheap insurance under the Work- men's Compensation Act. But that Com- mittee soon found out that the existing companies had captured all the paying business, and that what remained could not be taken at a cheap rate except at a loss to the State. The only practical way to deal with that matter is for the State to assume liability for the payment of all claims under that Act, and to meet the case either out of general taxation or by a special direct con- tribution from the employers.

In the Civil War Tracts published by the Chetham Society there is an account of the Easter Monday struggle of 1643. It was written 'by a Royalist five days after the battle and as it was written for the encouragement of other Royalists was hardly likely to give an impartial and balanced account. The writer says: "The first news of this day was a double defeat, given by the Earl of Derby to Sir W. Brereton, at Warrington. Brereton coming thither upon a hope of hindering the Earl's proceedings, who was going with part of his forces towards Manchester ; but Brereton being well beaten at the first outset with the loss of many of his men and some of his colours, had no mind to go away until he had perfected the Earl's victory and his own overthrow, and therefore drew into the field again, with the accession of some new forces from Manchester, to play double or quits, which being perceived by the Earl of Derby, he purposely held off from accepting the - battle till the dusk of the evening, and then sent some of his own men, un(ler Brereton's colours, to make towards them, who being taken, and indeed, mistaken for their own party, were suffered to join with them, or come very near them on the one side, and the Earl, charging very hotly from the other they made a great impression upon both sides; and, having thus caught them in a trap, defeated them with great slaughter and little labour. But the particulars of this piece of service—how many of the rebels were taken prisoners, how many were slain, and with what loss on the King's side—is not yet made known,"

*. * * *

Some of the soldiers who fell in the combat are said to have been buried in the grave- yard at Hill Cliffe but the registers of the period are not preserved. The Royalists were buried without ceremony where they lay on the Heath and their bones rest under the streets and houses of the modern village. It may be worth while copying here the entries in the parish register of Great Budworth.

1643, April 6. Thomas Frith de Barnton Gardianus qui apud, Stockton Heath in prelio occisus fuit tertio die mensis Aprilis et sepul. Sexto die Aprilis., 1643, April 6. Johannes Amerie de Barnton Constabularius qui periit codem die et loco et sepult. Sexto die ejusdem Aprilis. 1643, April 16. Thomas Yewley flu Thomas Yewley sepul. Decimo sexto Aprilis: 1613, April 20. Thomas Yewley de Aston qui periit de vulneribus accepted apud Stockton Heath t,ertio die Aprilis Eepult. Vicesimo die ejusrlem mensis. 1643, April 22. Richardus Ridgway de Budworth qui periit opud Stockton Heath eod. die The two first-mentioned, Thomas Frith and John Amery appear to have been killed in the battle and buried on April 6th at Budworth. The three last died from wounds received and were buried on the dates given.

===== 0002601_19091113_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
PROTECTION NEEDED.

It is most important that the workman who is liable to the risk of accident should feel sure that he will get the legal compensa- tion if he should, unfortunately, require it. There is no compulsion on the employer to insure, and the small employers rarely do. In such cases the relatives of the dead work- man have often to choose between losing the compensation or making the employer a bankrupt. But insurance has not always been a guarantee of payment. There have been a considerable number of bogus com- panies catering for accident assurance busi-

===== 0002601_19091113_art0145.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
'". = . .

===== 0002601_19091113_art0146.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
• ••• ,- •

===== 0002601_19091113_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
ness ; and the present state of the law is such that such companies can be formed with absolute freedom.

A Life Assurance Company must deposit £20,000 with the Board of Trade; but fire and accident companies have, not been required to do so. The Bill which is now before Parliament amends the law in this respect; and if it passes, all insurance com- panies will in future have to make the deposit. This will be some guarantee of the bona fides of an accident insurance company. The need for some protection will be seen when, it is mentioned that within recent. years there have been companies registered for accident and fire insurance business with a large nominal capital, but which never had any substantial assets at all. In 1905 a com- pany called the Castle General Insurance Co. was wound up with assets of £l4. For seven years it had been carrying on insur- ance business, and had never had a bank balance exceeding £7OO, although it had insurance policies issued carrying a risk of £1,500,000. Other similar cases might be quoted.

===== 0002601_19091113_art0148.txt | included=true | included =====
A GOLD MINE.

Fire and Accident insurance is a business which the State might at once taken over with great advantage to both the assurants and the community. They are branches of insurance much more easily managed than life assurance, and the huge profits which are made by the companies in this branch of insurance business prove that the public could be much more economically served by a State system which was not run for profit.

===== 0002601_19091113_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
R MISCIELLANYI

A further brief reference may be permitted to our mention last week of the battle of Stockton Heath. The Parliamentary troops threw up a hastily-made barricade in the form of a trench and bank across the main road below the Mulberry Tree inn. The trench skirted the bottom of the garden of the inn and it is interesting to know that when this land was excavated for building purposes large quantities of clay pipes came to light. These were found in groups of half-a- dozen to a dozen and the fact that they were found among other military relics suggests that they date from the period of the Civil War.

** * *

The hedge bank of Red-lane also furnished some possibly relics of the war in the form of money, probably concealed there of the time of Jam es I. and Charles I. On the hill side a field is still called " The field of prayer." Tradition has it that Cromwell's soldiers met there in the early morning for a service of praise and prayer during the period when Warrington was held for the King and Stockton Heath for Parliament.

CHAPTER VI.

It took time for the full measure of the 'catastrophe to enter into the mind of Rufus (=ridge. He had been so secure, so utterly safe • and now, in a moment, as by a miracle, he found himself stranded on the world, at dead of night, in the old and shabby clothing that properly belonged to him, and with the great house he had so cunningly taken by assault barred against him. In a halting, frightened way, he crept round the place, trying doors and windows, in the hope that LI might be able to Fet in- aide, and SO again take up the position he had usurped. But for the fact that the miracle that had happened had shaken his ,wita a little he would have gone hammering ,at the great door of the house, and demand- ing admittance. But he saw in this sudden change of affairs something beyond his imme- diate comprehension, some trick he had not rt fathomed. He was outside in the world, with the whole position completely reversed; he must make up his mind what to do. Glancing at the dark house over his shoulder, he went down through the grounds, and across the high road into the wood in which John Baxendale and his friend Munby had been hidden that night; and there, curi- ously enough, he Was watched. He lay there for a long time looking at the house and try- ing to decide what was best to be done; curs- ing his luck at being turned adrift at that hour with all that he had tried to do undone itt a moment. And near him among the trees crouched the girl Bella, watching, as she sup- posed, John Baxendale, and gleeful in the thought that he had failed in what he meant to do. For this man was dressed as she knew John Baxendale had been dressed. And she told herself -that Rufus Cridge was in the house, in the disguise in which she had seen him staggering down the road; she told her- pelf that the plot had failed. Rufus Cridge, all unconscious of her prea- time there, presently saw the dawn growing in the sky, and knew that this was no place for him to be discovered in. He must get away. In that hour he seemed to need help as he had never needed it yet. Some trick had been played upon him. These people against whom he had plotted were stronger than himself, and must be fought with more subtle weapons than any he had yet found.

The shock of that alarm that had flung Elm from his bed and sent him flying out into the night had sobered the man;_ he began to think over the whole matter, and so to arrive at some conclusion as to what had been done. The one thing that puzzled him was that he should be wearing again those. deplorable clothes that had belonged to hirn as Rufus Cridge, and which he had seen being put upon John Baxendale in the room in Middle- ton's Rents. He could not for the life of him understand how he had got hold of them, or how contrived to dress himself in them at the time when the clamour of the bell bad startled him from sleep. There could be but one meaning attached to that: that in some fashion John Baxendale or the clothes had been removed from Middleton's Rents and transferred to Baxendale Hall.

."We've changed places again; but how's it been done?" he muttered to himself. "There's been treachery somewhere; some- body's sold me. It can't be Baxenctale; they never let him go— And yet I left him in these clothes! Well, it's a puzzle! But I'll get back somehow to what I,was. What a fool I was to let the drink soak my brains away like that. Someone's caught me nap- ping, that's what it He came to the rapid decision that he could do no good by hanging .about there. The trick he had played in the first place on Bax- endale had spoilt any chance of compromise; there could be no question now of the division of the fortune; it must be, as he had first suggested, all or nothing—and in all proba- bility it spelt nothing. Within an hour he was on the road to London, with grimly-set face, and the determination in his heart to find out exactly what had happened—and, above all, where the real John Baxendale was to he found.

Surprisingly enough, there was money in fill* pockets of his ragged clothing—some of that Leant to John Baxendale by Dick Munby --eo that the journey to London was not a difficult matter. He landed in due course at the great London terminus, and melted into the streets, and so came by devious ways to ifiddleton's Rents, Lambeth, and climbed the stairs of the house in search of Johanna Cridge. He was to learn again how extra- lard in a.r v go that likeness be tweet) iiimeeL

and the other man wheh, on entering the room unannounced, he came face to face vith Amos Voles, who had paid an early morning call on his patroness. Amos looked round as the door opened, sprang to his feet, and stared at Cridge. "Ytiu—you've come back?" he gasped; for, 'of course, he imagined that this was the an about whose disappearance from Use cellar of the house they had been so deeply concerned. "Of course I've come back, you idiot!" exclaimed Rufus Cridge, striding into the room angrily. "Where's Mother Cridge?" But Mr. Voles did not answer. This was the chance, as he thought, of his lifetime, to secure, single-handed, this desperate man. Rufus Cridge twisted round just in time to eee the locked door and to take in the situation. He sank into a chair with a roar of laugh- ter. "He takes me for the other one!" he exclaimed. "Which means, of course, that .i.ok other one has really got away. Well—l suppose we shall get an explanation pres- ently," he added, philosophically, as he stretched himself in the chair and looked down at his broken boots. "It looks very much to me as if the game was up. I suppose f: started with a bad hand, and 'didn't even know-how to play that." The key turned in the lock presently, and Johanna Cridge walked in and faced him. She, at least, was not afraid of him, although she saw in him that John Baxendale who had managed to get away from the house after his capture. But a glance at him, when she got nearer, as sufficient to assure her that this was, after all, Rufus Cridge, and not John Baxendale. His careless, casual nod to her, and his dejected attitude, shewed her so much at least. She bit her lip, and sat down opposite him, and asked the one question the situation seemed to demand. "Well—how have you failed?" "Tell me first what has become of the man I left here—that precious brother of mine," said Cridge. "See for yourself," he added, 'starting to his feet and indicating by a tune the clothes he wore. "I've come back to you dressed as I left him dressed." "He got away. He was let out from the cellar in which we'd locked him," replied Johanna Cridge, savagely. "Perhaps you ..9.11 guess who did that?" The man took a step nearer to her, and stared into her eyes. "You don't mean—" The woman nodded. "I mean Bella Ogden," she said. "She stole my keys and crept down there and let the man out. The keys were found in the door. Perhaps you can give me some idea of why she should do that?"

"A bit of revenge, I suppose," replied Rufus. "But she shall pay for this—/-by Jove! she shall pay a big price. She's been pestering me for months past to marry her— and I might have done it if this bigger game hadn't come along. But there'd' something else afoot now, mother—or there was until this disaster happened. I see it all now. I understand that this man—let loose by her— has got back to the place I took, and has min- trived to get me out into the world again."

The woman looked at him contemptuously for a moment or two, and then spoke her mind with characteristic energy. "I can guess pretty well what has happened," she said, bitterly. "You never could keep your head in any sudden prosperity. You were a poor creature always. I might have known you'd fling away your chances. pidn't I warn you that you must first establish your- self before you began to play rack and ruin with their smug respectability? Tell me how it happened." So he told her, sulkily enough; and she listened, nodding from time to time, as she let each point sink into her mind. She saw, as he did, that they must begin again; she saw, as he had failed to see, that so much was lost by reason of this first mishap. But while she thought that, the man's mind was turning over and over that thought about the woman who had betrayed him; for in Bella's act he saw only one of revenge against him- self. He stood there, grinding his teeth and beating one clenched hand into the palm of the other.

"What are you thinking about?" de- manded Mother Cridge at last. "I'm thinking about Bella," he retorted, with a grim laugh. "I'm thinking about a word I've got to say to her; I'm thinking about that white young throat of hers that I can get my hands round when the proper time comes. Do you know where she ie7 "

"I've a shrewd suspicion," replied the woman. "With him, I suppose?" He looked quickly at her, and she nodded. "She thinks she'll play the game opt with the richer man; she thinks she'll give me the go-by! Well, we'll see about that. I've not clone yet. Now I know the truth I'm going back to Bazen- dale Hall."

"You've pluck enough, at any rate," re- torted the woman. "After this I don't mind what you do. I let you loose upon them to do what you like. But let the girl alone; we'll have no violence, mind." "Leave that to me," he retorted. "If I can't get what I want in any other fashion I'll do murder. Oh, you needn't look at me like that," he went on with a laugh; "that's been in your thoughts all the time. You're not the one to sit down under wrongs; you're not the one to have nursed for twenty-five years this wrong done to you, and then to wish me to go into the business with hands kid-gloved. shew you some fun in the next day or two. I'll set the country ringing, mark my words if I don't. Where's Voles, and the other man?"

"They'll be here presently, although you've frightened the life almost out of Amos Voles," replied. Johanna Cridge, with a laugh. "What do you want with them?" "I shall want my friends with me when I return to Baxendale B. 411," replied Rufus. "We'll go down together. We shall be three to one, any way. They've tricked me; they shall pay for that, I promise you." "I see that I can leave you to do the thing," said the woman, looking at him steadily. "But' let's have no violence. You'll do no good that way." "You can leave it to me," he retorted. "I'm going back at once, send Voles and the other after me. I'll be on the look-out down there for them, and you shall have word of what is happening at once. But it's Bella first; it's my beautiful Bella that's to be dealt with ! "

He kissed the -woman with rough tender- ness, and then walked quickly out of the room and out of the house. There was a glow upon her face as she watched him from the whcclow striding away, for she knew at last that that which she had trained in the man was alive. She knew that she could trust him in any coming fight with John Baxen- dale.

That long journey back to Deepington did not improve the temper of the man. Fortu- nately for himself he had money in his pockets, and he indulged his appetite freely on the way down, so that when he slouched out of the little station into the cool country evening he was a thing to shudder at, a thing of vile pas6ions—a man who would stop at nothing to gratify any desire for vengeance he might have. And foremost in his thoughts was the girl Bella—that girl. he had held in his arms so often, the while he whispered to her promises that should make her as wax in his hands. But now he knew, or thought he knew, that she had betrayed him; that on ac- count of her he stood to-night outcast again wider the stare, with his work to be done afresh.

He/ had no definite idea in his mind as to where hd should find her; he had only a dim idea that she might be somewhere in the neighbourhood of the great house—perhaps even in the house itself. His duty was to wait, and watch, and to be ready. He lay hidden in the grounds of the house —in a remote part of them—for a long time, and from thence he saw a panorama, as it were, of what was happening in the house and about it. In the distance he saw the old man, Fells Goads. nzn;ua nn and dnwn on

-

the terrace smoking, with a hand through the arm of John Baxendale—a picture of peace and security that scarcely appealed to Rufus Cridge just then. Even while he watched the two men came clown from the terrace, and strolled across the grounds towards the spot where Rufus Cridge lay hidden, still chatting, - and. as it seemed, with the old man laying down the law. They were making their way towards the house of Miss Dorcas Stubbs, and although the watching man could not know what was said, as a matter of fact, Felix Goode was impressing upon John Baxendale the necessity for caution in regard to his conduct. "Remember, my dear boy," he said, "that this young and delicate girl Evelyn has met, under the strangest circumstances, this man Rufus Cridge and has very naturally be. lieved that that man was yourself. From what I have already heard from her aunt, the man appears to have disgusted her, raised up in her pure young mind some protest at the in- solence of his conduct. That is to say, my boy "—the old man laid his hand on the arm of the younger one—" the insolence of your conduct. That is an impression you must re- move; it is something you must live down. This man is done with. If we hear anything more of him it will only be when he sues to us for some allowance, and then we can make our own terms with him. At all events, 'your duty now lies with Evelyn. You must calm her mind, and let her understand as best you may that it was some trouble that charged you, and made you behave as I believe this fellow did behave." "I'll do my best. But the thought that his hands have touched her, and that his eyes have looked at her, seems to drive me mad," said John Baxendale. "And I don't believe that we've done with Rufus Cridge yet. There is a fear that oppresses me that we may see him again, and that he may bring ruin and disaster upon us." They went on their way, and Rufus Cridge, lurking in the shadows of the trees and bushes, followed. So he came at last to the house to which they were going, saw the two men enter, and chafed bitterly at the thought of what he had lost, and what this man had regained. For it is not too much to say that, in his own more brutal fashion, the creature groped blindly for something higher than he had known—for something better and greater and finer than any Bella Ogden; which is to say, that in his fashion he loved this girl, Evelyn Kaylor, whom he had held in his arms for one brief moment. Now, hunted and homeless, and reckless, the girl proved a strong factor in that chain that was to draw him again into their lives. He thought more of her, perhaps, at that time than of the posi- tion that had been his for a brief few hours.

"I'm as good a man as he is—nay, I'm better," he muttered, hoarsely, crouching there in the darkness, and watching the lighted windows. "Born of the same mother, bearing the same name, so like him that they can scarcely tell us apart, why, in the name of common sense, should he have all the luck of it; and I none? Why must coarse women fall to my share and the dainty ones to his? I've not done yet; I'll win my game yet!" His thoughts went again to the woman who had betrayed him, that woman who had let loose upon the world a man Rufus Cridge had captured, and held, in a sense, at ransom. His fingers itched to get at her throat, to hold her brutally at his mercy, while' she whined, and pleaded, and strove to explain. More than all else, the Woman stood between him and this daintier creature he had held in his arms. She was a menace to him, not only by reason of the fact that by this time she must know the secret concerning himself and John Baxendale, but by reason of the fact that she would cling to him while ever there was any hope that he would set her right in the eyes of the world, even of that poor world to which she belonged. With all these thoughts twisting about. in his tortured brain—now urging him, as it seemed, upon this course, and now suggest- ing another—the man lurked in the shadows, and watched the house into which his brother had gone. Mr. Felix Goode, with a nice sense of what was due to him, had devoted himself to Miss Dorcas Stubbs, leaving John Baxendale free to talk to Evelyn. So that it presently happened that Baxendale and tit( girl stepped out into the little garden of the cottage, knowing nothing of that forlorn-look- ing wretch who waited in the shadows and watched them. So, like one shut out ffoin hiE paradise, Rufus Cridge saw this brother of his pacing up and down in the moonlight with the girl, heard the sound of her low laughter floating to him, as from a *great distance to which he could never reach. And the soul of the man was bitter and vengeful within him. He went away at last, to meditate upon what he 'should do, and so came into the woods that lay below the high road leading to Deepington. The night was fine, and for a long, time he lay there, not impressed by any beauty that was about him, but rather contrasting the peace of it all with what raged within himself. And in that way it may be said that there was a very madness in him when, presently, he heard the light rustl- ing of leaves, and knew that there was some- one coming towards him through the wood. He crouched there, knowing as by an instinct that this must be someone with whom he had intimately to deal, knowing that it could be no stranger in those woods at that hour.

There came out into the light of the moon under the trees the girl, Bella Ogden. She had no eyes for anyone lurking in the shadows; she had come there with a definite purpose in her mind. She wanted to see tip- man who had so suddenly been lifted into for- tune—Rufus Cridge. For, of course, it must be remembered that she knew nothing of that extraordinary change that had taken place in the two men again. She remembered only that John Baxendale, in the shabby, ragged clothes of Rufus Cridge, had spoken to her in that very wood, and, with another man, had warned her to keep away, and to leave the business to othere. Rufus Cridge, the man she loved, had been too many for them. She believed that he was still in possession of that place he had usurped in Baxendale Rufus Cridge could afford to wait. He lay there in the shadows, watching the girl. He knew that she must come back there at a later hour, when, in the silence of the woods, he would be waiting for her. He let her go on past him. He could aimed have touched •her dress as she fluttered along through the shadows, and climbed to the road and crossed it, and disappeared into the grounds of Baxendale Hall. He did not clearly know where she was going; he only knew that she must come back that way, and that he could wait

It has, of course, to be explained that poor Bella had waited in the hope to catch a glimpse of this greatly transforlned lover of hers. She believed that Rufus Cridge, under that new disguise, had carried all before him at Baxendale Hall. As she had, in a sense, helped to bring that about, she told herself that the man would be glad to turn to her now that she had followed him SO faithfully. This talk of another woman was ridiculoas. Bella could hold her own, by the memory, at least, of old days of struggle and privation. But it happened that night that she went to the cottage in which lived Evelyn Kaylor and her aunt, and there saw the sight that Rufus Cridge had seen with different feel- ings already. She saw, as she believed, her lover walking with this strange and dainty woman; heard the ripple of laughter from her lips; saw the eyes of the man regarding her: Maddened with jealousy . and hatred she crept away, and sought again the shelter of the woods, the better to cry her heart out there, and to decide what was to be done. So she came, in that mad hurry, face to face at last with Rufus Cridge, and looked upon him merely as that John Baxendale who had striven to hold her back on the previous night. Rufus, for his part, saw in her only the woman who had betrayed him, and put his rival in the place Rufus Cridge should have occupied. The hour had come for a reckoning on that matter, and in the moon- light the eyes of the man were deadly. I've been waiting for you," he said, lounging forward on the bank on which he sat, with his elbows on his knees. " something to say to you, a score to be settled between us, my girl ! " She shrugged her shoulders disdainfully. Even at that moment the fact that her re- creant lover had triumphed, as she believed, outweighed all other considerations. "There's nothing you can have to say to me." she said. " You've failed. as I thought

you'd fail. You've been dealing With a better man."

"Curse you!" he exclaimed, springing to his feet and striding towards her. "Is it for you to taunt Me with that? You've let loose the -man I thought I had safely in my hands. You've brought him back here; you've un- done all that I strove so hard to do-"

She came up to him and caught him by his ragged coat and stared into his eyes; imder- stood in a moment who he was, and what she had done. Almost like a child she laughed, in the sheer delight of coming face to face with him, and of knowing, above all things, that there was no other woman in the case— that he was alone here with her.

"Oh! my dear—my dear!" she exclaimed. "I didn't understand before. I thought you were the other man. I thought you'd got your place there in the great house, and that you'd forgotten all about me. Oh, my dear!" . lie flung her brutally from him, and, as she knelt at his feet, looked down at her with eyes that blazed with hatred and contempt. Her steadfast eyes were on him, and they did not flinch. But the man was beside himself with rage, and he saw nothing except this woman, who had dogged his footsteps, and had spoilt the thing he had tried to do. "Now, listen to me," he said, bending over her and speaking in a suppressed voice, as -Though he feared the very shadows might hear him, "I'm going to tell you what I mean to do. I'm sick of you! I wish I bad never seen your face. With all my heart and soul I love another woman, a woman a thou- sand times better than you are, and a thou- sand times better-looking. You're dirt of the dirt compared with her. You're not to be counted as being in any way near her. Take that for your comfort!" "Rufus! Rufus! You don't mean what

're saying," she pleaded, clinging to him. I mean every word of it—and more. I'm going to win the game to which I've set my hand, but I'll win it without you. Listen now to what I say, it's the last thing you're going to hear on this earth!" She crouched before him, but with no real terror. The love that had blinded her to everything else blinded her to the purpose in his mind and made her reckless of it. He knew nothing, and saw nothing, of the sublimity of that love; he saw only a woman that ,had served his purpose and was in his way. Thanks to you, I can destroy John Baxendale and you at one stroke, and can lift myself into his place. John Baxendale shall go down to what I was—to what I am now. I'll come out top dog yet. And it shall be known then that he had to do with you, Bella. Your name shall be linked with his, because men will call him Rufus Cridge. I'm going to kill you! I'm going to leave you here as a legacy for him! I'm going to have another try to get into that house, and I'm going to take my place beside that woman— (if you've seen her you'll know how pretty she is, you jade! and how much worth the having)—and I'm going to be quit of you and of my brother at one stroke. Come out here into the light, where I can see you!" Even at the last she pleaded with him, and struggled with him, speaking of her love for him, imploring him to go away with her and to let her live with him in poverty and misery, if only he would stay his hand and say that he loved her. With that hand upon her throat, and with a knife that he had dragged out and opened with his teeth held above her, she still pleaded, still called him by every endearing name she knew. So much, at least, she moved him, that he turned away his face when he struck down at her, al- thOugh he struck none the less truly; felt her Slip from his grasp,' and drew back, horri- fied at last with what he had done. She lay there in the moonlight, without sound or movement. He felt that she was dead, and he drew back, stricken, realising, now that his passion was spent, what he had. done. After looking in all directions, and seeing no one near—assuring himself, in fact, that he was alone in the wood with the murdered girl—he crept back to look at her, and strove to steady his nerves the better to understand the magnitude of what he had done. For this spilling of blood was a bigger matter than he had understood. Almost he felt the rope tightening about his neck, even there in the sweetness and beauty of the woods. He thought that she moaned once and moved. But that could have been merely fancy and nothing else. At all events, he dared not go near her. He made a wide cir- cuit and got out of the Iv:4;4s, and struck across the open country, reckless of where he went or what he did. It had all been a blunder from the beginning. He had put his neck in a noose and there was but small hope for him, unless he could fly the country before an alarm was given. But there was to be a way ef escape for him, after all, and that in the strangest fashion. Poor Bella Ogden had paid bitterly enough for her loyalty to the man. But his aim had not been quite straight, when in his madness he drove the knife towards the heart that had been faithful to him. She woke from w'iat seemed to be the very bitterness of ideath, knowing clearly in her own mind that ishe was near the end, and that she must die in this place alone. She tried to pray, tried to understand what it was 'that had happened to her, and what must happen to the man who had killed her. That thought brought her, with the courage and the strength of de- spair, to her knees: She knelt the-e, with her life ebbing away, striving to think what she could do, even in this last hour, to save .the man from the consequences of what lie had done—lift away from him the burden of his guilt. It was all confused, and she was weary to death itself, and was fast losing conscious- ness. But she remembered, as she might have remembered some story she had been ,told, that story of the two brothers—the one she loved, and the other who was nothing to her. She remembered that there had been a great likeness between the men, and that one .had been mistaken for the other. Slowly she fumbled out the solution of this desperate matter for herself, and with a last effort 'dragged herself towards the shelter of some ?trees. She saw above her, as she half lay upon the ground, that one of them had had the bark stripped away to mark it for felling. With a last mistaken loyalty to the man who had iuined and killed ..her, she put a finger to her breast, where her life blood was ebb- ing away, raised the hand, and wrote upon the white wood above her, haltingly and stumblingly, but plain for anyone to /1314.; "John Basendale killed me."-

Who Pays the Duty in the. End P

LOCAL VALUER RECOVERS DAMAGES FOR LIBEL.

Before Mr. Justice Bray and a special jury in the Nisi Prins Court at Manchester Assizes yesterday week an action was brought to recover damages for libel from a writer of a pamphlet. The plaintiff was Mr. R. R. Meacock, rating surveyor of the Leigh Union, and also valuer to the Warrington Union, and the defendant Mr. Albert Lees, formerly a member of the Leigh Board of Guardians. Mr. Langdon K.C., and Mr. Atkinson were counsel for the plaintiff, and Mr. Rigby Swift and Mr. Morris for the defendant. The pamphlet, it was stated, was issued at a time when the defendant was seeking re-election on the Board of Guardians, and was entitled "The Shame and Infamy of the Poor Law System." It had been pleaded in defence that in so far as the pamphlet was a statement of facts, the latter were true, and so far as it contained comments, these were made on a matter of public interest. Counsel for the plaintiff read extracts from the pamphlets in which the defendant had said the plaintiff bad never been dawn a coalmine in his life, bad no experience of the kind of work required for the valuing of such property, and consequently was "absolutely at the mercy of the mineowners of the district." He also spoke of 'personal influence, intrigue, and a shameless, unscrupulous disregard for the commonweal" on the part of owners who became members of the Assessment Committee, and who "thereby influence the appoint- ment of the type of man who best suits their purpose to such an important position as that of valuer." Evidence was given by the plaintiff, and in cross- examination he said that he did not, for three years after his appointment to the union go down a mine. Before 1902 he bad not been down a mine for eighteeft years, and had not made a valuation of a coalmine, but had assisted in doing so. He said he was the only valuer in England holding his appointment with the approval of the Local Government Board. In reply to further questions, he said he did not agree that in assess- ing a mine it was necessary to " go down and see for himself." As a rule, colliery returns were practically exact.

Mr. R. J. Clegg, chairman of the Assessment Com- mittee of the Leigh Union, testified to the fact that the plaintiff had discharged his duties as valuer exceedingly well. Mr. Ralph Pletcher, who was for between 30 and 40 years a member of the Assessment Committee, said there was no truth in the suggestion that the mine owners valued their own property. In addressing the jury Mr. Langdon called attention to the fierceness of the language used by the defendants and said before making misstatements be might at least have seen Mr. Meacock, and been corrected on matters of fact.

On behalf of the defendant (who was not called) Mr. Rigby Swift asked the jury to distinguish between certain facts and the fair inference which might be made. lie did not say that the statements of the defendant were in the best taste. To say of ft man that he had uttered "a frigid and calculating lie " (laughter)—might be strong language, but it might be very fair comment. Some of the allegations quoted were directed, counsel held, against the Assessment Committee and not against the plaintiff. The jury returned a verdict for the plaintiff, dsmages £2OO.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

Ring Manuel's Visit.—The King of Portugal. who celebrates his twentieth birthday on November 15th will next week pay his State visit to their Majesties at Windsor Castle. There will be shooting parties, state banquets and theatrical performances at the -Castle, and on the Wednesday the young King is to be enter- tained at luncheon at the Guildhall by the Corporation of the City of London. The visit is invested with general interest on account of the King's youth, and the tragic circumstances which surrounded his acces- sion to the throne. The following week King Manuel is expected to pay a visit to his uncle and aunt—the Duke and Duchess of Orleans, at Wood Norton, in Norcestershire, The last occasion upon [which Royal guests were entertained at Wood Norton, was when the Queen of Portugal was staying there for the marriage of her sister, Princess Louise of Orleans, before the tragic event which placed the Queen's younger son on the Portuguese throne. London's New Lady Mayoress.—The Lord Mayor's procession in London still retains its popularity, as a spectacle for women and children. It is a proud day for many a business man, who takes his family to town, not only to see the show, but the wonders of the great city in which the bread and cheese is earned. • This year the show presented an additional attraction by the presence of the Lady Mayoress and her maids of honour, who joined the Procession on its return from the Law Courts to the Mansion House. In former times, this custom was not unusual, but this was the first occasion for thirty years upon which a Lady Mayoress has appeared in the Procession on the 9th of November. Sir John Knoll—the new Lord Mayor— belongs tbo an old West of England family, and has resided at Blackheath, a residential suburb of London, since his marriage to Lady Knoll twenty-seven years ago. They are a Catholic family, and Lady Knoll devotes a great deal of her time to visiting the Work- houses and Infirmaries, and helping the poor and suffering. A Shop Girl's Academy.—Manners and business ability on the pert of shop assistants have much to do with attracting or repelling custom. Shoppers natur- ally prefer those establishments where they are politely and intelligently attended to with businesslike prompti- tude, but there seems no need for the shopkeepers to over-reach themselves, in teaching girl assistants the art of cajoling customers into buying what they do not want. Such a notion comes from Paris where it is nroposed to establish a school in which girls are to be taught this particular art in their business and are to be given degrees, according to their proficiency in "talking points," so as "to lead a lady on discreetly to the limit of her purse," end to persuade her "that she absolutely does not want what she is purchasing." Of course art of this hind is rather wasted on the experienced shopper, who knows what she does and does not want. To her, "talking points" and the old- fashioned "And the next article madam?" are one and the same thing, but there are numbers of people who, to put it plainly can be humbugged into buying what they do not want, and it is obviously rpainly with this object in view, that it is proposed to establish the Shop-girl's Academy in Paris. In Shopland.—We have grown accustomed when visiting large emporiums to the luxury of a dark room, where we may try effects by artificial light, but Et distinct novelty is the " theatre " fitted with foot- lights, in which actresses—in the part reserved for the "audience "—may study the modes, as presented by the graceful mannequins, who sweep about the stage. Never was shopping made more easy than at the moment, and the tale—once deemed incredible—of the American girl who "fixed "an extensive trousseau in two hours, may now be considered well within the realm of possibilities. Black and Gold.--Our dressmakers are again trying to "boom" the all black evening gown, hut it must be confessed that to great numbers of our country women, the idea is not a becoming one. The majority look better in "relieved" black, and just now there is quite an epidemic of black relieved with gold in the Paris showrooms. Black Brussels net, lightly embroidered with dull gold silk, the pattern being outlined with a thread of tiny jet beads, is enjoying a great vogue in the gay City. This embroidery looks exceedingly well by artificial light, especially when mounted upon black chiffon over bright black satin.

Cold Feet.—Many doctors tu:e advising suffer habitual cold feet to take an alternate hot and cold foot bath, both night and morning. The feet, so say these men of medicine' should be placed in a bath of very. hot water, for about five minutes, next they should be last dipped into a bath of cold water, then back into the hot water for two or three minutes, alternating in this way five or six times. The feet should be dried from the cold water, when they will be found to Llow and keep warm for hours afterwards.. .

__- Dearer Nuts.—No doubt people will go on disputing between the relative merits of a vegetarian or meat dietary until the end of all things, but the no-flesh eaters seem to have fairly well established the reputa- tion of nuts as a serious article of diet. Owing to the increased demands of manufacturers of nut foods, such as nut-butter, nut-meat, nut-cake, nut-milk, and so on, nuts seem likely to cost us more, although dried fruits, as well as most of the important articles of Christmas fare, are expected to be cheaper this season. Beef is the only likely important exception, unless we have severe weather, but nuts of all kinds will he consider- ably dear. That, however, is not a very serious matter far those who only eat nuts by way of desert, but it is evident that people in increasing numbers have faith in the analysts who tell us that there is twice as much nutriment in nuts as in butchers' meat, even of the best kinds.

Undesirable Literature.—Canon Pairs Cox has given some offence to women writers as a whole, by his remarks at Chester, that the least desirable novels are often written by women. Such a sweeping indictment scarcely justified, for the women writers of the class the Canon refers to, could probably be numbered twice over on the fingers of one hand, and they contribute but a trifling proportion to the undesirable number of books that are placed on the market. The blame cannot be placed at the door of women writers as a whole who have produced some of the noblest works in literature, such as, Mrs. Beecher Stower's book—to mention only one—that ended the Slave trade in America. The one effectual way of putting down the plague of unclean literature is for the Press to ignore works of authors who offend in this way. Notoriety is what they thrive upon, and if this is denied them, something at least would be done to check the circulation of unworthy novels.

• The Cult of the Jumping Bean.—The new winter game discovered by Mr. Cyril Maude, seems likely to start the cult of the jumping bean. A warmed dinner plate is provided on which a series of circles have been drawn. The players bring their own particular beans which are placed in the centre of the plate, and the owner of the one which first jumps over the outer circle, takes the pool of the stakes whatever they may be. These jumping beans which have a live maggot inside them, are brought from Mexico, and it is this little worm which bas a life of several months, that gives the bean the jumps. The hot plate urges it into unwonted energy, but whether the maggot likes it, in the same way that the fox is said to enjoy being bunted it is impossible to say. It is however mild treatment of the worm, compared with sticking it on a fish-hook, and if the game catches the popular fancy it will no doubt lead to the active quest of prize jumping beans with perhaps the possibility in some cases, that the same thing may happen to them as did to Mark Twain's jumping frog.

MADGE'.

PREFERMENT FOR A RUNCORN CURATE.

The Bishop of Chester has offered the benefice of St. Barnabas, Crowe, to the Rev. William Scholes, M.A., and it has been accepted by him. Mr. Scholes was ordained deacon and priest by the Bishop of Chester in the years 1895 and 1896 respectively, and has been curate of the parish church of Runcorn since 1895. The net annual living of St. Barnabas, Crewe, is £270, with house of residence.

Frodsham.

ALLEGED DAKUHROUS ROAD.-At the meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council on Tuesday, Mr. R. Showcross, of Cromer's Lake, Overton, Frodsham, wrote denying that the road of which he bad complained, hy letter to the last meeting was an occupation road. He contended that that was repairable by the Council as it Was used by the public going to Newton and Five Crosses. He intimated that he would certainly have something done to the road, by the Council or a higher authority and he concluded" We are not going to pay poor rates and get no return. The road is dangerous and in a very unhealthy state, a quagmire, still danger- ous for children falling and getting suffocated in the mud so I shall expect you to put the matter before the Council at the next meeting. I shall not let you rest till it is done." The matter was referred to the High- way Executive Committee.

ADMIRAL JONES-PARRY.—At the meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians on Tnesday, Admiral Jones-Parry wrote saying that he had no intention to seek re-election, and asking the Clerk to convey his farewell message to the meeting. He quite appreciated the uniform courtesy that always greeted him during the many years that be had attended, and had striven to act as became a Guardian and a Councillor.—The Clerk said Admiral Parry had left the district until titter the next election, and did not intend to seek re-elelltion.— Mr. Wright: lam very sorry. We were all glad to see Admiral Parry.—Mr. Pilling moved that the letter be acknowledged regretting his resignation. Continu- ing, he said Admiral Parry had been with the Board for a long time, and be was the only man who represented Thelwall district. They did not like to part with old members. (Hear, bear.)—Mr. Bolton seconded and the motion was carried.

INDEPENDENT METHODIST CH lIRCH.-011 Saturday last a very successful tea and entertainment, promoted by the young ladies, was held in the schoolroom. LYMM FREE CHURCHES.—On Wednesday evening the first of the winter's United Services was held in the Congregational Church and the attendance was most discouraging. It makes one, ask the question where is Mnconformity in Lymm? There were present: Congregationalists.ll; Primitive Methodists, 4; Zion Independents, 4; Baptists, 1; Wesleyans, nil. Twenty persons present representing five churches is very serious. Had it been a whist drive or a dance the place would have been full. It is time the officials of our churches asked the question who is to blame and seek, the remedy, for the members of the churches seem to have reversed the saying of the Master "Seek ye first the Kingdom of God." MNI CHORAL SOCIETY.—On behalf of the Lymm District Nurseh fund, this society gave on Sunday last In the Parish Church, Gaul's sacred cantata "The Holy City." There was a crowded congregation present, numbering over eight hundred, who listened with rapt attention to an exceedingly capable rendering of this well known cantata, ably conducted by the honorary conductor of the society, Mr. T. M. Eastham. The Rev. G. B. Thurston, M.A., Rector of Lymm, officiated at the opening devotional exercises. The soloists were Mrs. Wilson, Mrs. Wyatt, Misses Bradbury and Rogerson and Messrs. E. A. Jones and F. T. Singleton (of Manchester). Mr. William Cooke, F.R.C.0., organist of the Parish Church, presided at the organ and rendered most helpful service. The Society was augmented for the occasion by several members of the Parish Church choir. The parts were well balanced and full justice was done to the two prominent choruses Thine is the Kingdom" and "Great and marvellous," whilst the more delicate work required in the shorter choruses, especially in the quartette and chorus "List the cherubic host" (in which Mrs. Wilson and Mr. Singleton sang to the accompaniment of ladies' voices) was equally well rendered. As a commencement was not made until three o'clock it was necessary to omit two or three items and there was general disappointment that the double chorus "Let the heavens rejoice" had to be included in these omissions. Everyone is agreed that the members of the society have made more excellent progress during the four years the society has been in existence and the performance of Sunday has bcen spoken of on all hands with unstinted approval and appreciation. The offer- tory realised the sum of ZlO ss. which will be duly handed to the Nurse's Fund. LYMM WOMEN LIBERALS. WELCOME TO LADY CROSSLEY. - -

It was a.ii-aiipy thought of those responsible for the Lymm Women's Liberal Association meeting on Tues- day evening that Mrs. Crossley should preside, but it was a far happier thought on the part of the King which resulted in Lady Crossley fulfilling that office. A large audience of men and women were assembled in the Congregational schoolroom ready to welcome Lady Crossley when she arrived in order to show their appreciation of the honour conferred upon her and her husband, and they did this right cordially. Lady Crossley was supported on the platform by Miss Garland (London), Mrs. Jones, Miss West and Miss Anderson. _ _ _ _

The meeting commenced with the hearty singing of "Liberals, all arise to action." Lady Crossley, who made her first appearance in public as "Lady Crossley," welcomed the men to their meeting and hoped that the men would at times give the ladies an invitation to their meetings. They were very proud of their women Liberal a of Lymm, and although they bad not had an association long it was going apace. (Applause.) Miss Garland gave a very lucid address on " The Budget." She began by complimenting Sir William and Lady Crossley upon being recipients of one of the King's birthday honours. She was perfectly delighted to know that their Member of Parliament was included in the list. (Applause.) On such occasions people asked what the men who received honours had done,and when they saw their Member's name they knew that honour had at last met with its due—(applause)— and that honour was bestowed in the right, place. (Applause.) Coming to her subject the speaker said that some people thought the Budget was rather a dry subject. It might be to men but it was not so to women, because they were chancellors in their own households and men had only one Chancellor of the Exchequer, who was Mr. Lloyd-George. What had the Government done for the households? When they came into office a little over three years ago they came in on the plea of "peace, retrenchment and reform." They had given us peace and reform and for the first three years they gave us retrenchment because each succes- sive Budget reduced taxes until this one. They took a halfpenny in the 2 off tea. We much appreciated that and by it £1,120,000 was saved to the households. Although the Government had increased the duty on tobacco this year they had taken a part of the duty off amounting to £170,000 before. Then they had reduced the income tax by 3d. in the £ ; they bad reduced taxation by nearly £9,000,000. Compare that with what the Tories did who were in office ten years and added £40,000,000. We never heard any cries tben that the House of Lords should throw out the Budget.We never heard any outcry about"robbery and confiscation," and let her remind her audience that the Tories took out of their pockets no less than £40,000,000 a year more than the Liberals did when they lett office in 1895. The Tories did not pay their way as they went along mil they added £150,000,000 to the National debt. Since the present Government bad been in office Mr. Asquith bad paid off £47,000,000 of the National debt. Now be was asking for £16,000,000. He bad taken. £9,000,000 off in taxation so there was only £7,000,000 that he wanted. What was the money wanted for? The greater part of the extra eApenditure was for old-age pensions. (Applause.) She did not think that any money which had been spent by any Exchequer had given more happiness and comfort to the people than the money spent on old age pensions. Some people thought that because the Workhouse system was an unpopular one it must be an economical one. For every £1 spent last year in the Workhouses to relieve the inmates it cost £1 Os. 3d. to administer the money. That was the most extravagant system they could devise. The ratepayers were spending £15,000,000 a year for the relief of the poor. The old age pension enabled the old people tp spend the money how they liked, There was a hir: in the Pension Act which was, that the old people who had already accepted out-door relief could not be included. Some of them were moat deserving and could not help going on tbe rates. Mr. Lloyd-George in the House of Commons had said that if the Budget passed, by next April that disqualification would be removed. Continuing, she said that if the Budget was thrown out of the House of Lords, they would not only get their Budget, but they would get their way with regard to the House of Lords. Applause.) She then referred to the money to be spent- on "' Dreadnoughts." She did not agree with the extra, 'expenditure. Germany had not got all her" Dread- noughts," and she (the speaker) did not see how she was going to get the money to pay for them. We had to I have " Dreadnoughts," but before long we would come to some understanding with Germany for a curtailment of armaments. Leaving that phase of her subject, the speaker next said that a great deal of the money was for social reform. 'There would be afforestation, which would enable men to get on to the land instead of keep- lug iq the towns and swelling the ranks of the unemployed. Then roads were to he made ;and repaired and labour exchanges were to be built. The Budget attacked two monopolies in our country—and we had only two monopolies to attack—which were the drink trade vid the land monopoly. With regard to what we were getting out of the drink trade,it was a very small sum. The liquor trade told them that their monovoly was worth £150,000,000. They were paying something like £2,000,000 and now they were going to pay a little more-24d. on a barrel of beer. Whim the sugar duties were reduced it saved the brewers 144. per barrel. The brewers had raised their.prices and were making a profit of 12s. a barrel. If the brewers cried out it was perfect hypocrisy. Tbey were making a tremendous profit out of the Budget and they stood to win any way. If the Budget did not Fars they had already received extra

The Examiner, Saturday, November 13, 1909.

profit during the summer and they would not return it. The speaker then dealt with land. Mr. Lloyd-George's Budget was going to stop things going on as they were with respect to land. Land was to men what water was to fish—they must have it. It was in the hands of a very few people in our country Our population was 42.000,000 and the people who owned land numbered only 320,000. Was that so in other countries? In France the population was 39,000,0008nd she had an acreage of 128,000—England had an acreage of 78,000,000. After the last war in 1872 when the statistics were taken, besides the big landlords there were half a million of proprietors who owned on the average 750 acres each. Those were the big landlords. rhere were 5,000,000 proprietors who owned on the average 71 acres each, so that there were nearly 6,000,000 landowners in France. Only 14 out of i every hundred did not own land at all. Land n England was not always a monopoly. In olden times a great deal of the land was not cultivated, it was "common" land. At that time people could go to the rivers and fish and everybody who was a free man had the right to the common. When men went to the House of Commons they were called "Commoners." and to this day they talked about the House of Commons. The people who owned land in those days not only received rents but they had responsibilities to fulfil. They had. to supply the King with military men in times of war and they also had to go to battle themselves. They had to keep Royal fortresses and mend the ditches and roads. Now we had got an army expenditure of millions a year which the country had to keep up. Some of the land at one time was held by the Church who educated the people and looked after the poor. Now we had got education and poor rates. The speaker dealt in detail with the various land taxes and the plaudits of the sudience showed that they met with their approval. When they came to think of our present land policy the wonder was that the men had allowed it to go ou. The women took no responsibility of it. (Laughter.) She thought the Budget was a moderate one and in codtlusion 'appealed to the audience to do all they could for the return of Sir William Crossley to the House of Commons when the election came. (Applause.) Mrs. Jones on behalf of the Association extended their heartiest congratulations to Sir William Crossley and.his good wife and she also moved a vote of thanks to Lady Crossley for presiding and to Miss Garland for her very able speech. Miss West seconded and the motion was carried very cordially. Lady Crossley replying mentioned that they had been inundated with telegrams all day. They had received a telegram from Mr. Coningsby Disraeli who said it was a well merited honour. She thought that was very good of him. (Applause.) Up to 11 o'clock the telephone. had been in full swing, but then it refused to work (Laughter.) The company then partook of refreshments and dispersed.

WESLEY GUILD.-At a meeting of the Penketh Wesley Guild on Wednesday evening, Mr. C. E. Parker gave a very instructive lecture on some phases of the land questlon. The lecturer dealt very thoroughly u ith the economics of the question and afterwards gave con- vincing illustrations of the great evil of the existing conditions, whereby the landowner, from no exertion of his own, unfairly reaped the benefit of the labours of the capitalists and workmen. Mr. Parker finally spoke about the great advantages that would result from the nationalisation of the land.—A hearty vote of thanks to the lecturer was accorded on the proposition of Mr Midwcod seconded by Mr. Faulkner. - -

DEATH. OF A SANKEY LADY.—The death took place on Monday evening of Mrs. Long, late of Manley House, Great Sankey, at the residence of her son, Mr. Ralph Long, Highfield-avenne. RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

ALLEGED THEFT AT WIDNES. COPPER PLATES AND CHALK MARKS.

At Viiclo-e-;: OIT--life-riday, Job Dykes, marine-store dealer, Waterloo-road, Widnes, was charged with steal- ing and receiving two large copper plates, weighing 180Ib., and valued at £7, the property of the Liverpool Silver and Copper Company.—Supezintendent Strickland said that on the evening of the 15th of last month twenty plates of copper were seen safely in the deposit seed at the works. At 9.45 the same evening the watchman, while on his round, placed a chalk mark on the foremost plate, but some time later he found it missing. A search was made, and six plates were found to have ilisappeared. On the morning of the 23rd information led to the police finding four of the plates bidden on the Marsh, Widnes. They were left there, and on the morning of the 27th two men named Carter and Dunn were arrested whilst trying to take them away. These men were afterwards committed to the Liverpool Assizes.—Subsequent inquiries revealed the fact that Dykes consigned nine bags of scrap and copper to a firm of dealers in Newcastle. A representa- tive of the firm went up there, and from a letter sent to the police Dykes was iitrested. Amongst the bags of scrap the two missing plates were found, each broken up into eleven pieces.—The accused denied the theft, but said nothing about the receiving.—Bail was granted in the sum of £4O and two of £2O, and a remand sanc- tioned for a week. RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS. '

The fortnightly meeting of the above Guardians was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday. Mr. T. Baxter presided and there were also present :—Messrs. J. Spruce, J. Hallows, T. Wood, S. Woodward, J. B. Chadwick, C. E. Linaker, jun. T. Wright, .1. H. Davies, G. Egerton Warburton, G. Vining, H. Mynekyme' NV. M. Morris, A. Broome, T. Nixon, J. Turner, with the clerk (Mr. G. F. Ashton), the deputy clerk (Mr. NV. E. Hough), Thomas Cook, W. de Prez and J. Pearson (relieving officer), and the master (Mr. A. J. Parsons). The Clerk of the Peace wrote stating that the standing Joint Committee of the County had fixed the Workhouse of the Union as the place of detention for children and young persons and decided to allow the sum of one shilling per day to the Guardians of the Union. The Clerk said that not long ago the Board agreed to take children into the Workhouse at a cost of main- tenance which the County Council could arrange with other Unions. Mr. Morris: The question is, how long, have they to be detained? The Clerk : They ore detained according to the order for detention. The motion was carried. _ . .

Mr. Spruce said that he number of inmates for the week was 176, compared with 178 the corresponding date last year. Vagrants relieved were 65 compared with 61 the corresponding week last year. Owing to the pressure in the male side of the house all the beds were occupied, and they had had to make arrangements with the doctor of the hospital to draft the old MO infirm men there so as to reduce the pressure for the time being. The m!nutes were approved.

ST. HELENS.

REMARKABLE MOTORING. INCIDENT AT ST

HELENS.

The St. Helens magistrates were occupied for a con- siderable time on Monday bearing a charge of driving a motor-car at a speed. dangerous to the public against John Roberts, motor engineer, of Cook-street, Leigh. Constable Jones siid that aboyit five minutes to eleven on Sunday night, October 31 he saw a motor- car coming down Croppers-hill and along Westfield- street at a terrific speed. It wou)d be going 30 miles an hour. Other constables corroberated, and Police-officer Broughton stated that the car ran into a tramcar. He alleged that the occupants of the car were under the influence of drink, and that the lamps were in Such a poor state that it was impossible to make out the number as the car was travelling. The tram driver and tram inspector gave evidence, and said that the car dashed into the end of the tramcar with such momentum that the tramcar, which weighed between eight and ten tons, was knocked off the rails. Mr. Hayward said that he bad conducted a good many cages, but this was one of the most difficult be had had for a longtime. Defendant was charged with an offence at a specific place, and then evidence of all kinds was brought aeroust him which had nothing to do with the charge. He protested against the manner in which the case had been brought, and said that it had been over- loaded, and that the witnesses had been asked ques- tions which were not proper to put to anyone. Defendant and three other witnesses dented the exces- sive speed. ' The magistrates said that they would deal only with what had happened in Westfield-street, and imposed a fine of £5 and costs.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

PLEDGING STOLEN GOODS.

Mary Jane Bartley, a woman who spends her time between St. Helens and Earlestown, was dealt with at the St. Helens Police Court on Monday on three charges of theft. In the first case it was stated that on the 9th and 12th of March the woman was with a man, who threw her a pair of boots out of the shop, and she went away with them. Later they were pledged at Earlestown. The Chief Constable complained of the conduct of the manager of Mr. Evett's pawnshop at Earlestown, who bad been seen three times immediately after the boots were pledged, but denied having them. Prisoner had been convicted thirty times, seven of stealing, and others of loitering at Leigh, Warrington, Salford, Liverpool, Manchester, Stalybridge, &c. The woman was sentenced to two months on each charge—six months altogether; and the bench disallowed the expenses of Evett's manager. "HAYDOCK WOMAN'S SHOCKING DEATH.

• On Wednesday, at Burtonwood, Mr. S. B.ighouse inquired into the death of Edith Cook, aged 42, the wife of Herbert Cook, a labourer, of Haydock, who was found shockingly mutilated on the railway near the Bold sidings on Sunday morning.—The husband, who had only identified the body three-quarters of an hour before the inquest, said he 'last saw her alive at seven o'clock on Saturday night, between Collins Green and Earlestown. She left him saying she did not know whether to go to St, Helens or Earlestown. When he went home she was going towards the station. He got home about eleven o'clock but she did not turn.up. He told Police Constable Sinclair either on Saturday or Monday, that she had not come back, but he thought she had gone to her mother's in Staffordshire.—The Coroner said he knew something about the case. In August last he held an inquest on the woman's son, who was found drowned in the canal at Haydock. She was then censured by the jury for her conduct towards the boy, it being alleged that she had told him to go out of the house and not let her see his face again, while she and the stepfather were subsequedtly prosecuted for cruelty at Newton.—The inquest was adjourned for a week in order that a post-mortem examination might be made. STREET BETTING AT HAYDOCK. REMARKABLE CASE.

John Harrison, a single man, of Haydock-lane Haydock, was -charged at the Newton Petty Sessions on Thursday, with street betting in Clipsley:lane and Cooper-lane, Haydock. Mr. E. A. White prosequted, and Mr. J. W. Knowles defended. Mr. White stated that on October 29th, Police- constable Sinclair saw ten men, four women and a boy, each hand the defendant a slip of paper and money. The defendant occasionally moved out of sight, and on several occasions another man named John Cross made signals to him, and Harrison then came out again. The officer at length made his appearance, and when he was 'observed Harrison banded several slips of paper to Cross, who ran away. Harrison then rushed at the officer and struck him a violent blow in the face, and tried to run away. The officer, however, gave chase, and after he bad received several more blows he suc- ceeded in overpowering the man. On being searched lit the police station a slip relating to beta on horses which were running that day was found on him. There was also a memorandum book containing betting transactions. The defendant had £6 lls. 71d. in his pockets while the transactions amounted to £3O 16s. 2d. When charged the defendant said he was only settling up for the previous day. Mr. White pointed out that it was equally an offence to settle up for betting as to loiter for betting,alihough it was not so before the last Act was passed. The bench retired, and on their return the Cha'rman (Colonel Wood) intimated that they had decided to convict. -

Mr. White said he would prove a previous conviction against Harrison. Mr. Knowles objected on the ground that the informa- tion did not charge his client, that that was a second offence. It was not a matter to smile about. He was right in his objection. The Clerk (Mr. Greenell) : lam against you there. Mr. Knowles: You might t,ake a note of my objection. The Clerk was then sworn, and he proved by the court register a previous conviction in 1007. Harrison was fined .£l5 and costs. A charge of assaulting the police was withdrawn. J‘ihn Cross, of Clipsley-lane, Heydock, was fined 40s. and costs for aiding and abetting Harrison.

The Smell That Kills.

Sir.—l was pleased to read in your last Saturday's issue the remarks which Alderman Burton made at the meeting of the Property Owners Asscciation about the nuisance which exists in the Longford district when he said in reply to one of the members that in his opinion thematter would be shortly dealt with. On Sunday morning I was riding through Longford. and While I am not prepared to say the effluvia which pervaded the air was enough to kill " Old Harry," still,l would certainly say it was most obnoxious even to apasser-by, but to the residents it must be almost intolerable. Surely the Health Authorities which ought to have the public health and sanitation at heart will do something to remedy this evil. Thanking you in the anticipation of publishing this letter.—Yours &c.,

H. G. BRANDWOOD. 26, Percy-street, Warrington.

Women's Suffrage and Political Violence. Sir,—At a recent quarterly meeting at Caidiff, the Council of the National 'Union of Women's Suffrage Societies to which the Warrington Women's Suffrage Society is affiliated, passed the following resolutions :- 1. That the Council of the National Union of Women's Suffrage Societies strongly condemns the use of violence in political propaganda, and, being con- vinced that the true way of advancing the cause of /women's suffrage is by energetic law-abiding propaganda, re affirms its adherence to 'constitutional I principles, and instructs the Executive iCommittee of the Societies to communicate this resolution to the press 9

"That while condemning methods .of violence, the Council of the National Union of Women's Snffrage Societies also protests most earnestly against the manner in which the whole Suffrage agitation has been handled by the responsible Government.— YGurs &c.,

E. J. MASSY, President of the W.W.S.S. L. F. WARING, Hon. Sec. of the W. W.S.S.
