" A Nation in

Sir,—Will you kindly allow. me to reply 'eery briefly to certain letters in your issue of October, 30th. E.V.W. says "A. nation in arms is a depraved ideal." He prefers, I suppose, the simple plan of paying some- body else to protect him and his, or to relying once more on the willing horse. Or is it that be would like to have a nation without arms at all,as is evidently the wish of the gentleman with the grandiloquent, Latin title who says frankly that he hopes the Kaiser, will come and sit upon us. We can afford not to take such remarks seriously because the number of people -who would be prepared to band over Great Britain to Germany, provided they could fill their own pockets, is infinitesimal. Your correspondent who wishes for the peaceful German- Invasion has no real knowledge of German methods if he thinks our people would take kindly to them. The German nation is far more disciplined and organised than our own and regulations which pass by them unnoticed will be intolerable to us. That the social evils in our Midst are serious few; will deny. We are not, however, going to make them; less by becoming weak and therefore becoming insecure. In fact in becoming strong we shall gain that strength which jamn itself such a powet ful remedy against social disease. Let us be strong in order that in peace and security we may be able to worry out the social problems which surround us.—Yours -GEORGE CROSFIELD. ; "Ex,"]

'This correspondence must now cease.—ED.

===== 0002601_19091113_art0190.txt | included=true | included =====
The Needs of the Infirmary.

Sir,—ln your current issue there will doubtless appear a report of the meeting held at the Infirmary' on Monday last, and I would earnestly direct the attention of your readers to the resolutions which Airere then adopted and to the arguments put forward in their support. • Apathy is often the result of failure to appreciate the work which is being done, and the Board of Management cordially invite personal visits to the Institution as the best means of bringing home to the people of Warrington and District the nature of .the great services which are daily being rendered in the alleviation of suffering. The enthusiasm of all connected with the manage-i meat is most remarkable but is it surprising ?' Association with the work done makes one realise, as nothing else can, that there are thousands, who, from want of means, would otherwise be absolutely! denied that surgical advice and treatment which those more fortunately situated command as a matter of course: and regardless of expense when they themselvis are the sufferers: Think of the hundreds of cases of appendicitis, growths, accidents, nnd other terrible: afflictions which must be experienced amongst a large population like ours. The Infirmary is a charity which ought to stand the first of all, a charity five from any pauperizing influence, and one which returns its supporters the most substantial value for their money. As a result of the meeting on Monday last there is already a notable increase in tile pumber of £lO 10s. subscribers. I earnestly appeal to everyone to come forward and assist in the work, and at the same time I venture to express the hope that subscribers will measure the extent of their generosity by the excep, tional character of the work for which this appeal is made.

In conclusion I would make this general appeal to

your readers. Do not wait hr a peisonal application, but write at once to the Treasurer or Secretary at the Infirmary and advise him the subscription you will be prepared to give, be the amount large or small.— Yours faithfully,

W. PETER RYLANDS.

===== 0002601_19091113_art0191.txt | included=true | included =====
Christian Charity Through Holy Trinity Spectacles.

Sir,—Perhaps it may be worth while to place before your readers the enclosed extract from" Holy Trinity Magazine." It shows a great deficiency in the Christian charity of tbe editor of that Magazine and a great want of hope and faith in the success of his cause, in fact, it shows that. the power of the three cardinal virtues" Faith, Hope and Charity" is at a very low ebb with him, In denouncing temperance reformers and including with them all who are not Churchmen he seems to forget the time when the Bishop of Liverpool stood alone of all beneficed clergy in Warrington on the Temperance platform in the Parr Hall, the whole of the Warrington clergy having apparently gone over bag-and-baggage to the Trade. I love the dear old Church of England which has weathered many a storm but, if this is sample of the Christian charity of the Warrington clergy they are doing their best to make Churchmen and Churchism stink in the nostrils of all fair-minded people. Let the editor of the Holy Trinity Magazine before he indites another fillipic against his fellow Christians turn to and read the 13th chapter of the first epistle to the Corinthians.—l remain yours, &c.

PHIL SQUOD.

The extract quoted is as follows : THE COMING ELECTION. "It is just as well that ,we should be on our gucird in view of the many doubtful things tbat are apt to be done at election times. It is surprising how much religious activity is aroused in certain circles when preparation for the contest begins. At the last election, the religious meetings at Parr Hall, with the addresses on Socialism, ending in a special address to workingmen on the subject, on the eve of the election, created quite an impression ,that religious emotionalism of the people was being exploited for party purposes. And the fact that the meetings ceased at once when the election had taken place, seemed to confirm the Impression. Of course' all this may have been quite unintentional ; but we would point out to all promoters of such meetings, the advisability pt having their special religious efforts in a way to avoid all suspicion of ulterior motives. It is sinfortunate also that special revivals of interest in the temperance question usually take place,

when an election is looming in the distance. A little more discretion in carrying on the work we feel sure would not be at all inimical to the Temperance cause. Unhappily the religious aspect of the Temperance movement as carried on by some has been seriously discounted since it came out that all the expense of the legislative reforms proposed are sought to be got out of the publican without any contribution at all from the

ho-called Temperance Reformer. We doubt not but that the hat will be going round for the support of these and similar efforts under the plea of unsectarianism. But it is quite time that Churchmen at any rate should take care that none of their money went into unsectarian coffers, as th* have no guarantee at all that the motley they give will not be used in a way detrimental to the Church's interests. When they give their money to unsectarianism, they give it to some- thing that is pledged to nothing. tinsectarianism has no standard of belief, no standard of owner- ship, and we are not sure about it, standard of conduct."—" Holy Trinity Magazine."

NATIONAL SERVICE LEAGUE.

Annual Prize Distribution.

THE POLICY AT THE GENERAL ELECTION.

The annual prize distribution to the Winners of the shooting competition in connection with the Warring- Division of the National Service League took place on Wednesday evening in Crosfield's Dining Hall. About 120 sat down to a very enjoyable supper. Captain G. R. Crosfield presided and he was supported by Major Strachan (organising secretary for Lancashire), Captain Layton and Mr. J. H. Smethurst. After supper the prizes were distributed by Mrs. F. A. Frost. The toast of the " King " having been honoured on the proposition of the CHAIRMAN, Mr. FROST submitted the toast of" President of the League-Field-Marshal Lord Roberts." Mr. Frost gave a short sketch of Lord Roberts' life, and said that he bad done more than his share of work for his country and people, and that it was time he had a rest. There was one man in the country who differed from that opinion very strongly, and that was Lord Roberts himself. He had won many victories, and now he said that every man should be taught to use a rifle, to know how to defend himself and his home if called upon, and to have some idea of discipline. Continuing, the speaker said that the use of a rifle ought to be such a part of a man's education as the use of a needle ought to be for a woman. It would do a man no harm, but good, and be would mit with others on an equal footing. In case of war we were all equal, just as we were at the time of death. He could not see the slightest objection to it as it worked in other countries. Their proposals were that young men between the ages of 18 and 22 should receive four months training and for two years after- wards put in a certain number of drills and a few days in camp. Concluding he hoped that Lord Roberts' ambition would be realised and that the National Service League proposals would be in force and then he would have accomplished anotber victory. The toast was drunk to musical honours the company rising and singing " For he's a jolly gooefellow." Mrs. Frost then distributed the prizes to the following : CIVILIAN COMPETrI'ION.-1, F. Garside, 30, silver cup; 2, J. Hartley, 27, pair bronze horses; 3, B. Crompton, 27' cardS table; 4, J. Sankey, 27, cruet. MILITARY COM Pr.rmos.-1, A. Adams, 32, Challenge Cup and clock, 2nd time in 4 years, presented by Col. R. Walker; 2, P. D. Gardiner, 30, medal; 3, C. W Bowyer, 29, case of carvers; 4, J. Lloyd, 28, cruet; 5, R. Done, 27, clock; 6, T. Libbiter, 27, medal. OPEN COM PETITIOV.-1, J. W. Plinstoo, 33, challenge cup and medal (2nd time in succession), presented by Mr. T. H. Lyon; 2, E. Howard, 32, cruet 3, J. Allardice, 30, brass stool and cruet; 4, H. Potts, 30, umbrella; 5, R. Thompson, 29, pipe case and pouch • 6, J. Done, 29, clock; 7, T. Taylor, 29, boots; 8, J. W. Elliot, 18, drapery ; r. 9, F. Bennton, 28, pipe; 10, A. E. Longsbaw, 28, cruet.. Having finisbed this task Mrs. FROST said: I feel highly honoured in having been asked to distribute the prizes to-night I congratulate you all. You are not the only people who have won prizes. I won a prize once at the Appleton Rifle Club, but to be honest with , you I never hit the target. Still I got a prize: because Imy side won. I did not contribute a shot towards it. ( Laughter and applause). Captain CROSFIELD proposed the health of Mr. and Mrs. Frost. Be said it helped them enormously in their work when ladies like Mrs. Frost came down. Their work was a difficult one because they had been sunk in security for so long that people imagined that nothing could happen to us. However, people were beginning to waken up. gii.t the next election it would no doubt be the case of the Budget; but they hoped that at the election after the next, the country would be sufficiently prepared for Lord Roberts' scheme becoming the law of the land. (Applause). Mr. FROST briefly replied. Mr. J. H. S ETIKIRST proposed the health of " The President of tint local division-Mr, T. H. Lyon." He did not think it was possible to have a gentleman more suited to the position than Mr. Lyon. He was an example which many country gentlemen might copy. It was always a pleasure to see how Mrs. Lyon welcomed them at the Hall every year. Possibly that might be another inducemrit for Captain Crosfield to take to, himself a wife. (Laughter).

, The toast was honoured cordially. Major STRACHAN said that he bad been addressing many meetings lately upon their proposals and from the drawingrooms to the workshop he bad found any amount of enthusiasm. But that was not enough for they would never succeed until they could convince their great politicians that adherents were coming in much more frequently from all over the country. His firm conviction was that if their proposals were made .law to-morrow they would be received by the people in a better manner than compulsory education. Con- tinuine, be said that out of 41 years in the Army he had spent 18 years in India with Lord Roberta and he did not leave the country until he became the champion shot of the British Army in India. Concluding, be said that rifle shooting was an education for them and it benefited both mind and body. (Applause.) _

Captain CROSFIELD proioseci the toast of "The Donors of the prizes, the Artistes, and the Shooting Committee and Mr. Ball" (the secretary who was res- ponsible for the arrangements that evening. 1 He said that at the next election they were going all over the .country to the various candidates of Parliament, and were going to give them such tangible evidence as they could of the way the . ground had been prepared for 'them. They were going to them with the list of the 'members, associates and adherents of the League. There were already 1300 adherents to the League in Warrington. (Applause.). During the evening the following contributed items: The Apollo Quartette (consisting of Messrs. N. W. Cook, J. Gittens, W. Critchley and L. Auckland), Mr. J., W. Willett, Mr. L. Auckland, Miss Carrie Hastings. *Messrs. A. and H. Graham were in charge of the gramo- phone. Mr. W. Pemberton acted as the pianist.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

The first visit to Warrington of Mr. Arthur Roberts :the famous London cemedian, has proved a great success. Mr. Roberts' name has long been synonymous with hilarity, and the sketch he is now producing:at Warrington fulfils all expectations. It is called "The Importance of being another rnan's wife" and is written by another well-known comedian, Mr. Harry Pleon. The characters are Fitzawful Smith, a Masher Mr. Arthur Roberts Major Thunder, Retired Sammy, his son • Mrs. Green Miss Barry Eldon Martha Kitty Masham, Mrs.Ehunder's Sister Miss Ida Furneaux

Mrs. Thunder Miss Ruby Celeste

Scene—Mr. Thunder's Flat, London.

Fitzawful Smith appears on the'scene without any preliminary announcement, and his arrival at the flat while only Mrs. Thunder is at home turns out rather inopportune. Some very funny scenes follow, in the course of which Mr. Roberts makes an inimitable appearance in feminine garb and evokes roars of laughter in the by-play with Major Thunder and Dirs. Green. The ladies' parts are all well- taken and it can be said with sincerity that there is not a dull moment in the sketch. Adams and Ward have specialised as acrobatic comedians, and their turn is notable for originality, cleverness and humour. Mr. and Mrs. Harold Finden present a sketch in which husband and wife are seen at daggers drawn. Both say some amusing things, and the disagree- ment terminates in a very laughable farce. Edie Reynolds plays very tastefully on the violin; and Harry Breezy, the eccentric sailor, sings a popular ditty. Grace Hunt and Edith St. Clare are smart comediennes, the latter earning hearty applause in the role and garb of a principal boy. H. D. Adams, the juggling page boy, is one of the smartest followers of Cinquevalli seen on the stage for some time, and should go far in his profession. He possesses dexterity and co4dence to a marked degree, and no juggling feat should he impossible to him in course of time.

For next week's programme, Jack Lane, familiarly known as" The Yorkshire Rustic "is announced. Another popular item will doubtless be that of Orpheus, instrumentalist, described as a master of melody. There are also announced : Rose D'Aulby, chorus vocalist • Woodhouse and Wells, comedians and dancers; Nancy St. John, comedienne; James Curry, comedian • Sisters Carlton. duettists and dancers, and the Weiniars, speciality artists, and the Palace pictures.

DISAPPEARING OLD WARRINGTON.

THE BACK OF MESSRS. SHAW'S LATE PREMISES IN BRIDGE-STREET.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE. "The Prodigal Son."

, The return visit of this dra—ma has proved a powerful attraction at the Royal Court Theatre, for although "The Prodigal Son" is not a great play, the fact that it is the creation of ?Jr. Hall Caine—a stage adapta- tion of one Of his nbvels which had an immense vogue a few years ago—seems to be sufficient to commend it to the average play-goer. It is a sordid story depict- ing ugly phases Of life, the only redeeming feature being that the prodigal, after a faithless and dissolute course, comes to his senses in the-last act and attones to some extent for his wrong-doing. The public therefore consoles itself with the fact that the moral is a good one—and that is sup- posed to cover a multitude of sins. Mr. George D. Day's company gives a capable production, Mr. Elliot Ball being exceptionally good as the rugged, long-suffering Magnus. His manly bearing and, rare elocutionary gifts eminently fit him for the part and he proves a great favourite. The difficult Ole of Oscar is in the hands of Mr. Scott Leighton and although he is not a sufficiently ardent lover as to appear a formid- able rival to Magnus, he is more convincing in the later stages of the play and wins rounds of applause for his forceful acting in the trying scene when he forsakes the temptress Helga. Miss Isla Garnet Vayne gives effective portrayals of Thora and Elin 'and Miss May Beatrice is clever in the unenviable part of Helga. The other characters are capably sustained and the play is well mounted. The orchestra, under 'Mr. Norman Latham's conductorship, gives sbme bright selections in pleasing style. .

"THE MERRY WIDOW."

Next week we shall have a first visit of Mr. George Eclwardes' Excellent Company, in "The Merry Widow." The production has created a record in l'every eity and town in any part of the 14 countries now playing this remarkable Musical play. The special attractiveness of this play evidently arises from the fact that on its musical side the work is of the character .of real opera, whilst on the humorous side it partakes of real comedy the happy blending of the two going to make it a substantial success. Theatre patrons should flock in thousands next week for it will be found a capital cast in every detail. Miss Sylvia May, who sustains the part of "Sonia," "The Merry Widow," shows herself to be an artist of considerable talent. Mr. Fred Leslie, the son of the old London Gaiety Theatre favourite as "Prince Danilo," with a good voice and fine presence, togetller with a pleasing characterisation, fills the part admirably. Mr.Riply Evans as the "Vicomte," con- tributes a great vocal charm to the perfermanee by his beautiful tenor voice, and his duets with that capital " Natalie " (Miss Ada Thomas), are really a delightful feature of the play. Mr. Edward D'Arcy as "Baron Popoff," and Mr. Fred Fowler es " Nisch," keep the audience in a merry mood; these comedians are a delight in these two parts.

A representation a the "Danse D'Apache " is g'w,pn in the third act.

The plan is now open at the theatre, and seats may be booked during the times announced on our front page.

THE NEW SALOON. AND LOUNGE

Many improvements have been effected at the Royal Court Theatre since Mr. Beecham purchased it twelve months ago,but the most notable is one that is just bi3ing completed:this is a new saloon and smoking lounge,with entrance from the dress circle, which will be opened on Monday. An archway adjoins the staircase and through this is entered a /estibule from the right of which is the ladies' cloak room. Proceeding through the swing doors at the end of the vestibule the large saloon is entered which is tastefully decorated, the walls being hung with a rich old gold paper. The bar fittings and woodwork are in white and old gold, the crimson draperies affording a pleasing relief. The manager's office adjoins the bar and at the far end of the room are provided gentlemen's lavatories. The extension is a decided impr,ovement. The whole of the building, decorating and furnishing

has been effectively carried out by ,Messrs. J. M. Greenhalgh and Co., of Warrington. An entirely new electrical plant is being laid down with a system for storing the current, so that in future there will be no fear of the light failing during performances. This will be completed in a week's time. The manage- ment have also secured some exceptionally good book- ings for the winter-season, and patrons of the theatre are assured not only of comfort, but of high-class entertainment.

CROSS COUNTRY RUNNING.

WARRINGTON UNITED HARRIERS.

The above club had as visitors on Saturday, Runcorn Holy Trinity Harriers, to run their first club run of the season from headquarters 'The Black Horse Inn," Sankey Bridges. About 24 of he home club turned out, while only twelve of Runcorn turned out. The home team was weak, only one of the Northern team turned out while A. Willcocks the captain was ill and couldnot run. Trail was taken out by W. Boltonand W. Miller with one or two of Runcorn over seven miles of stiff country. The teams started from Hood-lane and a good race ensued. After about 43mins. two Warrington men appeared in sight, who proved to be W. Stewart who ran with splendid judgment all through winning from C. Vow (club champion) by about six yards, with E. Preece of Runcorn about 180 yards behind, 3rd. The plat:sings were as follows :—Warrington United—W. Stewart 1, C. Vose 2. J. Litherland 4, Jack Hands 5, F. Shadwell 6, total 18 points; Runcorn Holy Trinity —T. Preece 3, S. Moores 7, Dunbobbin 9, Burton 10, G. Thomas 16, total 45 points; Warrington winning by 27 points. To-day Warrington United run Rylands Bros. Harriers from Gorsey-lane at 2.30 p.m., when a novice medal will be given to the first novice home.

MR. CROSFIELD, M.P., ON NATIONAL SERVICE.

NO BELIEF IN INVASION SCARE.

Pusentation of Bowling Prizes.

Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P., presented the Crosfield Challenge Cup and prizes in connection with the Warrington Bowling Association, on Thursday evening, at the Midland Hotel. About 30 sat down to a very enjoyable tea and afterwards the prize distribution and a smoging concert took place. Pending the arrival of the Member, Mr. Freeman (treasurer) presided. Mr. F. W.Upton (secretary) rend letters of apology for non- attendance from Sir Gilbert Greenall,But., Mr. Robert Pierpoint, Captain Crosfield and Councillor .1. Fulton- Smith.

Mr. Crosfield having arrived, Mr. .7. WILLETT pro- posed "The Association." Be said he was very glad to find that the Association was growing and the members intended to make it grow. He believed they intended to open out aJ4 mile radius and then they would be able to bring Leigh and other clubs down and if they could get that they could give the "Lancashire and Cheshire" a bit of a miss. (Laughter and applause). The toast was cordially drunk. Mr. G. BALL responding said they had made splendid progress this season, but it was not so fast as they should like it. This year the individual merit com- petition had been a splendid success. 1 hey had only had 8 clubs competing for that handsome cup, and he hoped there would be three times as many next season. They bad the talent and the material tending towards a good competition and he hoped all the bowlers in Warrington would aid the Council in their efforts, so that they might have one of the finest competitions in Lancashire. Tbere was no reason why they should not. (Applause). Mr. FREEMAN proposed the toast of "The President and Vice-Presidents." They all knew what an excel- lent help Mr. Crosfield bad been in connection with bowling in Warrington. There was no doubt that the Prebident had been frilly repaid for his efforts to raise bowling in Warrington to the high standard it had attained. He had been the pioneer of bowling/in this district undoubtedly. He coupled with the toast the name of Alderman John Evans.

The toast was accepted with musical honours the company singing " For they are jolly good fellows" and giving three cheers for Mr. Crosfield and Alderman Evans and a cheer for Mrs. Crosfleld and Mrs. Evans.

Mr. Crosfield responding to the toast said he was extremely sorry he had clashed two dates, but he had been engaged for a very long time past to take his wife to a most delightful concert at the Parr Hall that evening. Continuing, he said that there was always good fellowship with them all at their meetings, as there was at so many other gatherings of bowlers in Warrington. He had been very glad and had thoroughly enjoyed taking an oar in the boat along with other Warrington bowlers. He knew perfectly • well that it was only a small share that he had done in the great progress of bowls in Warrington which had been made in the course of the last 15 or 20 years, but he was sure that Alderman Evans and himself were as thoroughly and heartily glad to have seen that old English game of bowls make the progress that it had done in Warrington and district, in Lancashire and Yorkshire", and all over England, as any bowler in that room. He was doubly glad to see that the old English love of out-door recreation was still as mani- fest in our country as ever it was and anybody who considered the developments of these modern days— the crowding of our population into mighty cities and enterprising towns like ours—must realise what it meant for the physique of our country. It had always struck him that bowls was pre-eminently an accessible game for the workers—both middle class and artisan— of our country. (Applause). That was why he had felt particularly glad to see the progress of a game in which they could all meet in friendly rivalry —both old and young just as they could in golf. One of the seniors had shown that he could hold his own with the young ones. (Hear, hear). He thought that, beside the pleasure of the game it kept up the physique, of our race, and if they would, the martial spirit of the race too. He did not want to keep any of them awake that night by frightening them with the thought of an impending invasion by Germany, because frankly, he did not believe it. (Hear, hear.) He was however, strong on the question of our National and Imperial defence. He did not say that as a mere party man—he was talking to Radicals and Tories—but as a nonparty man. He believed in maintaining the defences of our insular position. We could not afford to play the idealist on a question of that kind, although he believed that in years to come there would be a check to the excessive expenditure which,he was going to say, 'had cswollen and swollen until it had now reached £100,000,000 sterling per annum. He looked forward to the day when there would be a check. He did not believe that they as Englishmen could afford to play the idealist, and he was very glad that our English games kept alive the old patriotic spirit. He hoped that they would ilever allow anything to deter that section of the popu- llition who, by their physical advantages and by their natural bent,were best fitted to serve their country by adopting a military career. On the other hand he told them frankly he had never subscribed to the doctrine that a man was a shirker if he served his country in some other way than as a soldier. He did not at all think that was the only way in which a man could well serve his country, but he did think it was a matter of para- mount importance and he sincerely hoped that War- rington would be well to the fore in support of 'the differeht branches of the forces responsible for the defence of the Empire. He mentioned that in , connection with English outdoor games because the I things hanged together and he hoped that the fighting I spirit would be directed to the defence of the Empire as far as was necessary for the purpose, and with it also that they would learn to strive for other objects in these days—for the objects that helped to push forward the country in the right direction in the great march of civilization. (Applause.) Concluding he said that the Individual Merit Competition had created good fellowship between man and man and works and works and as regarded the Association he sincerely hoped that it might grow in influence in every way, and would add to the recreation, enjoyment, and well- being of the people. (Loud applause.) Alderman EVANS in replying mentioned that he was one of the original committee that first formed the Warrington Bowling Association. Mr. Crosfield then handed the cup to Mr. A. Cleave the captain of the Bull's Head team who had had the honour of winning it for live years in succession. He alpo presented prizes to the members of the team as follows :—A. Cleave, J. W. Jolley, I'. Hackett, J. Berry, H. Williams, 11. Eyes, E. Shaw, W. Daniels, Joe Cleave, J. Swanton, W. Andrews and W. Simister. The runners up (Runcorn), were also suit- ably recognised,the following being the recipients : George Woods, R. Wilson. J. Rawlinson, W. Banner, J. Clarke, C. Woodward, R. Jakiman, C. Woods, A. Richardson, T. Woods, S. Houghton, B. Jolley. The individual merit recipients were as follows : J. W. Jolley, H. Clare, IL Langley, A. Cleave T. Davies, W. Swanton A. Shaw, A. Ford, Joe Cleave, Bothwell, J. Hardman, Weston, J. Bedworth, J. Bouldeswortb, J. Langley, George Monks. On the proposition of Mr. G. Mr. J. WILLETT moved and Mr. 11. FOGG seconded a vote of thanks to the artistes, pianist, host and hostess. Durinetbe evening a capital programme was sub- mitted. Mr. G. ADJECTIVES!

Rev. A. M. Mitchell Finds Hell Here.

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell, the outspoken Vicar of Burtonwood, has an article in the current issue of his parish magazine entitled "Warrington for Christ." In the course of it he makes the following remarkable comments on the town:

"In all England there is not, perhaps, a town' of similar proportions to Warrington, and equal populution, so completely given over to social unrighteousness. For abominable smells, for awful slums, for abundance of, drink, Warrington can easily win a first prize. Its record is not goods nay, it has an evil record. Possessed of eight town Churches, with some Missions in addition, and a variety of Chapels, Warrington has gained, notwithstanding, a world-wide notoriety for intemperance, brutality, 'infant mortality, and civic rottenness. The town is so beer-sodden as to be offensive, so slummy as to be disgusting, so dirty, nauseous, and unhealthy as never to be free from epidemic, so depraved and well vaccinated as to have a death-rate of 51.*3 per thousand, in a portion of, its area, so socially, morally degraded that -the drink traffic looms _ . _ _ _ _

large On aCleast .one of its Councils and Board of Gitardians. Purge out the old leaven." After readiug the above, Warritigtonians may be Lnelined to wonder what Burtonwood is like.
